The reason why George Finch—for it was he—was behaving strangely was that he was a shy young man and consequently unable to govern his movements by the light of pure reason. The ordinary tough-skinned everyday young fellow with a face of brass and the placid gall of an Army mule would, of course, if he had decided to pay a call upon a girl in order to make inquiries about her dog, have gone right ahead and done it. He would have shot his cuffs and straightened his tie, and then trotted up the steps and punched the front-door bell. Not so the diffident George.

George’s methods were different. Graceful and, in their way, pretty to watch, but different. First, he stood for some moments on one foot, staring at the house. Then, as if some friendly hand had dug three inches of a meat-skewer into the flesh of his leg, he shot forward in a spasmodic bound. Checking this as he reached the steps, he retreated a pace or two and once more became immobile. A few moments later, the meat-skewer had got to work again and he had sprung up the steps, only to leap backwards once more on to the sidewalk.

When Mr. Waddington first made up his mind to accost him, he had begun to walk round in little circles, mumbling to himself.

===Chinese===
乔治·芬奇——正是此人——举止之所以怪异，是因为他是个腼腆的年轻人，因而没法单凭纯粹的理性行动。换作寻常那种皮糙肉厚的市井小伙子——脸皮黄铜打就，胆气如军用骡子般安详稳健——倘若打定主意要拜访一位姑娘、顺便问问她家狗儿的近况，自然说去就去，径直办妥。他会抻一抻袖口，正一正领带，然后蹬蹬蹬登上台阶，戳响前门的门铃。怯生生的乔治却全然不是这路数。

乔治的路数自成一格。优美，而且自有其可观之处，但自成一格。起先，他单脚立定片刻，直勾勾望着那所房子。随即，仿佛有只友好的手把一根三寸长的烤肉签戳进了他的腿肉，他痉挛似的一跃向前。冲到台阶跟前又把这股劲头煞住，倒退一两步，重新僵立不动。片刻之后，烤肉签又发起功来，他弹上了台阶——却又一个倒纵，重新落回便道上。

待沃丁顿先生头一回打定主意上前搭话时，他已经开始绕着小圈圈打转，嘴里喃喃自语了。

===Original===
Sigsbee Waddington was in no mood for this sort of thing. It was the sort of thing, he felt bitterly, which could happen only in this degraded East. Out West, men are men and do not dance tangoes by themselves on front doorsteps. Venters, the hero of *Riders of the Purple Sage*, he recalled, had been described by the author as standing “tall and straight, his wide shoulders flung back, with the muscles of his arms rippling and a blue flame of defiance in his gaze.” How different, felt Mr. Waddington, from this imbecile young man who seemed content to waste life’s springtime playing solitary round-games in the public streets.

“Hey!” he said sharply.

The exclamation took George amidships just as he had returned to the standing-on-one-leg position. It caused him to lose his balance, and if he had not adroitly clutched Mr. Waddington by the left ear, it is probable that he would have fallen.

===Chinese===
西格斯比·沃丁顿可没心思看这等把戏。他痛切地感到，这等把戏只可能出在堕落的东部。在西部，男人才是男人，绝不会独自在人家门前台阶上跳探戈。《紫艾草骑士》里的男主角文特斯（Venters），他记得，书里是这样描写的：“身躯颀长挺直，宽阔的双肩向后张开，臂上肌腱滚动，眼底燃着一朵挑衅的蓝焰。”沃丁顿先生心想，眼前这个痴小子跟那是何等不同——他竟心满意足，在公共大街上玩单人环转游戏，白白糟蹋人生的春天。

“喂！”他厉声喝道。

这一声喝，正打在乔治刚摆回单脚独立姿势的腰腹要害。他顿时失了平衡，若非手疾眼快、一把攥住沃丁顿先生的左耳，多半就摔倒了。

===Original===
“Sorry,” said George, having sorted himself out.

“What’s the use of being sorry?” growled the injured man, tenderly feeling his ear. “And what the devil are you doing anyway?”

“Just paying a call,” explained George.

“Doing a what?”

“I’m paying a formal call at this house.”

===Chinese===
“对不起，”乔治说，手脚已经各归各位。

“对不起顶什么用？”受害人咕哝着，心疼地揉着耳朵，“你到底在搞什么鬼？”

“就是来拜访个人。”乔治解释道。

“拜访个啥？”

“我到这家来正经拜会。”

===Original===
“Which house?”

“This one. Number sixteen. Waddington, Sigsbee H.”

Mr. Waddington regarded him with unconcealed hostility.

“Oh, you are, are you? Well, it may interest you to learn that I am Sigsbee H. Waddington, and I don’t know you from Adam. So now!”

George gasped.

“You are Sigsbee H. Waddington?” he said reverently.

“I am.”

===Chinese===
“哪家？”

“就这家。十六号。西格斯比·H·沃丁顿府上。”

沃丁顿先生毫不掩饰地满眼敌意。

“哦，是嘛？那好，告诉你也许值得一听：我就是西格斯比·H·沃丁顿，而你，我认都不认得。就这么着！”

乔治倒抽一口气。

“您就是西格斯比·H·沃丁顿？”他恭恭敬敬地说。

“正是在下。”

===Original===
George was gazing at Molly’s father as at some beautiful work of art—a superb painting, let us say—the sort of thing which connoisseurs fight for and which finally gets knocked down to Dr. Rosenbach for three hundred thousand dollars. Which will give the reader a rough idea of what love can do: for, considered in a calm and unbiased spirit, Sigsbee Waddington was little, if anything, to look at.

“Mr. Waddington,” said George, “I am proud to meet you.”

“You’re what?”

“Proud to meet you.”

“What of it?” said Sigsbee Waddington churlishly.

“Mr. Waddington,” said George, “I was born in Idaho.”

===Chinese===
乔治望着莫莉的父亲，如同望着一幅绝美的艺术品——姑且说，一幅旷世名画——行家们抢破头争夺、最终由罗森巴赫博士（Dr. Rosenbach）以三十万美元落槌购得的那种东西。这大约能让读者约略领会爱情的伟力：因为，平心静气、不偏不倚地看，西格斯比·沃丁顿这张脸实在乏善可陈，即便有，也寥寥无几。

“沃丁顿先生，”乔治说，“认识您，我深感荣幸。”

“你说什么？”

“认识您，深感荣幸。”

“那又怎样？”西格斯比·沃丁顿没好气地说。

“沃丁顿先生，”乔治说，“我生在爱达荷州。”

===Original===
Much has been written of the sedative effect of pouring oil on the raging waters of the ocean, and it is on record that the vision of the Holy Grail, sliding athwart a rainbow, was generally sufficient to still the most fiercely warring passions of young knights in the Middle Ages. But never since history began can there have been so sudden a change from red-eyed hostility to smiling benevolence as occurred now in Sigsbee H. Waddington. As George’s words, like some magic spell, fell upon his ears, he forgot that one of those ears was smarting badly as the result of the impulsive clutch of this young man before him. Wrath melted from his soul like dew from a flower beneath the sun. He beamed on George. He pawed George’s sleeve with a paternal hand.

“You really come from the West?” he cried.

“I do.”

===Chinese===
往海上怒涛里浇油能收镇静之效，前人著述已备；且史有明载，中世纪的年轻骑士只消一睹圣杯的幻象横贯彩虹滑过，再凶猛的厮杀血气多半也就此平息。然而自有史以来，只怕还不曾有过这般立竿见影的转变——从吹胡子瞪眼的敌视，一下变成满面春风的和气——像此刻的西格斯比·H·沃丁顿这样。乔治的话音如魔咒般落进他耳朵里，他登时忘了其中一只耳朵还火辣辣地疼着——拜眼前这位年轻人冲动的一攥所赐。怒气从他心头消融，像日光底下的花朵抖落露珠。他朝乔治满面堆笑，伸出慈父般的手，扒拉乔治的衣袖。

“你真是打西部来的？”他喊道。

“是啊。”

===Original===
“From God’s own country? From the great wonderful West with its wide open spaces where a red-blooded man can fill his lungs with the breath of freedom?”

It was not precisely the way George would have described East Gilead, which was a stuffy little hamlet with a poorish water-supply and one of the worst soda-fountains in Idaho, but he nodded amiably.

Mr. Waddington dashed a hand across his eyes.

“The West! Why, it’s like a mother to me! I love every flower that blooms on the broad bosom of its sweeping plains, every sun-kissed peak of its everlasting hills.”

George said he did, too.

“Its beautiful valleys, mystic in their transparent, luminous gloom, weird in the quivering, golden haze of the lightning that flickers over them.”

===Chinese===
“从上帝亲自看顾的国土来？从那片壮丽神奇的西部、那片辽阔的旷野来？在那儿，一腔热血的男子汉可以吸满一肺自由气息！”

这倒不完全是乔治描述东吉利亚德（East Gilead）的方式——那是座闷热的小村落，供水寒碜，冷饮柜的糟糕程度在爱达荷州也排得上前几名——不过他还是和颜悦色地点了点头。

沃丁顿先生抬手在眼前用力一抹。

“西部啊！它简直跟亲娘一个样！我爱它莽莽平原那宽阔胸脯上绽放的每一朵花，爱它万古群山沐着日吻的每一座峰。”

乔治说他也是。

“它那美丽的山谷，澄澈的幽暗里透着神秘，电光在谷上闪烁，金色雾霭颤动，诡异万状。”

===Original===
“Ah!” said George.

“The dark spruces tipped with glimmering lights! The aspens bent low in the wind, like waves in a tempest at sea!”

“Can you beat them!” said George.

“The forest of oaks tossing wildly and shining with gleams of fire!”

“What could be sweeter?” said George.

“Say, listen,” said Mr. Waddington, “you and I must see more of each other. Come and have a bite of dinner!”

===Chinese===
“啊！”乔治说。

“那深暗的云杉，梢头闪着微光！那白杨在风里深深弯腰，活像海上暴风雨里的波浪！”

“谁比得了它们！”乔治说。

“那栎树林子疯狂翻腾，火光点点，熠熠生辉！”

“还有什么比这更甜美？”乔治说。

“我说，听着，”沃丁顿先生说，“咱们俩得多来往。来吃口晚饭吧！”

===Original===
“Now?”

“Right this very minute. We’ve got a few of these puny-souled Eastern millionaires putting on the nosebag with us tonight, but you won’t mind them. We’ll just look at ’em and despise ’em. And after dinner, you and I will slip off to my study and have a good chat.”

“But won’t Mrs. Waddington object to an unexpected guest at the last moment?”

Mr. Waddington expanded his chest, and tapped it spaciously.

“Say, listen—what’s your name?—Finch?—Say, listen, Finch, do I look like the sort of man who’s bossed by his wife?”

It was precisely the sort of man that George thought he did look like, but this was not the moment to say so.

===Chinese===
“现在？”

“就这会儿。今晚有几个这种没魂儿的东部百万富翁要跟咱们一块儿上槽吃料，不过你甭把他们放在心上。咱们就瞧他们一眼，鄙夷一番。饭后，你我溜进我的书房，好好聊一聊。”

“可是临上饭桌又添个不速之客，沃丁顿夫人不会见怪吗？”

沃丁顿先生挺起胸膛，豪迈地叩了两下。

“我说，听着——你叫什么来着？——芬奇？——我说，听着，芬奇，我瞧着像那种叫老婆管着的人吗？”

乔治心想，他瞧着恰恰就是那种人，只是这话眼下说不得。

===Original===
“It’s very kind of you,” he said.

“Kind? Say, listen, if I was riding along those illimitable prairies and got storm-bound outside your ranch at East Gilead, you wouldn’t worry about whether you were being kind when you asked me in for a bite, would you? You’d say, ‘Step right in, pardner! The place is yours.’ Very well, then!”

Mr. Waddington produced a latchkey.

“Ferris,” said Mr. Waddington in the hall, “tell those galoots down in the kitchen to set another place at table. A pard of mine from the West has happened in for a snack.”

===Chinese===
“您太客气了。”他说。

“客气？我说，听着，倘若我骑马跑在那无边无际的大草原上，风暴把我困在你东吉利亚德牧场的门外，你请我进去吃口东西的时候，还会计较客气不客气吗？你只会说：‘快请进来，伙计！这地方就是你的。’这不就结了！”

沃丁顿先生掏出一把自家的钥匙。

“费里斯，”沃丁顿先生走进门厅说，“告诉厨房底下那帮笨瓜，桌上再添一副餐具。我西部的一位老伙计顺路过来垫补一口。”

## III / 第三章

### I / 一

===Original===

The perfect hostess makes a point of never displaying discomposure. In moments of trial she aims at the easy repose of manner of a Red Indian at the stake. Nevertheless, there was a moment when, as she saw Sigsbee H. caracole into the drawing-room with George and heard him announce in a ringing voice that this fine young son of the western prairies had come to take potluck, Mrs. Waddington indisputably reeled.

She recovered herself. All the woman in her was urging her to take Sigsbee H. by his outstanding ears and shake him till he came unstuck, but she fought the emotion down. Gradually her glazed eye lost its dead-fishy look. Like Death in the poem, she “grinned horrible a ghastly smile.” And it was with a well-assumed graciousness that she eventually extended to George the quivering right hand which, had she been a less highly civilised woman, would about now have been landing on the side of her husband’s head, swung from the hip.

“Chahmed!” said Mrs. Waddington. “So very, very glad that you were able to come, Mr.—”

She paused, and George, eyeing her mistily, gathered that she wished to be informed of his name. He would have been glad to supply the information, but unfortunately at the moment he had forgotten it himself. He had a dim sort of idea that it began with an *F* or a *G*, but beyond that his mind was a blank.

The fact was that, in the act of shaking hands with his hostess, George Finch had caught sight of Molly, and the spectacle had been a little too much for him.

Molly was wearing the new evening dress of which she had spoken so feelingly to her father at their recent interview, and it seemed to George as if the scales had fallen from his eyes and he was seeing her for the first time. Before, in a vague way he had supposed that she possessed arms and shoulders and hair, but it was only at this moment that he perceived how truly these arms and those shoulders and that hair were arms and shoulders and hair in the deepest and holiest sense of the words. It was as if a goddess had thrown aside the veil. It was as if a statue had come to life. It was as if … well, the point we are trying to make is that George Finch was impressed. His eyes enlarged to the dimensions of saucers; the tip of his nose quivered like a rabbit’s: and unseen hands began to pour iced water down his spine.

===Chinese===

完美的女主人素来以不动声色为本分。到了紧要关头，她一心追求的，是火刑柱上的印第安人那种安详从容的气度。然而，当她眼看着西格斯比·H（Sigsbee H.）挽着乔治·芬奇（George Finch）踌躇满志地旋进客厅，又听见他亮开洪钟般的嗓门宣布，这位西部草原的好儿郎是来蹭一顿便饭的，沃丁顿夫人（Mrs. Waddington）毫无疑问踉跄了一瞬。

她稳住了。她满腔的女性本能都在怂恿她一把揪住西格斯比·H那对招风大耳，把他摇到散架为止，可她把这一股情绪硬生生压了下去。呆滞的目光渐渐褪去了死鱼眼似的神情。她如同诗篇里的死神，“咧出一副狰狞可怖的狞笑”。最终，她以一副假扮得天衣无缝的和蔼，向乔治伸出了那只微微发颤的右手——倘若她是一位教养稍逊的妇人，这只手此刻早已从胯侧抡圆，正落在她丈夫的脑袋侧面上了。

“幸——会！”沃丁顿夫人说，“您能赏光前来，真是太高兴、太高兴了，先生——”

她顿住了。乔治迷迷瞪瞪地望着她，明白她是想知道自己的姓名。他本也乐于奉告，无奈此刻连他自己也想不起来了。他隐隐约约觉得那名字八成以 F 或者 G 开头，除此之外，脑子里一片空白。

事情是这样的：就在同女主人握手的那一刹那，乔治·芬奇瞥见了莫莉·沃丁顿（Molly Waddington），那幅光景实在叫他有点消受不起。

莫莉穿上了不久前同父亲恳谈时动情说起的那件新晚礼服。在乔治看来，仿佛鳞翳从双目剥落，他才头一回真正看清了她。从前，他只是迷迷糊糊地知道她也长着手臂、肩膀和头发；直到此刻他才领悟，这手臂、这肩膀、这头发，才是最深、最神圣意义上的手臂、肩膀与头发。仿佛女神揭开了面纱。仿佛雕像活了过来。仿佛……总而言之，我们想说的重点是：乔治·芬奇被镇住了。他的双眼瞪得有茶碟那么大；鼻尖像兔子似的直颤；还有看不见的手开始顺着他的脊梁骨浇冰水。

===Original===

Mrs. Waddington, having given him a long, steady look that blistered his forehead, turned away and began to talk to a soda-water magnate. She had no real desire to ascertain George’s name, though she would have read it with pleasure on a tombstone.

“Dinner is served,” announced Ferris, the butler, appearing noiselessly like a Djinn summoned by the rubbing of a lamp.

George found himself swept up in the stampede of millionaires. He was still swallowing feebly.

There are few things more embarrassing to a shy and sensitive young man than to be present at a dinner-party where something seems to tell him he is not really wanted. The something that seemed to tell George Finch he was not really wanted at tonight’s festive gathering was Mrs. Waddington’s eye, which kept shooting down the table at intervals and reducing him to pulp at those very moments when he was beginning to feel that, if treated with gentle care and kindness, he might eventually recover.

It was an eye that, like a thermos flask, could be alternately extremely hot and intensely cold. When George met it during the soup course he had the feeling of having encountered a simoom while journeying across an African desert. When, on the other hand, it sniped him as he toyed with his fish, his sensations were those of a searcher for the Pole who unexpectedly bumps into a blizzard. But, whether it was cold or hot, there was always in Mrs. Waddington’s gaze one constant factor—a sort of sick loathing which nothing that he could ever do, George felt, would have the power to allay. It was the kind of look which Sisera might have surprised in the eye of Jael the wife of Heber, had he chanced to catch it immediately before she began operations with the spike. George had made one new friend that night, but not two.

The consequence was that as regards George Finch’s contribution to the feast of wit and flow of soul at that dinner-party we have nothing to report. He uttered no epigrams. He told no good stories. Indeed, the only time he spoke at all was when he said “Sherry” to the footman when he meant “Hock.”

===Chinese===

沃丁顿夫人久久地、笔直地瞪了他一眼，瞪得他脑门上火辣辣地起了泡，随即转过身去，同一位汽水业大亨攀谈起来。她其实并无真意要弄清乔治姓甚名谁——不过哪天在墓碑上读到这个名字，她想必会颇为受用。

“晚餐备好了。”男管家费里斯（Ferris）通报道，悄没声儿地现出身形，活像搓一搓神灯召出来的灯神。

乔治发觉自己已被卷进百万富翁们蜂拥而入的洪流。他还在有气无力地做吞咽动作。

对一个腼腆而敏感的年轻人来说，世上很少有比出席一场晚宴、却又总觉得有什么在暗示他此处并不欢迎他更难堪的事了。今晚这场欢宴上，那个暗示乔治·芬奇他并不受欢迎的“什么”，便是沃丁顿夫人的眼睛。那只眼睛每隔一阵便沿餐桌扫射过来，偏挑在他刚开始觉得、只要得到温柔体贴的照料自己兴许还能缓过来的时候，把他碾成一摊烂泥。

那只眼睛跟保温瓶一样，能够滚烫与冰寒交替换挡。喝汤的时候乔治迎上它，感觉就像横越非洲沙漠时撞上一场西蒙风（simoom，沙漠焚风）；而当他摆弄着盘中的鱼、被它冷枪狙击时，那滋味又如同探寻北极的旅人猝不及防地闯进一场暴风雪。可是无论冷热，沃丁顿夫人的目光里总有一个恒定不变的要素——一种病态的憎恶。乔治觉得，自己无论做什么，都休想将它平息。那眼神，就像西西拉（Sisera）万一赶在希伯之妻雅亿（Jael）抡起帐棚橛子动手之前觑上一眼，会在她眼里瞧见的那种。那天晚上，乔治交上了一位新朋友，可也就仅此一位，交不上第二位了。

结果，对于乔治·芬奇在那场妙语纷飞、才思涌流的晚宴上所作的贡献，我们无可奉告。他没吐出一句警句，没讲上一个好故事。老实说，他统共只开过一次口——本想说“霍克酒”（Hock，莱茵白葡萄酒），却对男仆说了声“雪利酒”。

===Original===

Even, however, had the conditions been uniformly pleasant, it is to be doubted whether he would have really dominated the gathering. Mrs. Waddington, in her selection of guests, confined herself to the extremely wealthy: and, while the conversation of the extremely wealthy is fascinating in its way, it tends to be a little too technical for the average man.

With the soup, someone who looked like a cartoon of Capital in a Socialistic paper said he was glad to see that Westinghouse Common were buoyant again. A man who might have been his brother agreed that they had firmed up nicely at closing. Whereas Wabash Pref. A, falling to 73⅞, caused shakings of the head. However, one rather liked the look of Royal Dutch Oil Ordinaries at 54¾.

With the fish, United Beef began to tell a neat, though rather long, story about the Bolivian Land Concession, the gist of which was that the Bolivian Oil and Land Syndicate, acquiring from the Bolivian Government the land and prospecting concessions of Bolivia, would be known as Bolivian Concessions, Ltd., and would have a capital of one million dollars in two hundred thousand five-dollar “A” shares and two hundred thousand half-dollar “B” shares, and that while no cash payment was to be made to the vendor syndicate the latter was being allotted the whole of the “B” shares as consideration for the concession. And—this was where the raconteur made his point—the “B” shares were to receive half the divisible profits and to rank equal with the “A” shares in any distribution of assets.

The story went well, and conversation became general. There was a certain amount of good-natured chaff about the elasticity of the form of credit handled by the Commercial Banks, and once somebody raised a laugh with a sly retort about the Reserve against Circulation and Total Deposits. On the question of the collateral liability of shareholders, however, argument ran high, and it was rather a relief when, as tempers began to get a little heated, Mrs. Waddington gave the signal and the women left the table.

Coffee having been served and cigars lighted, the magnates drew together at the end of the table where Mr. Waddington sat. But Mr. Waddington, adroitly sidestepping, left them and came down to George.

“Out West,” said Mr. Waddington in a rumbling undertone, malevolently eyeing Amalgamated Toothbrushes, who had begun to talk about the Mid-Continent Fiduciary Conference at St. Louis, “they would shoot at that fellow’s feet.”

===Chinese===

不过，即便席间氛围一路怡人，他是否真能主宰全场也颇可怀疑。沃丁顿夫人挑选客人，只盯着巨富这一档；而巨富们的谈吐固然自有一种迷人之处，对普通人来说，往往又未免太专业了一点。

喝汤时，一位长相活像社会主义报纸上“资本”漫画人物的先生表示，欣见威斯汀豪斯普通股重又坚挺。另一位八成是他兄弟的先生附和说，收盘时确实稳稳走强。至于沃巴什优先股 A 跌到 73⅞，则引来一片摇头。不过，荷兰皇家石油普通股在 54¾ 上，倒是相当叫人看好。

吃鱼时，联合牛肉公司开讲了一则关于玻利维亚土地特许权的故事，讲得干净利落，只是略嫌冗长。大意是：玻利维亚石油与土地辛迪加从玻利维亚政府手中取得全国土地及勘探特许权之后，将更名为玻利维亚特许权有限公司，资本总额一百万美元，分为二十万股面值五美元的“A”股与二十万股面值半美元的“B”股；出让辛迪加虽分文现金不取，却将获配全部“B”股，作为出让特许权的对价。而且——这正是讲故事人的点睛之笔——“B”股可分得可分配利润的一半，并在任何资产分配中与“A”股享有同等顺位。

故事讲得大获成功，谈话于是漫无边际地铺展开来。关于商业银行经手的信贷形式何等富有弹性，席间不乏善意的调侃；还有一回，有人就流通准备金与存款总额来了一句狡黠的妙答，惹起一阵哄笑。然而，一转到股东的连带责任问题，争辩便趋于白热。正当火药味渐渐浓起来的时候，沃丁顿夫人打出信号，女眷们离席，众人才算松了口气。

咖啡奉上、雪茄点着之后，诸位大亨聚拢到沃丁顿先生（Mr. Waddington）坐的那一头。可沃丁顿先生身法灵巧地一闪避，撇下他们，径直来到乔治跟前。

“在西部老家，”沃丁顿先生压着嗓子咕哝道，一双眼睛恶狠狠地盯着联合牙刷公司——这位刚刚开口谈起了圣路易斯中大陆信托业会议——“他们准会朝那家伙的脚板开枪。”

===Original===

George agreed that such behaviour could reflect nothing but credit on the West.

“These Easterners make me tired,” said Mr. Waddington.

George confessed to a similar fatigue.

“When you think that at this very moment out in Utah and Arizona,” said Mr. Waddington, “strong men are packing their saddlebags and making them secure with their lassoes you kind of don’t know whether to laugh or cry, do you?”

That was the very problem, said George.

“Say, listen,” said Mr. Waddington, “I’ll just push these potbellied guys off upstairs, and then you and I will sneak off to my study and have a real talk.”

===Chinese===

乔治表示赞同：这等行径只会给西部增光添彩。

“这些东部人真叫我腻歪。”沃丁顿先生说。

乔治供认，自己也有着同样的疲乏。

“你想想，就在此时此刻，在犹他和亚利桑那，”沃丁顿先生说，“多少硬汉正收拾着马鞍行囊，又用套索捆得结结实实，这会儿你简直不知道该笑还是该哭，对不对？”

乔治说，这正是难题所在。

“喂，听着，”沃丁顿先生说，“我去把这群大腹便便的家伙轰上楼，然后你我俩溜到我的书房里，好好唠一唠。”

### II / 二

===Original===

Nothing spoils a tête-à-tête chat between two newly-made friends more than a disposition towards reticence on the part of the senior of the pair: and it was fortunate therefore, that, by the time he found himself seated opposite to George in his study, the heady influence of Zane Grey and the rather generous potations in which he had indulged during dinner had brought Sigsbee H. Waddington to quite a reasonably communicative mood. He had reached the stage when men talk disparagingly about their wives. He tapped George on the knee, informed him three times that he liked his face, and began.

“You married, Winch?”

“Finch,” said George.

“How do you mean, Finch?” asked Mr. Waddington, puzzled.

“My name is Finch.”

“What of it?”

===Chinese===

两个新结交的朋友促膝密谈，最扫兴的莫过于年长的这一位惜字如金；所以也算侥幸，等他在书房里坐到乔治对面时，赞恩·格雷（Zane Grey）的醉人熏陶，加上晚宴间相当放量的一通畅饮，已经把西格斯比·H·沃丁顿送到了相当肯开怀倾谈的心境。他已抵达男人们开始数落自家太太的阶段。他拍拍乔治的膝盖，一连三遍宣称自己喜欢他这张脸，随后开了腔。

“你结婚了没有，温奇？”

“是芬奇。”乔治说。

“你这算什么意思，芬奇？”沃丁顿先生一头雾水地问。

“我的名字是芬奇。”

“那又怎么啦？”

===Original===

“You called me Winch.”

“Why?”

“I think you thought it was my name.”

“What was?”

“Winch.”

“You said just now it was Finch.”

===Chinese===

“你刚才叫我温奇。”

“为什么？”

“我猜你当它是我的名字来着。”

“当什么来着？”

“温奇。”

“你方才不是说叫芬奇吗。”

===Original===

“Yes, it is. I was saying. …”

Mr. Waddington tapped him on the knee once more.

“Young man,” he said, “pull yourself together. If your name is Finch, why pretend that it is Winch? I don’t like this shiftiness. It does not come well from a Westerner. Leave this petty shilly-shallying to Easterners like that vile rabble of widow-and-orphan oppressors upstairs, all of whom have got incipient Bright’s Disease. If your name is Pinch, admit it like a man. Let your yea be yea and your nay be nay,” said Mr. Waddington a little severely, holding a match to the fountain-pen which, as will happen to the best of us in moments of emotion, he had mistaken for his cigar.

“As a matter of fact, I’m not,” said George.

“Not what?”

“Married.”

===Chinese===

“对，是芬奇。我正要说的是……”

沃丁顿先生又拍了拍他的膝盖。

“年轻人，”他说，“打起精神来。你的名字既然是芬奇，何苦假称它叫温奇？我不喜欢这种鬼鬼祟祟。这副做派从一个西部人口里说出来可不像话。这点磨磨唧唧的小家子气，就留给楼上那帮恶棍渣滓——那伙压榨寡妇孤儿的东部佬——去干吧，他们个个都得了初期的布莱特氏肾病。你的名字就算是平奇，也得像个男子汉那样认下它。是就说是，不是就说不是。”沃丁顿先生口气颇为严厉，一面划着火柴凑向那支钢笔——人在情绪激动的当口，再了不得的人物也难免把钢笔错当成雪茄。

“其实，我不是。”乔治说。

“不是什么？”

“没结婚。”

===Original===

“I never said you were.”

“You asked me if I was.”

“Did I?”

“Yes.”

“You’re sure of that?” said Mr. Waddington keenly.

“Quite. Just after we sat down, you asked me if I was married.”

===Chinese===

“我又没说你结了。”

“你刚才问我结没结来着。”

“我问过吗？”

“问过。”

“你敢肯定？”沃丁顿先生急切地问。

“肯定。咱们刚坐下的时候，你问我结婚了没有。”

===Original===

“And your reply was … ?”

“No.”

Mr. Waddington breathed a sigh of relief.

“Now we have got it straight at last,” he said, “and why you beat about the bush like that, I cannot imagine. Well, what I say to you, Pinch—and I say it very seriously as an older, wiser, and better-looking man—is this.” Mr. Waddington drew thoughtfully at the fountain-pen for a moment. “I say to you, Pinch, be very careful, when you marry, that you have money of your own. And, having money of your own, keep it. Never be dependent on your wife for the occasional little sums which even the most prudent man requires to see him through the day. Take my case. When I married, I was a wealthy man. I had money of my own. Lots of it. I was beloved by all, being generous to a fault. I bought my wife—I am speaking now of my first wife—a pearl necklace that cost fifty thousand dollars.”

He cocked a bright eye at George, and George, feeling that comment was required, said that it did him credit.

“Not credit,” said Mr. Waddington. “Cash. Cold cash. Fifty thousand dollars of it. And what happened? Shortly after I married again I lost all my money through unfortunate speculations on the Stock Exchange and became absolutely dependent on my second wife. And that is why you see me today, Winch, a broken man. I will tell you something, Pinch—something no one suspects and something which I have never told anybody else and wouldn’t be telling you now if I didn’t like your face. … I am not master in my own home!”

===Chinese===

“那你的回答是……”

“没有。”

沃丁顿先生如释重负地舒了一口气。

“这下总算说明白了，”他说，“你干吗要那样拐弯抹角，我真想不通。好吧，我跟你说一句掏心窝子的话，平奇——这话我以一个比你年长、比你睿智、也比你俊不少的人的身份，说得非常郑重。”沃丁顿先生对着钢笔若有所思地嘬了一口。“我跟你说，平奇，将来结婚的时候千万留神，一定要有自己的钱。有了自己的钱，还得攥牢它。千万别落到靠老婆接济的地步——隔三差五的小开销，再谨小慎微的男人也得靠它撑过一天。拿我自己来说。我结婚那阵子，是个有钱人。我有自己的钱。多得很。人人都爱我，因为我慷慨得没边儿。我给我太太——我说的是我第一任太太——买过一条珍珠项链，足足花了五万美元。”

他朝乔治斜翘起一只晶亮的眼角。乔治觉得总该评点什么，便说这实在令人敬佩。

“不是敬佩，”沃丁顿先生说，“是现金。冷冰冰的现金。整整五万美元。结果怎么样？续弦之后没多久，我在证券交易所投机失手，赔了个精光，从此完全仰仗我第二任太太的鼻息过活。所以你今天见到的温奇，才是个垮掉了的人。我跟你说件事儿，平奇——一件谁也想不到的事，一件我从没对旁人吐露过的事，要不是喜欢你这张脸，如今也不会对你讲。……我在自己家里做不了主！”

===Original===

“No?”

“No. Not master in my own home. I want to live in the great, glorious West, and my second wife insists on remaining in the soul-destroying East. And I’ll tell you something else.” Mr. Waddington paused and scrutinised the fountain-pen with annoyance. “This darned cigar won’t draw,” he said petulantly.

“I think it’s a fountain-pen,” said George.

“A fountain-pen?” Mr. Waddington, shutting one eye, tested this statement and found it correct. “There!” he said, with a certain moody satisfaction. “Isn’t that typical of the East? You ask for cigars and they sell you fountain-pens. No honesty, no sense of fair trade.”

“Miss Waddington was looking very charming at dinner, I thought,” said George, timidly broaching the subject nearest his heart.

“Yes, Pinch,” said Mr. Waddington, resuming his theme, “my wife oppresses me.”

===Chinese===

“做不了主？”

“做不了主。在自己家里做不了主。我想住到那伟大、辉煌的西部去，我第二任太太却死活要赖在这片消磨灵魂的东部。我再告诉你一件事。”沃丁顿先生顿了顿，恼火地端详起那支钢笔。“这根该死的雪茄抽不动。”他使着性子说。

“我看这是一支钢笔。”乔治说。

“钢笔？”沃丁顿先生眯起一只眼验了验，发现此言不虚。“你瞧！”他带着几分悻悻然的得意说，“这不就是东部的做派吗？你要买雪茄，他们卖给你钢笔。毫无诚信，毫无公平交易可言。”

“我觉得，晚餐桌上沃丁顿小姐可真迷人。”乔治说，怯生生地挑起了那个最贴近他心窝的话头。

“是啊，平奇，”沃丁顿先生绕回原先的话头说，“我太太欺压我。”

===Original===

“How wonderfully that bobbed hair suits Miss Waddington.”

“I don’t know if you noticed a pie-faced fellow with an eyeglass and a toothbrush moustache at dinner? That was Lord Hunstanton. He keeps telling me things about etiquette.”

“Very kind of him,” hazarded George.

Mr. Waddington eyed him in a manner that convinced him that he had said the wrong thing.

“What do you mean, kind of him? It’s officious and impertinent. He is a pest,” said Mr. Waddington. “They wouldn’t stand for him in Arizona. They would put hydrophobia skunks in his bed. What does a man need with etiquette? As long as a man is fearless and upstanding and can shoot straight and look the world in the eye, what does it matter if he uses the wrong fork?”

“Exactly.”

===Chinese===

“沃丁顿小姐那头波波短发剪得多好看，太衬她了。”

“不知道你留意到没有，晚餐桌上有个大饼脸的家伙，戴着单片眼镜，蓄着牙刷胡子？那就是亨斯坦顿勋爵（Lord Hunstanton）。他老跟我念叨什么礼仪规矩。”

“他人真好。”乔治壮着胆子搭了一句。

沃丁顿先生盯着他的那副神情，让他确信自己又说错了话。

“好？你还好意思说好？这叫多管闲事，叫放肆无礼。他就是个害人精。”沃丁顿先生说，“在亚利桑那，谁也容不下这种人。人家会往他床上塞几只患狂犬病的臭鼬。要那劳什子礼仪有什么用？只要一个人无所畏惧、顶天立地，枪打得准，眼睛敢直视这个世界，叉子拿错了把又有什么要紧？”

“一点儿没错。”

===Original===

“Or wears the wrong sort of hat?”

“I particularly admired the hat which Miss Waddington was wearing when I first saw her,” said George. “It was of some soft material and of a light brown colour and. …”

“My wife—I am still speaking of my second wife. My first, poor soul, is dead—sicks this Hunstanton guy on to me, and for financial reasons, darn it, I am unable to give him the good soak on the nose to which all my better instincts urge me. And guess what she’s got into her head now.”

“I can’t imagine.”

“She wants Molly to marry the fellow.”

“I should not advise that,” said George seriously. “No, no, I am strongly opposed to that. So many of these Anglo-American marriages turn out unhappily.”

===Chinese===

“帽子戴错了款式又怎样？”

“我尤其欣赏沃丁顿小姐头回见面时戴的那顶帽子。”乔治说，“料子软软的，浅棕色，而且……”

“我太太——我还在说我第二任。头一任，可怜的人儿，已经过世了——撺掇这个亨斯坦顿家伙来缠我，而为了钱袋的缘故，该死，我只好死死按捺住天性，不能照着良心结结实实给他的鼻子来上一拳。你猜她这会儿又打起了什么主意。”

“我猜不着。”

“她要让莫莉嫁给那家伙。”

“这个主意我可不敢恭维。”乔治郑重其事地说，“不行，不行，我坚决反对。这种英美联姻，落得个不幸收场的例子太多了。”

===Original===

“I am a man of broad sympathies and a very acute sensibility,” began Mr. Waddington, apropos, apparently, of nothing.

“Besides,” said George, “I did not like the man’s looks.”

“What man?”

“Lord Hunstanton.”

“Don’t talk of that guy! He gives me a pain in the neck.”

“Me, too,” said George. “And I was saying. …”

===Chinese===

“我这个人胸怀宽广，感情又特别细腻。”沃丁顿先生开了口，说的显然跟什么全不挨着。

“况且，”乔治说，“我不喜欢那人那副长相。”

“哪个人？”

“亨斯坦顿勋爵。”

“别提那家伙！他叫我脖颈子疼。”

“我也是。”乔治说，“我刚才正要说……”

===Original===

“Shall I tell you something?” said Mr. Waddington.

“What?”

“My second wife—not my first—wants Molly to marry him. Did you notice him at dinner?”

“I did,” said George patiently. “And I did not like his looks. He looked to me cold and sinister, the sort of man who might break the heart of an impulsive young girl. What Miss Waddington wants, I feel convinced, is a husband who would give up everything for her—a man who would sacrifice his heart’s desire to bring one smile to her face—a man who would worship her, set her in a shrine, make it his only aim in life to bring her sunshine and happiness.”

“My wife,” said Mr. Waddington, “is much too stout.”

“I beg your pardon?”

===Chinese===

“我跟你说了件事儿吧？”沃丁顿先生说。

“什么？”

“我第二任太太——不是头一任——想让莫莉嫁给他。晚餐桌上你留意他了吗？”

“留意了。”乔治耐着性子说，“而且我不喜欢他那副长相。在我眼里，他冷漠而阴险，是那种会把一个热血冲动的年轻姑娘的心伤碎的男人。我深信，沃丁顿小姐想要的是能为她舍弃一切的男人——为了博她嫣然一笑，宁可牺牲自己心头的至爱——是把她供上神龛的男人，此生唯一的志向便是给她送去阳光和幸福。”

“我太太，”沃丁顿先生说，“胖得实在不像话。”

“您说什么？”

===Original===

“Much too stout.”

“Miss Waddington, if I may say so, has a singularly beautiful figure.”

“Too much starchy food, and no exercise—that’s the trouble. What my wife needs is a year on a ranch, riding over the prairies in God’s sunshine.”

“I happened to catch sight of Miss Waddington the other day in riding costume. I thought it suited her admirably. So many girls look awkward in riding-breeches, but Miss Waddington was charming. The costume seemed to accentuate what I might describe as that strange boyish jauntiness of carriage which, to my mind, is one of Miss Waddington’s chief. …”

“And I’ll have her doing it before long. As a married man, Winch—twice married, but my first wife, poor thing, passed away some years back—let me tell you something. To assert himself with his wife, to bend her to his will, if I may put it that way, a man needs complete financial independence. It is no use trying to bend your wife to your will when five minutes later you have got to try and wheedle twenty-five cents out of her for a cigar. Complete financial independence is essential, Pinch, and that is what I am on the eve of achieving. Some little time back, having raised a certain sum of money—we need not go into the methods which I employed to do so—I bought a large block of stock in a Hollywood Motion Picture Company. Have you ever heard of the Finer and Better Motion Picture Company of Hollywood, Cal.? Let me tell you that you will. It is going to be big, and I shall very shortly make an enormous fortune.”

“Talking of the motion-pictures,” said George, “I do not deny that many of the women engaged in that industry are superficially attractive, but what I do maintain is that they lack Miss Waddington’s intense purity of expression. To me Miss Waddington seems like some. …”

===Chinese===

“胖得实在不像话。”

“恕我冒昧，沃丁顿小姐的身段可是一等一的漂亮。”

“淀粉质的东西吃太多，又从来不运动——毛病全在这儿。我太太需要的是去牧场待上一年，在上帝的阳光里骑马驰过大草原。”

“前几天我碰巧瞧见沃丁顿小姐穿着骑装。我觉得那身衣裳跟她相称极了。多少姑娘穿上马裤都显得笨手笨脚，沃丁顿小姐却俏生生的一片丰采。那身骑装似乎愈发衬出了——姑且这么形容吧——她那股奇特的、少年人似的轻快神气。依我看，那正是沃丁顿小姐最出众的……”

“而且要不了多久，我就送她去骑了。作为过来人，温奇——我结过两次婚，头一任太太，可怜的人，几年前过世了——让我跟你说句实话。一个男人要想在太太面前挺直腰杆，要把她的性子掰过来——这么说吧——他就得有完完全全的经济独立。要是五分钟后你就得嬉皮笑脸跟老婆讨二十五美分买雪茄，还掰什么性子。彻底的经济独立是根本，平奇，而这正是我即将到手的东西。前些日子，我筹到了一笔款子——用什么法子筹的，就不必细说了——买下好莱坞一家电影公司的一大笔股票。听说过加利福尼亚州好莱坞的更精更美电影公司（Finer and Better Motion Picture Company）吗？我告诉你，你准会听说的。它就要一鸣惊人了，我很快就要发一笔大财。”

“说起电影这行当，”乔治说，“我不否认，那一行里许多女子乍一看都妩媚动人，可我始终坚持一点：她们缺少沃丁顿小姐那种极致清纯的神情。在我眼里，沃丁顿小姐宛如……”

===Original===

“I shall clean up big. It is only a question of time.”

“The first thing anyone would notice on seeing Miss Waddington. …”

“Thousands and thousands of dollars. And then. …”

“A poet has spoken of a young girl as ‘standing with reluctant feet where the brook and river meet. …’ ”

Mr. Waddington shook his head.

“It isn’t only meat. What causes the real trouble is the desserts. It stands to reason that if a woman insists on cramming herself with rich stuff like what we were having tonight she is bound to put on weight. If I’ve said it once, I’ve said it a hundred times. …”

===Chinese===

“我要大捞一票。无非是早晚的事。”

“任何人头一眼看见沃丁顿小姐，头一个注意到的就是……”

“成千上万、成千上万的美元。然后呢……”

“一位诗人写过，少女就站在‘溪流与江河交汇之处，迈着依依不肯前行的脚步……’”

沃丁顿先生摇了摇头。

“不光是肉的事。真正的祸根是甜点。道理明摆着：一个女人要是死命往肚子里塞今晚这样的油腻厚味，体重必然有增无减。这话我要么不说，一说就说过一百遍了。……”

===Original===

What Mr. Waddington was about to say for the hundred and first time must remain one of the historic mysteries. For, even as he drew in breath the better to say it, the door opened and a radiant vision appeared. Mr. Waddington stopped in mid-sentence, and George’s heart did three back-somersaults and crashed against his front teeth.

“Mother sent me down to see what had become of you,” said Molly.

Mr. Waddington got about halfway towards a look of dignity.

“I am not aware, my dear child,” he said, “that anything has ‘become of me.’ I merely snatched the opportunity of having a quiet talk with this young friend of mine from the West.”

“Well, you can’t have quiet talks with your young friends when you’ve got a lot of important people to dinner.”

“Important people!” Mr. Waddington snorted sternly. “A bunch of super-fatted bits of bad news! In God’s country they would be lynched on sight.”

===Chinese===

沃丁顿先生那第一百零一遍到底要说什么，只好留作一桩历史悬案了。因为，正当他深深吸气、准备再度发表之际，门开了，门口立着一位光华四射的仙子。沃丁顿先生话说到一半戛然而止；乔治的心脏翻了三个后空翻，砰地撞上前牙。

“妈妈叫我下来看看你怎么回事。”莫莉说。

沃丁顿先生摆出一副庄严面孔，大约摆到了一半。

“好孩子，”他说，“我可没听说我‘出了什么事’。我不过是抓住机会，跟我这位西部来的年轻朋友安安静静聊聊。”

“哼，家里宴着这么多要人的时候，你可不能跟你的年轻朋友安安静静聊天。”

“要人！”沃丁顿先生厉声哼了一下，“一群油水肥得流光的坏消息！在上帝的国度，这帮人一露头就得给私刑收拾。”

===Original===

“Mr. Brewster Bodthorne has been asking for you particularly. He wants to play checkers.”

“Hell,” said Mr. Waddington, with the air of quoting something out of Dante, “is full of Brewster Bodthornes.”

Molly put her arms round her father’s neck and kissed him fondly—a proceeding which drew from George a low, sharp howl of suffering like the bubbling cry of some strong swimmer in his agony. There is a limit to what the flesh can bear.

“Darling, you must be good. Up you go at once and be very nice to everybody. I’ll stay here and entertain Mr.—”

“His name is Pinch,” said Mr. Waddington, rising reluctantly and making for the door. “I met him out on the sidewalk where men are men. Get him to tell you all about the West. I can’t remember when I’ve ever heard a man talk so arrestingly. Mr. Winch has held me spellbound. Positively spellbound. And my name,” he concluded, a little incoherently, groping for the door-handle, “is Sigsbee Horatio Waddington and I don’t care who knows it.”

===Chinese===

“布鲁斯特·博德索恩先生（Brewster Bodthorne）一直在特意找你呢。他想跟你下跳棋。”

“地狱里，”沃丁顿先生说，那腔调活像在引用但丁（Dante）的名句，“挤满了布鲁斯特·博德索恩这路人。”

莫莉搂住父亲的脖子，一往情深地亲了他一口——这一举动引得乔治喉咙里迸出一声低沉而尖利的哀嚎，活像灭顶的泅水好汉在剧痛中咕嘟冒泡的悲鸣。血肉之躯的忍耐终究有限。

“亲爱的，你得乖。这就上楼去，对每个人都客客气气的。我留在这儿招待先生——”

“他叫平奇。”沃丁顿先生说着，老大不情愿地起身朝门口挪去。“我是在外头人行道上跟他认识的，那地方才养得出真爷们儿。让他给你讲讲西部的种种。我记不得这辈子还听过哪个人讲话这么抓人。温奇先生把我迷住了，彻彻底底迷住了。还有，我叫——”他语无伦次地收了尾，一面摸索着门把手，“西格斯比·霍雷肖·沃丁顿，爱谁知道谁。”

### III / 三

===Original===

The chief drawback to being a shy man is that in the actual crises of real life you are a very different person from the dashing and resourceful individual whom you have pictured in your solitary daydreams. George Finch, finding himself in the position in which he had so often yearned to be—alone with the girl he loved, felt as if his true self had been suddenly withdrawn and an incompetent understudy substituted at the last moment.

The George with whom he was familiar in daydreams was a splendid fellow—graceful, thoroughly at his ease, and full of the neatest sort of ingratiating conversation. He looked nice, and you could tell by the way he spoke that he was nice. Clever, beyond a doubt—you knew that at once by his epigrams—but not clever in that repellant, cold-hearted modern fashion: for, no matter how brilliantly his talk sparkled, it was plain all the while that his heart was in the right place and that, despite his wonderful gifts, there was not an atom of conceit in his composition. His eyes had an attractive twinkle: his mouth curved from time to time in an alluring smile: his hands were cool and artistic: and his shirtfront did not bulge. George, in short, as he had imagined himself in his daydreams, was practically the answer to the Maiden’s Prayer.

How different was this loathly changeling who now stood on one leg in the library of Number 16, Seventy-Ninth Street, East. In the first place, the fellow had obviously not brushed his hair for several days. Also, he had omitted to wash his hands, and something had caused them to swell up and turn scarlet. Furthermore, his trousers bagged at the knees: his tie was moving up towards his left ear: and his shirtfront protruded hideously like the chest of a pouter pigeon. A noisome sight.

Still, looks are not everything: and if this wretched creature had been able to talk one-tenth as well as the George of the daydreams, something might yet have been saved out of the wreck. But the poor blister was inarticulate as well. All he seemed able to do was clear his throat. And what nice girl’s heart has ever been won by a series of roopy coughs?

And he could not even achieve a reasonable satisfactory expression. When he tried to relax his features (such as they were) into a charming smile, he merely grinned weakly. When he forced himself not to grin, his face froze into a murderous scowl.

But it was his inability to speak that was searing George’s soul. Actually, since the departure of Mr. Waddington, the silence had lasted for perhaps six seconds: but to George Finch it seemed like a good hour. He goaded himself to utterance.

===Chinese===

腼腆的男人最大的苦处在于：真到了人生的关键时刻，你跟独自做白日梦时设想的那位英姿勃发、随机应变的角色，判若两人。乔治·芬奇此刻正处在他千百次渴盼的境地——与心爱的姑娘独处——却只觉得自己这个正主儿被突然撤了下去，临时顶替上来的是个不堪造就的替补演员。

白日梦里他熟悉的那位乔治是个出色人物——风度翩翩，从容不迫，满肚子最讨人欢喜的漂亮谈吐。他模样俊，而且从谈吐一听便知人品好。机敏，更不必说——警句一出便知——但绝不是时下那种惹人反感、冷酷无情的机灵：因为无论他的言谈多么妙趣横生，始终可见他心地端正，而且纵有天大的才分，性情里也不掺一星半点的自负。他的眼睛闪着动人的光彩；嘴角时不时漾起勾人的微笑；双手清凉而富于艺术气息；衬衫前胸也不鼓囊。总而言之，白日梦里想象出来的乔治，简直就是《少女的祈祷》（The Maiden’s Prayer）的活答案——每个闺中少女弹着这支曲子默祷的，正是这样一位如意郎君。

眼下单腿直立在东七十九街 16 号图书室里的这个面目可憎的调包货，却何其不同。首先，这家伙显然一连几天没梳过头。其次，他忘了洗手，两只手不知怎的肿胀起来，红得发紫。再者，他的裤腿在膝盖处打了褶，领带正一路向左耳方向逃窜，衬衫前胸鼓得狰狞，活像球胸鸽的胸脯。惨不忍睹。

不过，相貌并非一切：倘若这个可怜虫说起话来能有白日梦里的乔治十分之一利落，沉船里好歹还能抢出点东西。可惜这颗可怜的水疱连话都说不利索。他好像只会清嗓子。而哪个正经姑娘的心，是靠一连串哑嗓子咳嗽赢来的？

而且，他连一个还算像样的表情都拿不出来。想把五官（就他那副五官而言）松弛成一个迷人的微笑，结果只是虚弱地咧嘴傻笑；强忍住不笑，脸又僵成一团凶神恶煞的怒容。

可真正灼烧乔治灵魂的，是他张不开口。实际上，自打沃丁顿先生走后，这阵沉默至多持续了六秒钟；但在乔治·芬奇看来，足足有一个钟头。他鞭策着自己开口。

===Original===

“My name,” said George, speaking in a low, husky voice, “is not Pinch.”

“Isn’t it?” said the girl. “How jolly!”

“Nor Winch.”

“Better still.”

“It is Finch. George Finch.”

“Splendid!”

===Chinese===

“我的名字，”乔治哑着嗓子低声说，“不是平奇。”

“不是吗？”姑娘说，“真有意思！”

“也不是温奇。”

“更好了。”

“是芬奇。乔治·芬奇。”

“好极了！”

===Original===

She seemed genuinely pleased. She beamed upon him as if he had brought her good news from a distant land.

“Your father,” proceeded George, not having anything to add by way of development of the theme but unable to abandon it, “thought it was Pinch. Or Winch. But it is not. It is Finch.”

His eye, roaming nervously about the room, caught hers for an instant: and he was amazed to perceive that there was in it nothing of that stunned abhorrence which he felt his appearance and behaviour should rightly have aroused in any nice-minded girl. Astounding though it seemed, she appeared to be looking at him in a sort of pleased, maternal way, as if he were a child she was rather fond of. For the first time a faint far-off glimmer of light shone upon George’s darkness. It would be too much to say that he was encouraged, but out of the night that covered him, black as the pit from pole to pole, there did seem to sparkle for an instant a solitary star.

“How did you come to know father?”

George could answer that. He was all right if you asked him questions. It was the having to invent topics of conversation that baffled him.

“I met him outside the house: and when he found that I came from the West he asked me in to dinner.”

===Chinese===

她似乎真心实意地高兴，笑吟吟地望着他，仿佛他从远方给她捎来了喜讯。

“你父亲，”乔治继续说下去，其实没什么可引申的，又撒不下这个话头，“以为是平奇。要不就是温奇。其实都不是。是芬奇。”

他的目光在屋里惶惶然四处乱转，一时撞上了她的眼睛；他愕然发现，那里面竟没有半点他自认自己的仪表举止理应在任何正经姑娘心中激起的那种瞠目生厌。说来骇人，她看他的神情竟有几分欣喜、几分母性，仿佛他是她挺喜欢的孩子。一缕遥远的微光头一回照进乔治的黑暗。说他大受鼓舞未免言过其实，但在那笼罩着他的黑夜里——那从这极到那极漆黑如深渊的黑夜——的确仿佛有颗孤星闪了一瞬。

“你是怎么认识爸爸的？”

这个乔治答得上来。只要人家问他问题，他就一切正常。叫他没话找话，才是要他的命。

“我在这宅子外头碰见他的：他听说我从西部来，就请我进来吃饭了。”

===Original===

“Do you mean he rushed at you and grabbed you as you were walking by?”

“Oh, no. I wasn’t walking by. I was—er—sort of standing on the doorstep. At least. …”

“Standing on the doorstep? Why?”

George’s ears turned a riper red.

“Well, I was—er—coming as it were, to pay a call.”

“A call?”

===Chinese===

“你是说，你打这儿路过，他就冲上来一把攥住了你？”

“哦，不是。我没在路过。我——呃——算是在门阶上站着来着。至少……”

“站在门阶上？为什么？”

乔治的耳朵红得更透了。

“这个，我是——呃——算是来——来拜访的。”

“拜访？”

===Original===

“Yes.”

“On mother?”

“On you.”

The girl’s eyes widened.

“On me?”

“To make inquiries.”

===Chinese===

“是的。”

“拜访妈妈？”

“拜访你。”

姑娘的眼睛睁大了。

“拜访我？”

“来问询一下。”

===Original===

“What about?”

“Your dog.”

“I don’t understand.”

“Well, I thought—result of the excitement—and nerve-strain—I thought he might be upset.”

“Because he ran away, do you mean?”

“Yes.”

===Chinese===

“问什么？”

“你的狗。”

“我不明白。”

“这个，我想着——受了惊吓——再加上神经紧张——我怕它心里会不好受。”

“你是说，因为它跑丢了？”

“对。”

===Original===

“You thought he would have a nervous breakdown because he ran away?”

“Dangerous traffic,” explained George. “Might have been run over. Reaction. Nervous collapse.”

Woman’s intuition is a wonderful thing. There was probably not an alienist in the land who, having listened so far, would not have sprung at George and held him down with one hand while with the other he signed the necessary certificate of lunacy. But Molly Waddington saw deeper into the matter. She was touched. As she realised that this young man thought so highly of her that, despite his painful shyness, he was prepared to try to worm his way into her house on an excuse which even he must have recognised as pure banana-oil, her heart warmed to him. More than ever, she became convinced that George was a lamb and that she wanted to stroke his head and straighten his tie and make cooing noises to him.

“How very sweet of you,” she said.

“Fond of dogs,” mumbled George.

“You must be fond of dogs.”

===Chinese===

“它跑丢了，你就担心它会精神崩溃？”

“车马太危险。”乔治解释道，“差点儿给碾着。应激反应。神经崩溃。”

女人的直觉是件了不得的东西。放眼全国，只怕找不出一位精神病医师，听到这里不会扑向乔治，一只手把他按倒，另一只手签下那张必不可缺的精神失常证明书。可莫莉·沃丁顿偏偏看得更深。她感动了。她意识到，这位年轻人把她看得如此之高，以致硬撑着那副令人替他难受的羞怯，也要想方设法钻进她的家门——凭的还是连他自己都心知肚明纯属天花乱坠的鬼话——她的心便向他暖了过来。她愈发确信，乔治是只小绵羊，她只想摸摸他的脑袋，替他扶正领带，冲他咕咕地哄。

“你真是太好心了。”她说。

“喜欢狗。”乔治咕哝道。

“你一定很喜欢狗啦。”

===Original===

“Are you fond of dogs?”

“Yes, I’m very fond of dogs.”

“So am I. Very fond of dogs.”

“Yes?”

“Yes. Very fond of dogs. Some people are not fond of dogs, but I am.”

And suddenly eloquence descended upon George Finch. With gleaming eyes he broke out into a sort of Litany. He began to talk easily and fluently.

===Chinese===

“你喜欢狗吗？”

“喜欢，我特别喜欢狗。”

“我也是。特别喜欢狗。”

“是吗？”

“是的。特别喜欢狗。有的人不喜欢狗，可我喜欢。”

就在这时，雄辩之神突然降临到乔治·芬奇身上。他两眼放光，念起了连祷文似的一长串。他说得从容流利起来。

===Original===

“I am fond of Airedales and wire-haired terriers and bulldogs and Pekingese and Sealyhams and Alsations and fox-terriers and greyhounds and Aberdeens and West Highlands and Cairns and Pomeranians and spaniels and schipperkes and pugs and Maltese and Yorkshires and borzois and bloodhounds and Bedlingtons and pointers and setters and mastiffs and Newfoundlands and St. Bernards and Great Danes and dachshunds and collies and chows and poodles and. …”

“I see,” said Molly. “You’re fond of dogs.”

“Yes,” said George. “Very fond of dogs.”

“So am I. There’s something about dogs.”

“Yes,” said George. “Of course, there’s something about cats, too.”

“Yes, isn’t there?”

===Chinese===

“我喜欢万能梗、硬毛梗、斗牛犬、京巴、希利哈姆梗、阿尔萨斯狼犬、猎狐梗、灵缇、亚伯丁梗、西高地白梗、凯恩梗、博美、西班牙猎犬、史奇派克犬、巴哥犬、马尔济斯、约克夏梗、俄罗斯猎狼犬、寻血猎犬、贝灵顿梗、指示犬、雪达犬、獒犬、纽芬兰犬、圣伯纳犬、大丹犬、腊肠犬、柯利牧羊犬、松狮犬、贵宾犬，还有……”

“明白了。”莫莉说，“你喜欢狗。”

“对。”乔治说，“特别喜欢狗。”

“我也是。狗这东西，就是有股说不出的可爱劲儿。”

“是啊。”乔治说，“当然啦，猫身上也有那么点儿说不出的可爱劲儿。”

“可不是嘛。”

===Original===

“But, still, cats aren’t dogs.”

“No, I’ve noticed that.”

There was a pause. With a sinking of the heart, for the topic was one on which he felt he could rather spread himself, George perceived that the girl regarded the subject of dogs as fully threshed out. He stood for a while licking his lips in thoughtful silence.

“So you come from the West?” said Molly.

“Yes.”

“It must be nice out there.”

===Chinese===

“不过话说回来，猫终究不是狗。”

“可不是，这一点我也留意到了。”

一阵冷场。乔治心头一沉——狗这个话题，他原觉得自己满可以大加发挥的——却发现姑娘已经认定，关于狗的讨论业已脱粒完毕、颗粒归仓。他站了一会儿，若有所思地舔着嘴唇，默然无语。

“这么说你是西部来的？”莫莉问。

“是的。”

“那儿一定很美吧。”

===Original===

“Yes.”

“Prairies and all that sort of thing.”

“Yes.”

“You aren’t a cowboy, are you?”

“No. I am an artist,” said George proudly.

“An artist? Paint pictures, you mean?”

===Chinese===

“是的。”

“大草原，诸如此类的。”

“是的。”

“你不是牛仔吧？”

“不是。我是画家。”乔治不无自豪地说。

“画家？就是画图画的那种？”

===Original===

“Yes.”

“Have you a studio?”

“Yes.”

“Where?”

“Yes. I mean, near Washington Square. In a place called the Sheridan.”

“The Sheridan? Really? Then perhaps you know Mr. Beamish?”

===Chinese===

“是的。”

“你有画室吗？”

“有。”

“在哪儿？”

“有。我是说，在华盛顿广场（Washington Square）附近。在一个叫谢里丹公寓楼（the Sheridan）的地方。”

“谢里丹？真的吗？那你兴许认识比米什先生（Mr. Beamish）？”

===Original===

“Yes. Oh, yes. Yes.”

“He’s a dear, isn’t he? I’ve known him all my life.”

“Yes.”

“It must be jolly to be an artist.”

“Yes.”

“I’d love to see some of your pictures.”

===Chinese===

“认识。哦，认识。认识。”

“他是个大好人，对不对？我打小就认识他。”

“是的。”

“当画家一定很快乐吧。”

“是的。”

“我很想看看你的画呢。”

===Original===

Warm thrills permeated George’s system.

“May I send you one of them?” he bleated.

“That’s awfully sweet of you.”

So uplifted was George Finch by this wholly unexpected development that there is no saying what heights of eloquence he might not now have reached, had he been given another ten minutes of the girl’s uninterrupted society. The fact that she was prepared to accept one of his pictures seemed to bring them very close together. He had never yet met anybody who would. For the first time since their interview had begun he felt almost at his ease.

Unfortunately, at this moment the door opened: and like a sharp attack of poison-gas Mrs. Waddington floated into the room.

“What are you doing down here, Molly?” she said.

===Chinese===

一股暖流贯穿了乔治的全身。

“我可以送您一幅吗？”他咩咩地小声问。

“你真是太好了。”

这一记全然料想不到的展开，把乔治·芬奇托上了云端。倘若再让他独享这姑娘的陪伴十分钟，天知道他的口才能蹿到什么高度。她竟愿意收下他的一幅画——这件事仿佛一下子把两人拉得极近。此前还从没有一个人肯收过。自打这次会面开始以来，他头一回觉得几乎自在了一些。

不巧，就在这时门开了：沃丁顿夫人像一阵剧毒的毒气袭来一般，飘进了房间。

“你在这儿楼下做什么，莫莉？”她说。

===Original===

She gave George one of those looks of hers, and his newly-born sangfroid immediately turned blue at the roots.

“I’ve been talking to Mr. Finch, mother. Isn’t it interesting—Mr. Finch is an artist. He paints pictures.”

Mrs. Waddington did not reply: for she had been struck suddenly dumb by a hideous discovery. Until this moment she had not examined George with any real closeness. When she had looked at him before it had been merely with the almost impersonal horror and disgust with which any hostess looks at an excrescence who at the eleventh hour horns in on one of her carefully planned dinners. His face, though revolting, had had no personal message for her.

But now it was different. Suddenly this young man’s foul features had become fraught with a dreadful significance. Subconsciously, Mrs. Waddington had been troubled ever since she had heard them by the words Molly had spoken in her bedroom: and now they shot to the surface of her mind like gruesome things from the dark depths of some sinister pool. “The sort of man I think I should rather like,” Molly had said, “would be a sort of slimmish, smallish man with nice brown eyes and rather gold-y, chestnutty hair.” She stared at George. Yes! He was slimmish. He was also smallish. His eyes, though far from nice, were brown: and his hair was undeniably of a chestnut hue.

“Who sort of chokes and turns pink and twists his fingers and makes funny noises and trips over his feet. …” Thus had the description continued, and precisely thus was the young man before her now behaving. For her gaze had had the worst effect on George Finch, and seldom in his career had he choked more throatily, turned a brighter pink, twisted his fingers into a more intricate pattern, made funnier noises and tripped more heartily over his feet than he was doing now. Mrs. Waddington was convinced. It had been no mere imaginary figure that Molly had described, but a living, breathing pestilence—and this was he.

And he was an artist! Mrs. Waddington shuddered. Of all the myriad individuals that went to make up the kaleidoscopic life of New York, she disliked artists most. They never had any money. They were dissolute and feckless. They attended dances at Webster Hall in strange costumes, and frequently played the ukulele. And this man was one of them.

===Chinese===

她照着乔治赏了一记她的招牌眼风，他那刚冒头的泰然自若顿时从根上冻成了青紫色。

“我在跟芬奇先生聊天呢，妈妈。多有意思——芬奇先生是位画家。他画图画的。”

沃丁顿夫人没有答话：一个骇人的发现叫她骤然哑了口。直到此刻之前，她还不曾凑近细看过乔治。方才她看他，不过是带着那种几乎不针对任何人的惊骇与嫌恶——随便哪位女主人，瞧见一个赘瘤在最后关头硬挤进自己精心筹划的晚宴，都是这么看的。那张脸虽然可憎，在她眼里却不曾指向任何切身的私怨。

可如今不同了。刹那之间，这个年轻人狰狞的五官有了可怕的含义。自从听见那些话，沃丁顿夫人的潜意识里就一直隐隐不安——莫莉在闺房里说过的那番话，此刻像阴森水池深处浮起的秽物，猛地蹿上了她心头。“我看我该会挺喜欢的那种男人，”莫莉是这么说的，“大概是瘦瘦小小的，长一双好看的棕色眼睛，头发有点金灿灿、又带点儿栗子色。”她死死盯住乔治。没错！他是瘦的。他也确实小。他的眼睛虽然远远算不上好看，却是棕色：而他的头发无可否认是栗色的。

“还动不动噎住、涨红了脸、手指头绞来绞去、发出些怪声音、被自己的脚绊个趔趄……”那番描述是这样接着说的，而她眼前的这个年轻人，此刻的一举一动分毫不差正是如此。原来她的目光在乔治·芬奇身上收到了最坏的效力：他这辈子还很少像现在这样，噎得这么喉头咕咕，脸涨得这么粉红，手指绞出这么繁复的花样，弄出这么滑稽的声音，脚下绊得这么结结实实。沃丁顿夫人信了。莫莉描述的绝非什么凭空想象的人物，而是一个有血有肉、会喘气的大瘟神——就是眼前这位。

而他还是个画家！沃丁顿夫人打了个寒噤。在拼成纽约万花筒般生活的芸芸众生里，她最恨的就是画家。他们从来身无分文。他们放荡又没出息。他们穿着奇装异服去韦伯斯特会堂（Webster Hall）跳舞，还动不动弹尤克里里琴。而这个人正是其中一员。

===Original===

“I suppose,” said Molly, “we’d better go upstairs?”

Mrs. Waddington came out of her trance.

“You had better go upstairs,” she said, emphasising the pronoun in a manner that would have impressed itself upon the least sensitive of men. George got it nicely.

“I—er—think, perhaps,” he mumbled, “as it is—er—getting late. …”

“You aren’t going?” said Molly concerned.

“Certainly Mr. Finch is going,” said Mrs. Waddington: and there was that in her demeanour which suggested that at any moment she might place one hand on the scruff of his neck and the other on the seat of his trousers and heave. “If Mr. Finch has appointments that call him elsewhere, we must not detain him. Good night, Mr. Finch.”

===Chinese===

“我看，”莫莉说，“咱们还是上楼去吧？”

沃丁顿夫人从出神中醒了过来。

“你——还是上楼去吧。”她说，把那个“你”字咬得极重，即便天底下最迟钝的男人也会印象至深。乔治心领神会。

“我——呃——想，也许，”他咕哝道，“时候也——呃——不早了……”

“你这就走？”莫莉关切地问。

“芬奇先生当然要走。”沃丁顿夫人说：她那副仪态里透着一股劲头，分明在暗示随时随地都可能一手揪住他的后脖颈、一手兜住裤腰后头，把他凌空抛出门去。“芬奇先生既然另有要务在身，我们不便强留。晚安，芬奇先生。”

===Original===

“Good night. Thank you for a—er—very pleasant evening.”

“It was most kay-eend of you to come,” said Mrs. Waddington.

“Do come again,” said Molly.

“Mr. Finch,” said Mrs. Waddington, “is no doubt a very ba-husy man. Please go upstairs immediately, Molly. *Good* na’eet, Mr. Finch.”

She continued to regard him in a manner hardly in keeping with the fine old traditions of American hospitality.

“Ferris,” she said, as the door closed.

===Chinese===

“晚安。谢谢你们——呃——如此愉快的一个晚上。”

“您能大驾光临，真是‘好——心’得很。”沃丁顿夫人说。

“一定要再来呀。”莫莉说。

“芬奇先生，”沃丁顿夫人说，“想必是位‘大——忙人’。这就上楼去，莫莉。‘晚——安’，芬奇先生。”

她继续那样打量着他，那副神情实在有违美国好客之道的优良古老传统。

“费里斯。”门一关上，她就叫道。

===Original===

“Madam?”

“On no pretext whatever, Ferris, is that person who has just left to be admitted to the house again.”

“Very good, madam,” said the butler.

===Chinese===

“夫人？”

“费里斯，方才离去的那一位，不论拿什么借口，一概不许再放进这所房子。”

“遵命，夫人。”男管家说。

### IV / 四

===Original===

It was a fair sunny morning next day when George Finch trotted up the steps of Number 16, Seventy-Ninth Street, East, and pressed the bell. He was wearing his dove-grey suit, and under his arm was an enormous canvas wrapped in brown paper. After much thought he had decided to present Molly with his favourite work, Hail, Jocund Spring!—a picture representing a young woman, scantily draped and obviously suffering from an advanced form of chorea, dancing with lambs in a flower-speckled field. At the moment which George had selected for her portrayal, she had—to judge from her expression—just stepped rather hard on a sharp stone. Still, she was George’s masterpiece, and he intended to present her to Molly.

The door opened. Ferris, the butler, appeared.

“All goods,” said Ferris, regarding George dispassionately, “must be delivered in the rear.”

George blinked.

“I want to see Miss Waddington.”

“Miss Waddington is not at home.”

===Chinese===

第二天是个晴朗的艳阳天，乔治·芬奇一路小跑登上东七十九街 16 号的台阶，按响了门铃。他穿着鸽子灰西装，胳膊底下夹着一大卷牛皮纸包着的画布。左思右想之后，他决意把自己最钟爱的作品《你好呀，欢乐的春天！》（Hail, Jocund Spring!）送给莫莉——画上是一位年轻女子，遮体之物寥寥，一望而知患着重症舞蹈病，正在一片缀满鲜花的田野上同羊羔共舞。在乔治替她选定的那一刻，从她的表情判断，她刚结结实实踩上一块尖石头。尽管如此，她仍是乔治的传世杰作，而他决意把她献给莫莉。

门开了。男管家费里斯出现在门口。

“凡属货物，”费里斯不动声色地打量着乔治说，“一律由后门递送。”

乔治眨了眨眼。

“我要见沃丁顿小姐。”

“沃丁顿小姐不在家。”

===Original===

“Can I see Mr. Waddington?” asked George, accepting the second-best.

“Mr. Waddington is not at home.”

George hesitated a moment before he spoke again. But love conquers all.

“Can I see Mrs. Waddington?”

“Mrs. Waddington is not at home.”

As the butler spoke, there proceeded from the upper regions of the house a commanding female voice that inquired of an unseen Sigsbee how many times the speaker had told him not to smoke in the drawing-room.

===Chinese===

“那么沃丁顿先生呢？”乔治退而求其次地问。

“沃丁顿先生不在家。”

乔治迟疑了片刻才再度开口。但爱情能征服一切。

“那我能见见沃丁顿夫人吗？”

“沃丁顿夫人不在家。”

男管家话音未落，宅子楼上便传下来一个威风凛凛的女声，正质问那位看不见的西格斯比：她到底讲过多少回，不许他在客厅里抽烟。

===Original===

“But I can hear her,” George pointed out.

The butler shrugged his shoulders with an aloof gesture, as if disclaiming all desire to go into these mysteries. His look suggested that he thought George might possibly be psychic.

“Mrs. Waddington is not at home,” he said once more.

There was a pause.

“Nice morning,” said George.

“The weather appears to be clement,” agreed Ferris.

===Chinese===

“可我听见她说话了呀。”乔治指出。

男管家漠然地耸了耸肩，仿佛无意深究这些玄机。那副神情似乎在说，他疑心乔治也许身负阴阳眼。

“沃丁顿夫人不在家。”他又说了一遍。

一阵冷场。

“早上天气真不错。”乔治说。

“天气看上去确实晴和。”费里斯附和道。

===Original===

George then tumbled backwards down the steps, and the interview concluded.

===Chinese===

随后，乔治倒退着咕噜噜滚下台阶，这次会见到此圆满结束。

## IV / 第四章

### I / 一

===Original===

“Tell me all,” said Hamilton Beamish.

George told him all. The unfortunate young man was still looking licked to a splinter. For several hours he had been wandering distractedly through the streets of New York, and now he had crawled into Hamilton Beamish’s apartment in the hope that a keener mind than his own might be able to detect in the encompassing clouds a silver lining which he himself had missed altogether.

“Let me get this clear,” said Hamilton Beamish. “You called at the house?”

===Chinese===

“把什么都讲给我听。”汉密尔顿·比米什（Hamilton Beamish）说。

乔治·芬奇（George Finch）把一切讲给了他。这位不幸的年轻人仍旧一副被打得落花流水的样子。几个钟头以来，他一直失魂落魄地在纽约街头游荡，方才才爬进汉密尔顿·比米什的公寓，指望一双比自己敏锐的头脑，能从四合的愁云里看出一道他自己压根儿没瞧见的银边。

“让我把事情理清楚。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“你登门拜访了那所宅子？”

===Original===

“Yes.”

“And the butler refused to admit you?”

“Yes.”

Hamilton Beamish regarded his stricken friend compassionately.

“My poor cloth-headed George,” he said, “you appear to have made a complete mess of things. By being impetuous you have ruined everything. Why could you not have waited and let me introduce you into this house in a normal and straightforward fashion, in my capacity of an old friend of the family? I would have started you right. As things are, you have allowed yourself to take on the semblance of an outcast.”

===Chinese===

“去了。”

“男管家不让你进？”

“是。”

汉密尔顿·比米什满怀怜悯地望着这位饱受重创的朋友。

“我可怜的木脑袋乔治，”他说，“你瞧你，把事情搅成了一锅粥。你太冒失，把一切都毁了。你怎么就不能等一等，让我以这家人的老朋友身份，用正常而光明磊落的方式把你引荐进门？我本可以给你开个好头。可如今呢，你由着自己把自己搞成了一副丧家之犬的模样。”

===Original===

“But when old Waddington invited me to dinner﻿—actually invited me to dinner.﻿ ﻿…”

“You should have kicked him in the eye and made good your escape,” said Hamilton Beamish firmly. “Surely, after all that I said to you about Sigsbee H. Waddington, you were under no illusion that his patronage would make you popular in the home? Sigsbee H. Waddington is one of those men who have only to express a liking for anybody to cause their wives to look on him as something out of the Underworld. Sigsbee H. Waddington could not bring the Prince of Wales home to dinner and get away with it. And when he drags in and lays on the mat a specimen﻿—I use the word in the kindliest spirit﻿—like you, and does so, moreover, five minutes before the start of a formal dinner-party, thus upsetting the seating arrangements and leading to black thoughts in the kitchen, can you blame his wife for not fawning on you? And on top of that you pretend to be an artist.”

“I am an artist,” said George, with a flicker of spirit. It was a subject on which he held strong views.

===Chinese===

“可老沃丁顿请我吃饭来着——实实在在请我吃饭来着……”

“你就该照他眼睛踢上一脚，趁机脱身。”汉密尔顿·比米什斩钉截铁地说。“我把西格斯比·H·沃丁顿（Sigsbee H. Waddington）的事跟你讲了一大堆，你总不至于还抱着幻想，以为得他垂青就能在他家里吃得开吧？西格斯比·H·沃丁顿就是那种人：只要对谁表示一点好感，他太太立刻把那人看成从阴曹地府里爬出来的玩意儿。西格斯比·H·沃丁顿哪怕把威尔士亲王领回家吃饭，也未必能平安收场。而他把你这么个标本——我用这个词是出于最善良的本意——拖进门厅，往门垫上一放，偏偏又赶在正式晚宴开席前五分钟，把座次安排搅得天翻地覆，还惹得厨房里人人动了歹念，这你能怪他太太不对你曲意逢迎吗？更何况你还自称是个画家。”

“我就是个画家。”乔治说，精神头闪了一下。在这个问题上他态度坚决。

===Original===

“The point is a debatable one. And, anyhow, you should have concealed it from Mrs. Waddington. A woman of her type looks on artists as blots on the social scheme. I told you she judged her fellow-creatures entirely by their balance at the bank.”

“I have plenty of money.”

“How was she to know that? You tell her you are an artist, and she naturally imagines you.﻿ ﻿…”

The telephone rang shrilly, interrupting Mr. Beamish’s flow of thought. There was an impatient frown on his face as he unhooked the receiver, but a moment later this had passed away and, when he spoke, it was in a kindly and indulgent tone.

===Chinese===

“这一点大有商榷的余地。再说，无论如何你也该瞒着沃丁顿夫人（Mrs. Waddington）。她那种女人，把画家看成社交版图上的污点。我告诉过你，她评判同类，全看对方在银行里的存款余额。”

“我有很多钱。”

“她又怎么会知道？你一说自己是个画家，她自然就把你想成了……”

电话铃尖声响起，打断了比米什先生奔涌的思绪。他摘下听筒时眉头拧着，颇不耐烦，可片刻之间这股不耐烦便烟消云散，开口说话时已是慈和纵容的声调。

===Original===

“Ah, Molly, my child!”

“Molly!” cried George.

Hamilton Beamish ignored the exclamation.

“Yes,” he said. “He is a great friend of mine.”

“Me?” said George.

Hamilton Beamish continued to accord him that complete lack of attention characteristic of the efficient telephoner when addressed while at the instrument.

===Chinese===

“啊，莫莉，我的孩子！”

“莫莉！”乔治叫道。

汉密尔顿·比米什对这声惊呼置若罔闻。

“是的。”他说。“他是我的挚友。”

“说我呢？”乔治说。

汉密尔顿·比米什依旧对他全然不予理会——通话当中受人搭话便一概不睬，这正是讲效率的通话者的本色。

===Original===

“Yes, he has been telling me about it. He’s here now.”

“Does she want me to speak to her?” quavered George.

“Certainly, I’ll come at once.”

Hamilton Beamish replaced the receiver, and stood for awhile in thought.

“What did she say?” asked George, deeply moved.

“This is interesting,” said Hamilton Beamish.

===Chinese===

“是啊，他刚把事情跟我讲了。他这会儿就在这儿。”

“她要跟我说话吗？”乔治声音发颤。

“那当然，我马上就来。”

汉密尔顿·比米什放回听筒，站了一会儿，陷入沉思。

“她说什么了？”乔治问，感动得不行。

“这事有意思。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。

===Original===

“What did she say?”

“This causes me to revise my views to some extent.”

“What did she say?”

“And yet I might have foreseen it.”

“What did she say?”

===Chinese===

“她说什么了？”

“这使我在一定程度上修正了自己的看法。”

“她说什么了？”

“其实我本该料到的。”

“她说什么了？”

===Original===

Hamilton Beamish rubbed his chin meditatively.

“The mind of a girl works oddly.”

“What did she say?”

“That was Molly Waddington,” said Hamilton Beamish.

===Chinese===

汉密尔顿·比米什若有所思地摩挲着下巴。

“姑娘的心思转起来就是邪性。”

“她说什么了？”

“刚才是莫莉·沃丁顿（Molly Waddington）。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。

===Original===

“What did she say?”

“I am by no means sure,” said Mr. Beamish regarding George owlishly through his spectacles, “that, after all, everything has not happened for the best. I omitted to take into my calculations the fact that what has occurred would naturally give you in the eyes of a warmhearted girl, surrounded normally by men with incomes in six figures, a certain romantic glamour. Any girl with nice instincts must inevitably be attracted to a penniless artist whom her mother forbids her to see.”

“What did she say?”

“She asked me if you were a friend of mine.”

===Chinese===

“她说什么了？”

“我丝毫不敢肯定，”比米什先生隔着眼镜、一副猫头鹰似的神情望着乔治，说道，“说到底，事情未尝不是朝着最好的方向去的。我推算时漏算了一条：出了这档子事，在一颗热心肠的姑娘眼里——何况她平日围着的全是年入六位数的男人——你自然平添了几分浪漫的光环。凡是天性纯良的姑娘，都必然会被一个身无分文、她母亲又禁止她来往的画家迷住。”

“她说什么了？”

“她问我，你是不是我的朋友。”

===Original===

“And then what did she say?”

“She told me that her stepmother had forbidden you the house and that she had been expressly ordered never to see you again.”

“And what did she say after that?”

“She asked me to come up to the house and have a talk.”

“About me?”

“So I imagine.”

===Chinese===

“然后她说了什么？”

“她告诉我，她继母把你拒之门外，还明令她从此不许再见你。”

“那之后她说了什么？”

“她请我到府上去谈一谈。”

“关于我？”

“我想是。”

===Original===

“You’re going?”

“At once.”

“Hamilton,” said George in a quivering voice, “Hamilton, old man, pitch it strong!”

“You mean, speak enthusiastically on your behalf?”

“I mean just that. How well you put these things, Hamilton!”

Hamilton Beamish took up his hat and placed it on his head.

===Chinese===

“你要去？”

“立刻就去。”

“汉密尔顿，”乔治声音发颤地说，“汉密尔顿，老伙计，替我使劲儿猛吹！”

“你是说，热情洋溢地替你美言几句？”

“我就是这个意思。这些话说得真好，汉密尔顿！”

汉密尔顿·比米什拿起帽子，扣到头上。

===Original===

“It is strange,” he said meditatively, “that I should be assisting you in this matter.”

“It’s your good heart,” said George. “You have a heart of gold.”

“You have fallen in love at first sight, and my views on love at first sight are well known.”

“They’re all wrong.”

“My views are never wrong.”

===Chinese===

“真是奇怪，”他若有所思地说，“这件事上出力的居然是我。”

“这是你心地好。”乔治说。“你有一颗金子般的心。”

“你一见钟情了，而我对一见钟情的看法是尽人皆知的。”

“那些看法全不对。”

“我的看法从来不会错。”

===Original===

“I don’t mean wrong exactly,” said George with sycophantic haste. “I mean that in certain cases love at first sight is the only thing.”

“Love should be a reasoned emotion.”

“Not if you suddenly see a girl like Molly Waddington.”

“When I marry,” said Hamilton Beamish, “it will be the result of a carefully calculated process of thought. I shall first decide after cool reflection that I have reached the age at which it is best for me to marry. I shall then run over the list of my female friends till I have selected one whose mind and tastes are in harmony with mine. I shall then.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“我不是说‘不对’，”乔治谄媚地急忙改口，“我是说，在某些情形下，一见钟情是唯一正经的感情。”

“爱情应当是一种经过理性推敲的感情。”

“可要是你猛地看见莫莉·沃丁顿那样的姑娘，就不是这么回事了。”

“等我结婚，”汉密尔顿·比米什说，“那必定是一番缜密推算的思维过程的产物。我要先冷静思考，确定自己已到最适合结婚的年纪。然后我要把女性朋友的名单通盘过一遍，选出一位心智与趣味同我契合的。然后我就……”

===Original===

“Aren’t you going to change?” said George.

“Change what?”

“Your clothes. If you are going to see Her.﻿ ﻿…”

“I shall then,” proceeded Hamilton Beamish, “watch her carefully for a considerable length of time in order to assure myself that I have not allowed passion to blind me to any faults in her disposition. After that.﻿ ﻿…”

“You can’t possibly call on Miss Waddington in those trousers,” said George. “And your shirt does not match your socks. You must.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“你不换衣服了吗？”乔治说。

“换什么？”

“你的衣服。你既然要去见‘她’……”

“然后，”汉密尔顿·比米什接着说，“我要经过相当长一段时间的仔细观察，确信自己没有因为激情盲目而看漏她性情上的任何缺点。再往后……”

“你总不能穿这条裤子去拜访沃丁顿小姐吧，”乔治说。“衬衫跟袜子也不相配。你一定得……”

===Original===

“After that, provided in the interval I have not observed any more suitable candidate for my affections, I shall go to her and in a few simple words ask her to be my wife. I shall point out that my income is sufficient for two, that my morals are above reproach, that.﻿ ﻿…”

“Haven’t you a really nice suit that’s been properly pressed and brushed and a rather newer pair of shoes and a less floppy sort of hat and.﻿ ﻿…”

“… that my disposition is amiable and my habits regular. And she and I will settle down to the Marriage Sane.”

“How about your cuffs?” said George.

===Chinese===

“再往后，只要这期间我没有物色到更合心意的候选人，我就走到她跟前，用几句朴素的话请她嫁给我。我要指出，我的收入足够两个人开销，我的品行无可指摘，还有……”

“你就没有一套熨得平平整整、刷得干干净净的好西装，一双新一点儿的皮鞋，一顶不那么软塌塌的帽子，还有……”

“……还有，我性情和顺，起居有常。然后她和我便安顿下来，过起‘理性婚姻’（the Marriage Sane）的日子。”

“你的袖口呢？”乔治说。

===Original===

“What about my cuffs?”

“Are you really going to see Miss Waddington in frayed cuffs?”

“I am.”

George had nothing more to say. It was sacrilege, but there seemed no way of preventing it.

===Chinese===

“我的袖口怎么了？”

“你当真要顶着一副磨破的袖口去见沃丁顿小姐？”

“是的。”

乔治无话可说了。这简直是亵渎，可看上去毫无办法阻止。

### II / 二

===Original===

As Hamilton Beamish, some quarter of an hour later, climbed in a series of efficient movements up the stairs of the green omnibus which was waiting in Washington Square, the summer afternoon had reached its best and sweetest. A red-blooded, one hundred percent American sun still shone warmly down from a sky of gleaming azure, but there had stolen into the air a hint of the cool of evening. It was the sort of day when Tin Pan Alley lyric-writers suddenly realise that “love” rhymes with “skies above,” and rush off, snorting, to turn out the song-hit of a lifetime. Sentimentality was abroad: and gradually, without his being aware of it, its seeds began to plant themselves in the stony and uncompromising soil of Hamilton Beamish’s bosom.

Yes, little by little, as the omnibus rolled on up the Avenue, there began to burgeon in Hamilton Beamish a mood of gentle tolerance for his species. He no longer blamed so wholeheartedly the disposition of his fellow-men to entertain towards the opposite sex on short acquaintance a warmth of emotion which could be scientifically justified only by a long and intimate knowledge of character. For the first time he began to debate within himself whether there was not something to be said for a man who was, like George Finch, plunged headlong into love with a girl to whom he had never even spoken.

And it was at this precise moment﻿—just, dramatically enough, when the bus was passing Twenty-Ninth Street with its pretty and suggestive glimpse of the Little Church Round the Corner﻿—that he noticed for the first time the girl in the seat across the way.

===Chinese===

一刻钟之后，汉密尔顿·比米什以一连串干脆利落的动作，顺着梯级爬上了停在华盛顿广场的绿色公共汽车，此时夏日午后正当它最明媚、最甜美的时分。一轮热血沸腾、百分之百美利坚的太阳，照旧从亮闪闪的蔚蓝天空里暖融融地照下来，可空气里已经悄然潜入一丝傍晚的凉意。这样的日子，廷潘胡同（Tin Pan Alley）的歌词作者会蓦然醒悟“爱情”（love）与“头上晴空”（skies above）押韵，呼哧呼哧地扑回去，赶制出一辈子的金曲。感伤在空气中四处游荡：它渐渐神不知鬼不觉，在汉密尔顿·比米什胸中那片寸草不生、寸土不让的硬土里播下了种子。

是的，公共汽车沿大道滚滚向前，一点点地，一种对同类的温和宽容之情开始在汉密尔顿·比米什心中抽芽。他的同胞们素来有这样一种癖性：相识不久，便对异性满怀一腔热忱，而按科学的标准，这份热忱唯有靠长年累月、知根知底的考察才站得住脚——对这种癖性，他不再那么义正辞严地指责了。生平头一遭，他开始在心底掂量：像乔治·芬奇这样，跟一位连话都不曾说过半句的姑娘一头栽进爱河，是否也有几分可取之处。

就在这当口——说巧也巧，戏剧性十足，公共汽车正驶过第二十九街，街旁一瞥之下，“转角小教堂”（Little Church Round the Corner）俏丽而引人遐思——他头一回注意到了对面座位上的姑娘。

===Original===

She was a girl of chic and élan. One may go still further﻿—a girl of espièglerie and *je ne sais quoi*. She was dressed, as Hamilton Beamish’s experienced eye noted in one swift glance, in a delightful two-piece suit composed of a smart coat in fine quality repp, lined throughout with crêpe-de-chine, over a dainty long-sleeved frock of figured Marocain prettily pleated at the sides and finished at the neck with a small collar and kilted frill: a dress which, as every schoolboy knows, can be had in beige, grey, mid-grey, opal, snuff, powder, burnt wood, puce, brown, bottle, almond, navy, black and dark Saxe. Her colour was dark Saxe.

Another glance enabled Hamilton Beamish to take in her hat. It was, he perceived, a becoming hat in Yedda Visca straw, trimmed and bound with silk petersham ribbon, individual without being conspicuous, artistic in line and exquisite in style: and from beneath it there strayed a single curl of about the colour of a good Pekingese dog. Judging the rest of her hair by the light of this curl, Hamilton Beamish deduced that, when combing and dressing it, she just moistened the brush with a little scalpoline, thus producing a gleamy mass, sparkling with life and possessing that incomparable softness, freshness and luxuriance, at the same time toning each single hair to grow thick, long and strong. No doubt she had read advertisements of the tonic in the papers and now, having bought a bottle, was seeing how healthy and youthful her hair appeared after this delightful, refreshing dressing.

Her shoes were of black patent-leather, her stockings of steel-grey. She had that schoolgirl complexion and the skin you love to touch.

===Chinese===

她是个又时髦又潇洒的姑娘。还可以更进一步——她带着几分法兰西式的娇俏顽皮（espièglerie），外加一点不可言传的妙韵（je ne sais quoi）。汉密尔顿·比米什目光老练，一瞥之间便看得分明：她穿一套可心的两件套，上身是上等棱纹绸（repp）裁成的俏丽短外套，通身衬着双绉（crêpe-de-chine）里子，底下是一条雅致的长袖提花马罗坎绉（Marocain）连衣裙，两侧打着好看的细褶，领口收一个小圆领，缀一圈褶裥荷叶边；至于这款衣裙，小学生都知道，备有米色、灰、中灰、蛋白石、鼻烟、扑粉、焦木、紫褐、棕、瓶绿、杏仁、藏青、黑以及深萨克森蓝诸色。她这一件，便是深萨克森蓝。

再一瞥，汉密尔顿·比米什便把她的帽子尽收眼底。他看出那是一顶很衬人的帽子，耶达维斯卡草（Yedda Visca）所编，镶的、箍的全是丝质罗缎带（petersham），别致而不招摇，线条见匠心，式样极精巧；帽檐底下溜出一绺鬈发，颜色约莫相当于一只上等京巴犬。就着这绺鬈发的光泽推断其余的头发，汉密尔顿·比米什得出结论：她梳头时必定只用刷子蘸了少许斯卡波琳（scalpoline）生发水，因而梳出一头光华流转的秀发，莹莹有生气，兼有那种无可比拟的柔软、鲜润与丰茂，同时又调理着每一根头发，长得又密、又长、又韧。毫无疑问，她在报纸上读过这种生发水的广告，如今买了一瓶，正亲眼验证：这么怡神惬意地一梳妆，头发瞧上去何等健康、何等青春。

她脚上是黑漆皮鞋，腿上是铁灰丝袜。一副女学生似的娇嫩气色，外加那种让人爱不释手的肌肤。

===Original===

All these things the trained eye of Hamilton Beamish noted, swivelling rapidly sideways and swivelling rapidly back again. But it was her face that he noted most particularly. It was just the sort of face which, if he had not had his policy of Sane Love all carefully mapped out, would have exercised the most disturbing effect on his emotions. Even as it was, this strong, competent man could not check, as he alighted from the bus at Seventy-Ninth Street, a twinge of that wistful melancholy which men feel when they are letting a good thing get away from them.

Sad, reflected Hamilton Beamish, as he stood upon the steps of Number 16 and prepared to ring the bell, that he would never see this girl again. Naturally, a man of his stamp was not in love at first sight, but nevertheless he did not conceal it from himself that nothing would suit him better than to make her acquaintance and, after careful study of her character and disposition, possibly discover in a year or two that it was she whom Nature had intended for his mate.

It was at this point in his reflections that he perceived her standing at his elbow.

There are moments when even the coolest-headed efficiency expert finds it hard to maintain his poise. Hamilton Beamish was definitely taken aback: and, had he been a lesser man, one would have said that he became for an instant definitely pop-eyed. Apart from the fact that he had been thinking of her and thinking of her tenderly, there was the embarrassment of standing side by side with a strange girl on a doorstep. In such a crisis it is very difficult for a man to know precisely how to behave. Should he endeavour to create the illusion that he is not aware of her presence? Or should he make some chatty remark? And, if a chatty remark, what chatty remark?

===Chinese===

这一切，汉密尔顿·比米什那双训练有素的眼睛一一记下，眼珠飞快地横扫过去，又飞快地扫回来。但他看得最仔细的，还是那张脸。恰恰是这样一张脸，倘若他不是早把“理性之爱”的方针擘画得周周全全，势必搅得他心旌摇荡。即便如此，这位坚强能干的汉子在七十九街下车时，仍免不了心头一酸——男人眼看一件好东西从指缝里溜走时，胸中泛起的就是那种怅惘的愁绪。

汉密尔顿·比米什站在 16 号门前的台阶上，正要按铃，心里怅怅地想：这姑娘怕是再也见不着了。自然，他这等人物是不会一见钟情的，然而他也不瞒自己：倘若能结识这位姑娘，再细细研究她的品格与性情，兴许一两年之后会发现，大自然原本就打算让她配给自己——天下再没有比这更称心的事了。

正思忖到此处，他发觉她就站在自己的手肘旁边。

便是最冷静的效率专家，也有难以保持镇定的时刻。汉密尔顿·比米什着实吃了一惊：换了个差些的人物，人们准会说他那一瞬间眼珠子都瞪圆了。且不说他方才还在想她、温柔地想她，单是跟一位陌生姑娘并肩站在人家门前的台阶上，就够尴尬的了。这样的危急关头，男人实在很难拿准该怎么做。是该努力制造一种全然不觉她在场的假象呢，还是该搭一句闲话？倘若搭闲话，又搭哪一句闲话？

===Original===

Hamilton Beamish was still grappling with this problem, when the girl solved it for him. Suddenly screwing up a face which looked even more attractive at point-blank range than it had appeared in profile, she uttered the exclamation “Oo!”

“Oo!” said this girl.

For a moment, all Hamilton Beamish felt was that almost ecstatic relief which comes over the man of sensibility when he finds that a pretty girl has an attractive voice. Too many times in his career he had admired girls from afar, only to discover, when they spoke, that they had voices like peacocks calling up the rain. The next instant, however, he had recognised that his companion was suffering, and his heart was filled with a blend of compassion and zeal. Her pain aroused simultaneously the pity of the man and the efficiency of the efficiency expert.

“You have something in your eye?” he said.

“A bit of dust or something.”

===Chinese===

汉密尔顿·比米什还在跟这道难题搏斗，姑娘已经替他解了围。她猛地一皱脸——这张脸近在咫尺看去，比侧影瞧着还要动人——发出一声惊呼：“哎哟！”

“哎哟！”这姑娘说。

这一瞬间，汉密尔顿·比米什心里只有那种近乎狂喜的宽慰：多情之士发觉漂亮姑娘的嗓音也可人时，胸中涌起的正是这种宽慰。他一生里见过太多这样的情形——远远仰慕一位姑娘，一等她开口，才发现人家叫起来跟孔雀唤雨一个腔调。然而下一瞬他便觉察到同伴正在受苦，他的心顿时让怜悯与热忱灌满。她的痛楚同时唤起了男人的恻隐和效率专家的效率。

“你眼睛里进了东西？”他说。

“一粒灰尘还是什么。”

===Original===

“Permit me,” said Hamilton Beamish.

One of the most difficult tasks that can confront the ordinary man is the extraction of foreign bodies from the eye of a perfect stranger of the opposite sex. But Hamilton Beamish was not an ordinary man. Barely ten seconds later, he was replacing his handkerchief in his pocket and the girl was blinking at him gratefully.

“Thank you ever so much,” she said.

“Not at all,” said Hamilton Beamish.

“A doctor couldn’t have done it more neatly.”

“It’s just a knack.”

===Chinese===

“容我效劳。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。

普通人所能面临的最棘手的差事之一，便是从一位素昧平生的异性眼里取出异物。可汉密尔顿·比米什不是普通人。不到十秒钟，他已把手帕收回口袋，姑娘正眨着眼睛，感激地望着他。

“太谢谢您了。”她说。

“不必客气。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。

“大夫也不会弄得比这更利落。”

“不过是个小窍门。”

===Original===

“Why is it,” asked the girl, “that, when you get a speck of dust in your eye the size of a pinpoint, it seems as big as all outdoors?”

Hamilton Beamish could answer that. The subject was one he had studied.

“The conjunctiva, a layer of mucous membrane which lines the back of the eyelids and is reflected on the front of the globe, this reflection forming the fornix, is extremely sensitive. This is especially so at the point where the tarsal plates of fibrous tissue are attached to the orbital margin by the superior and inferior palpebral ligaments.”

“I see,” said the girl.

There was a pause.

===Chinese===

“为什么呀，”姑娘问，“眼睛里进了一粒针尖大的灰，瞧着却有旷野那么大？”

这个问题汉密尔顿·比米什答得上来。这个科目他研究过。

“结膜是衬于眼睑内面并反折到眼球前面的一层黏膜，这一反折形成穹窿，感觉极为灵敏；在纤维组织构成的睑板借上、下睑韧带附着于眶缘之处，尤其如此。”

“明白了。”姑娘说。

随后是一阵沉默。

===Original===

“Are you calling on Mrs. Waddington?” asked the girl.

“On Miss Waddington.”

“I’ve never met her.”

“You don’t know the whole family, then?”

“No. Only Mrs. Waddington. Would you mind ringing the bell?”

Hamilton Beamish pressed the button.

===Chinese===

“您是来拜访沃丁顿夫人的吗？”姑娘问。

“拜访沃丁顿小姐。”

“我从没见过她。”

“那么这一家您都不认识？”

“不认识。只认得沃丁顿夫人。劳驾您按一下铃好吗？”

汉密尔顿·比米什按了按钮。

===Original===

“I saw you on the omnibus,” he said.

“Did you?”

“Yes. I was sitting in the next seat.”

“How odd!”

“It’s a lovely day, isn’t it?”

“Beautiful.”

===Chinese===

“我在公共汽车上看见您了。”他说。

“是吗？”

“是啊。我就坐在隔壁座位。”

“多巧啊！”

“天气真好，是不是？”

“好极了。”

===Original===

“The sun.”

“Yes.”

“The sky.”

“Yes.”

“I like the summer.”

“So do I.”

===Chinese===

“太阳。”

“嗯。”

“天空。”

“嗯。”

“我喜欢夏天。”

“我也喜欢。”

===Original===

“When it’s not too hot.”

“Yes.”

“Though, as a matter of fact,” said Hamilton Beamish, “I always say that what one objects to is not the heat but the humidity.”

Which simply goes to prove that even efficiency experts, when they fall in love at first sight, can babble like any man of inferior intellect in the same circumstances. Strange and violent emotions were racking Hamilton Beamish’s bosom: and, casting away the principles of a lifetime, he recognised without a trace of shame that love had come to him at last﻿—not creeping scientifically into his soul, as he had supposed it would, but elbowing its way in with the Berserk rush of a commuter charging into the five-fifteen. Yes, he was in love. And it is proof of the completeness with which passion had blunted his intellectual faculties that he was under the impression that in the recent exchange of remarks he had been talking rather well.

===Chinese===

“只要不太热。”

“嗯。”

“不过说实在的，”汉密尔顿·比米什说，“我一向有句话：让人受不了的其实不是热，而是潮气。”

这恰恰证明，就连效率专家，一旦一见钟情，也会跟同样处境下任何智力低劣的男人一样絮絮叨叨。奇异而猛烈的感情正摧折着汉密尔顿·比米什的胸襟：他把毕生信条抛诸脑后，毫不害臊地承认，爱情终于临到他头上了——不是像他设想的那样，科学地、蹑手蹑脚地潜入灵魂，而是像赶五点一刻班车的通勤客那样，横冲直撞、挥肘开路地挤了进来。是的，他恋爱了。而激情究竟把他的智力磨钝到了何等地步，有一个再好不过的证明：他还自以为，方才那番对答，他说得相当漂亮。

===Original===

The door opened. Ferris appeared. He looked at the girl, not with the cold distaste which he had exhibited earlier in the day towards George Finch, but with a certain paternal affection. Ferris measured forty-six round the waist, but Beauty still had its appeal for him.

“Mrs. Waddington desired me to say, miss,” he said, “that an appointment of an urgent nature has called her elsewhere, rendering it impossible for her to see you this afternoon.”

“She might have phoned me,” the girl complained.

Ferris allowed one eyebrow to flicker momentarily, conveying the idea, that, while he sympathised, a spirit of loyalty forbade him to join in criticism of his employer.

“Mrs. Waddington wished to know if it would be convenient to you, miss, if she called upon you tomorrow at five o’clock?”

===Chinese===

门开了。费里斯（Ferris）出现在门口。他望着姑娘，目光里没有今天早些时候对乔治·芬奇的那种冷冰冰的嫌恶，倒有几分父亲似的慈爱。费里斯的腰围足有四十六英寸，可美对他仍然有吸引力。

“沃丁顿夫人嘱我转告，小姐，”他说，“一件要紧的约事把她请往别处，今天下午无法见您了。”

“她也该给我打个电话呀。”姑娘抱怨道。

费里斯让一条眉毛霎时动了动，意思是说：他虽深表同情，忠义之心却不许他附和对雇主的非议。

“沃丁顿夫人想知道，小姐，她明天五点钟登门拜访，您方便不方便？”

===Original===

“All right.”

“Thank you, miss. Miss Waddington is expecting you, sir.”

Hamilton Beamish continued to stare after the girl, who, with a friendly nod in his direction, had begun to walk light-heartedly out of his life along the street.

“Who is that young lady, Ferris?” he asked.

“I could not say, sir.”

“Why couldn’t you? You seemed to know her just now.”

===Chinese===

“行啊。”

“谢谢您，小姐。沃丁顿小姐正等着您呢，先生。”

汉密尔顿·比米什还在目送那位姑娘。她朝他友好地点了点头，已经轻快地沿着街道，走出他的人生去了。

“那位小姐是谁，费里斯？”他问。

“小的说不上来，先生。”

“怎么说不上来？方才你好像认识她似的。”

===Original===

“No, sir. I had never seen the young lady before. Mrs. Waddington, however, had mentioned that she would be calling at this hour and instructed me to give the message which I delivered.”

“Didn’t Mrs. Waddington say who was calling?”

“Yes, sir. The young lady.”

“Ass!” said Hamilton Beamish. But even he was not strong man enough to say it aloud. “I mean, didn’t she tell you the young lady’s name?”

===Chinese===

“不是的，先生。小的从前从没见过这位小姐。不过沃丁顿夫人提过，她这个时辰会来拜访，并且吩咐小的转达方才那番话。”

“沃丁顿夫人没说来的是谁吗？”

“说了，先生。这位小姐。”

“蠢货！”汉密尔顿·比米什骂道。可就连他，也还没硬气到真把它骂出声的地步。“我是说，她没告诉你这位小姐的姓名吗？”

===Original===

“No, sir. If you will step this way, sir, I will conduct you to Miss Waddington, who is in the library.”

“It seems funny that Mrs. Waddington did not tell you the young lady’s name,” brooded Hamilton Beamish.

“Very humorous, sir,” agreed the butler indulgently.

===Chinese===

“没有，先生。请您这边走，先生，小的领您去见沃丁顿小姐，她在书房里。”

“沃丁顿夫人竟没告诉你这位小姐的姓名，这可有点蹊跷。”汉密尔顿·比米什沉吟道。

“确实滑稽，先生。”男管家宽厚地随声附和。

### III / 三

===Original===

“Oh, Jimmy, it was sweet of you to come,” said Molly.

Hamilton Beamish patted her hand absently. He was too preoccupied to notice the hateful name by which she had addressed him.

“I have had a wonderful experience,” he said.

“So have I. I think I’m in love.”

“I have given the matter as close attention as has been possible, in the limited time at my disposal,” said Hamilton Beamish, “and I have reached the conclusion that I, too, am in love.”

===Chinese===

“哦，吉米，你能来真是太好了。”莫莉说。

汉密尔顿·比米什心不在焉地拍了拍她的手。他心事太重，竟没有留意她用来称呼他的那个可恶的名字。

“我方才经历了一件奇妙的事。”他说。

“我也是。我想我恋爱了。”

“在我能支配的有限时间里，我已就此事作了力所能及的周详考察，”汉密尔顿·比米什说，“并得出结论：我也恋爱了。”

===Original===

“I think I am in love with your friend, George Finch.”

“I am in love with﻿ ﻿…” Hamilton Beamish paused. “I don’t know her name. She is a most charming girl. I met her coming up here on the bus, and we talked for awhile on the front doorsteps. I took something out of her eye.”

Molly stared incredulously.

“You have fallen in love with a girl and you don’t know who she is? But I thought you always said that love was a reasoned emotion and all that.”

“One’s views alter,” said Hamilton Beamish. “A man’s intellectual perceptions do not stand still. One develops.”

“I was never so surprised in my life.”

===Chinese===

“我想我爱上的是你的朋友乔治·芬奇。”

“我爱上的……”汉密尔顿·比米什顿住了。“我还不知道她的名字。她是一位极可爱的姑娘。我来这儿的公共汽车上遇见她，还在大门台阶上聊了一会儿。我从她眼睛里取出了个东西。”

莫莉难以置信地瞪着他。

“你爱上了一位姑娘，却不知道她是谁？我还当你一向说，爱情是种经过理性推敲的感情来着。”

“人的看法会变的。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“人的悟性并非一潭死水。人是要进步的。”

“我这辈子还没这么吃惊过。”

===Original===

“It came as a complete surprise to me,” said Hamilton Beamish. “It is excessively aggravating that I do not know her name nor where she lives nor anything about her except that she appears to be a friend﻿—or at least an acquaintance﻿—of your stepmother.”

“Oh, she knows mother, does she?”

“Apparently. She was calling here by appointment.”

“All sorts of weird people call on mother. She is honorary secretary to about a hundred societies.”

“This girl was of medium height, with an extremely graceful figure and bright brown hair. She wore a two-piece suit with a coat of fine quality repp over a long-sleeved frock of figured Marocain pleated at the sides and finished at the neck with a small collar and kilted frill. Her hat was of Yedda Visca straw, trimmed and bound with silk petersham ribbon.
