She had patent-leather shoes, silk stockings, and eyes of tender grey like the mists of sunrise floating over some magic pool of Fairyland. Does the description suggest anybody to you?”

===Chinese===

“对我来说这也是全然出乎意料，”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“恼火的是，我不知道她的名字，不知道她住在哪里，关于她一概不知，只知道她似乎是你继母的朋友——至少是个相识。”

“哦，她认识妈妈，是吗？”

“看来是。她是约好了来拜访的。”

“什么奇奇怪怪的人都来拜访妈妈。她当着大约一百个协会的名誉秘书呢。”

“这姑娘中等身材，体态极其婀娜，一头明亮的棕发。她穿一套两件套，上身上等棱纹绸短外套，底下长袖提花马罗坎绉连衣裙，两侧细褶，领口是小圆领，缀一圈褶裥荷叶边。帽子是耶达维斯卡草编的，镶的、箍的都是丝质罗缎带。脚上漆皮鞋，腿上丝袜，还有一双温柔的灰眼睛，像初升朝霞的雾霭，浮在仙境的魔池上。这番描摹，可让你想起什么人来？”

===Original===

“No, I don’t think so﻿—She sounds nice.”

“She is nice. I gazed into those eyes only for a moment, but I shall never forget them. They were deeper than the depth of waters stilled at even.”

“I could ask mother who she is.”

“I should be greatly obliged if you would do so,” said Hamilton Beamish. “Mention that it is someone upon whom she is to call at five o’clock tomorrow, and telephone me the name and address. Oh, to seize her and hold her close to me and kiss her again and again and again! And now, child, tell me of yourself. I think you mentioned that you also were in love.”

===Chinese===

“没有，想不起来——听着倒是个好人儿。”

“她确实好。那双眼睛我只凝视了片刻，却永远不会忘记。它们比暮色里静止的深水还要深。”

“我可以问问妈妈她是谁。”

“若蒙如此，感激不尽。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“就说是她明天五点钟要去拜访的什么人，然后把姓名和地址打电话告诉我。啊，真想一把将她搂住，紧紧贴在怀前，吻她，一遍又一遍，再一遍！好了，孩子，现在讲讲你自己的事吧。我记得你说过，你也恋爱了。”

===Original===

“Yes. With George Finch.”

“A capital fellow.”

“He’s a lambkin,” emended Molly warmly.

“A lambkin, if you prefer it.”

“And I asked you to come here today to tell me what I ought to do. You see, mother doesn’t like him.”

“So I gathered.”

===Chinese===

“是啊。爱上了乔治·芬奇。”

“一个顶好的人。”

“他是个小乖乖羊。”莫莉热情地修正道。

“小乖乖羊就小乖乖羊，随你喜欢。”

“我今天请你来，就是要你告诉我该怎么办。你是知道的，妈妈不喜欢他。”

“我料想到了。”

===Original===

“She has forbidden him the house.”

“Yes.”

“I suppose it’s because he has no money.”

Hamilton Beamish was on the point of mentioning that George had an almost indecent amount of money, but he checked himself. Who was he that he should destroy a young girl’s dreams? It was as a romantic and penniless artist that George Finch had won this girl’s heart. It would be cruel to reveal the fact that he was rich and the worst artist in New York.

===Chinese===

“她不许他进门了。”

“是啊。”

“我想，是因为他没有钱吧。”

汉密尔顿·比米什话已到嘴边，险些说出乔治的家财多得近乎有伤风化，又硬生生咽了回去。他是何等人，岂能粉碎一位少女的梦？乔治·芬奇是以一个浪漫的、身无分文的画家的身份，赢得这姑娘的心的。要揭穿他其实腰缠万贯、却是全纽约最糟的画家，那就太残忍了。

===Original===

“Your stepmother,” he agreed, “is apt to see eye to eye with Bradstreet in her estimation of her fellows.”

“I don’t care if he hasn’t any money,” said Molly. “You know that, when I marry, I get that pearl necklace that father bought for mother. It’s being held in trust for me. I can sell it and get thousands of dollars, so that we shall be as right as anything.”

“Quite.”

“But, of course, I don’t want to make a runaway marriage if I can help it. I want to be married with bridesmaids and cake and presents and photographs in the rotogravure section and everything.”

“Naturally.”

“So the point is, mother must learn to love George. Now listen, Jimmy dear. Mother will be going to see her palmist, very soon﻿—she’s always going to palmists, you know.”

===Chinese===

“你继母看人，”他表示赞同，“向来同布拉德斯特里特征信所（Bradstreet）见地一致。”

“我才不在乎他有没有钱。”莫莉说。“你知道的，等我结婚，我就能得到那条珍珠项链，就是爸爸买给妈妈的那条。那项链用信托替我保管着。我把它卖了能得几千美元，到那时我们就万无一失了。”

“说得是。”

“不过当然了，能不私奔还是不想私奔。我要办一场婚礼，有伴娘，有蛋糕，有礼物，有登上报纸影印画页（rotogravure）的照片，样样都要。”

“自然。”

“所以关键是，妈妈必须学会爱乔治。好，听着，吉米亲爱的。妈妈很快就要去看她的手相师了——你知道，她一向在手相师那里跑。”

===Original===

Hamilton Beamish nodded. He had not been aware of this trait in Mrs. Waddington’s character, but he could believe anything of her. Now that he came to consider the matter, he recognised that Mrs. Waddington was precisely the sort of woman who, in the intervals of sitting in the salons of beauty specialists with green mud on her face, would go to palmists.

“And what you must do is to go to this palmist before mother gets there and bribe her to say that my only happiness is bound up with a brown-haired artist whose name begins with a *G*.”

“I scarcely think that even a palmist would make Mrs. Waddington believe that.”

“She believes everything Madame Eulalie sees in the crystal.”

“But hardly that.”

“No, perhaps you’re right. Well, then, you must get Madame Eulalie at least to steer mother off Lord Hunstanton. Last night, she told me in so many words that she wanted me to marry him. He’s always here, and it’s awful.”

===Chinese===

汉密尔顿·比米什点点头。沃丁顿夫人的性格里竟有这一笔，他先前倒不知道，不过她的什么事他都信。如今细细一想，他认定沃丁顿夫人恰是这样一种女人：每隔一阵，总要敷着一脸绿泥面膜坐在美容专家的厅堂里，间隙里便会去会会手相师。

“你要做的，就是赶在妈妈之前先到这位手相师那里，把她买通，叫她说：我唯一的幸福，同一位棕发画家的名字拴在一起，此人名字的头一个字母是G。”

“只怕手相师就算真这么说，也未必能让沃丁顿夫人相信。”

“尤拉莉夫人（Madame Eulalie）的水晶球里看见什么，她信什么。”

“可这一条她未必信。”

“嗯，也许你说得对。那么这样，你至少得让尤拉莉夫人把母亲的心思从亨斯坦顿勋爵（Lord Hunstanton）身上引开。昨天夜里，她明明白白地告诉我，要我嫁给他。他老是在这儿转，烦死了。”

===Original===

“I could do that, of course.”

“And you will?”

“Certainly.”

“You’re a darling. I should think she would do it for ten dollars.”

“Twenty at the outside.”

“Then that’s settled. I knew I could rely on you. By the way, will you tell George something quite casually?”

===Chinese===

“这个我当然办得到。”

“那你办了？”

“自然。”

“你真是个宝贝。我看她十美元就肯办了。”

“顶多二十美元。”

“那就一言为定。我就知道靠得住的是你。对了，你能顺口给乔治捎句话吗？”

===Original===

“Anything you wish.”

“Just mention to him that, if he happens to be strolling in Central Park tomorrow afternoon near the Zoo, we might run into each other.”

“Very well.”

“And now,” said Molly, “tell me all about George and how you came to know one another and what you thought of him when you first saw him and what he likes for breakfast and what he talks about and what he said about me.”

===Chinese===

“但凭吩咐。”

“就跟他提一句：明天下午，他若碰巧在中央公园动物园附近溜达，咱们俩没准儿会碰上。”

“好的。”

“现在，”莫莉说，“把乔治的事原原本本讲给我听：你们俩怎么认识的、你头一回见他时觉得他怎么样、他早饭爱吃什么、平时聊些什么、他还说过我什么。”

### IV / 四

===Original===

It might have been expected that the passage of time, giving opportunity for quiet reflection on the subject of the illogical nature of the infatuation in which he had allowed himself to become involved, would have brought remorse to so clear and ruthless a thinker as Hamilton Beamish. It was not so, but far otherwise. As Hamilton Beamish sat in the antechamber of Madame Eulalie’s office next day, he gloried in his folly: and when his better self endeavoured to point out to him that what had happened was that he had allowed himself to be ensnared by a girl’s face﻿—that is to say, by a purely fortuitous arrangement of certain albuminoids and fatty molecules, all Hamilton Beamish did was to tell his better self to put its head in a bag. He was in love, and he liked it. He was in love, and proud of it. His only really coherent thought as he waited in the anteroom was a resolve to withdraw the booklet on “The Marriage Sane” from circulation and try his hand at writing a poem or two.

“Madame Eulalie will see you now, sir,” announced the maid, breaking in upon his reverie.

Hamilton Beamish entered the inner room. And, having entered it, stopped dead.

===Chinese===

照理说，时光流逝，给了他静静反思的工夫，让他想一想自己卷入的这场痴迷何其不合逻辑，像汉密尔顿·比米什这样一位清明而毫不容情的思想者，本该生出几分悔意。实则不然，简直适得其反。第二天，汉密尔顿·比米什坐在尤拉莉夫人营业室的等候间里，正为自己的痴愚沾沾自喜；他心中那个“更好的我”竭力向他指明：眼下发生的，无非是他让一张姑娘的脸擒住了——也就是说，让纯属偶然凑到一起的若干蛋白体和脂肪分子擒住了——而汉密尔顿·比米什的全部回应，就是叫那个“更好的我”把脑袋塞进麻袋里去。他恋爱了，而且乐在其中。他恋爱了，而且引以为荣。他在等候间里守着的时候，唯一真正连贯的念头，就是决意把那本论“理性婚姻”的小册子收回停售，自己试笔写上一两首诗。

“尤拉莉夫人现在可以见您了，先生。”女仆通报道，打断了他的遐想。

汉密尔顿·比米什走进里间。一走进去，便钉在了原地。

===Original===

“You!” he exclaimed.

The girl gave that fleeting pat at her hair which is always Woman’s reaction to the unexpected situation. And Hamilton Beamish looking at that hair emotionally, perceived that he had been right in his yesterday’s surmise. It was, as he had suspected, a gleamy mass, sparkling with life and possessing that incomparable softness, freshness and luxuriance.

“Why, how do you do?” said the girl.

“I’m fine,” said Hamilton Beamish.

“We seem fated to meet.”

“And I’m not quarrelling with fate.”

===Chinese===

“是你！”他失声道。

姑娘飞快地拍了拍头发——每逢意外的局面，女人的反应向来如此。汉密尔顿·比米什动情地望着那头秀发，发觉自己昨天的揣想分毫不差。正如他所料，那是一头光华流转的秀发，莹莹有生气，兼有那种无可比拟的柔软、鲜润与丰茂。

“哎呀，您好哇？”姑娘说。

“我很好。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。

“咱们好像命里注定要碰面。”

“我也不打算跟命运抬杠。”

===Original===

“No?”

“No,” said Hamilton Beamish. “Fancy it being you!”

“Fancy who being me?”

“Fancy you being you.” It occurred to him that he was not making himself quite clear. “I mean, I was sent here with a message for Madame Eulalie, and she turns out to be you.”

“A message? Who from?”

“From whom?” corrected Hamilton Beamish. Even in the grip of love, a specialist on Pure English remains a specialist on Pure English.

===Chinese===

“是吗？”

“不抬杠。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“居然会是你！”

“居然谁会是我？”

“居然你会是你。”他忽然觉得自己没有说清楚。“我是说，人家托我带口信给尤拉莉夫人，谁知她就是您。”

“口信？谁托的？”

“是‘受何人所托’。”汉密尔顿·比米什纠正道。纵然身在爱河，纯正英语专家仍旧是纯正英语专家。

===Original===

“That’s what I said﻿—Who from?”

Hamilton Beamish smiled an indulgent smile. These little mistakes could be corrected later﻿—possibly on the honeymoon.

“From Molly Waddington. She asked me to.﻿ ﻿…”

“Oh, then you don’t want me to read your hand?”

“There is nothing I want more in this world,” said Hamilton Beamish warmly, “than to have you read my hand.”

“I don’t have to read it to tell your character, of course,” said the girl. “I can see that at a glance.”

===Chinese===

“我说的就是这个——‘谁托的’？”

汉密尔顿·比米什宽厚地一笑。这类小毛病尽可以日后再纠正——兴许在蜜月里。

“莫莉·沃丁顿托的。她请我……”

“哦，那您不是要我给您看手相喽？”

“这世上我什么都可以不要，”汉密尔顿·比米什热切地说，“只求您给我看手相。”

“当然了，看您的性格，我犯不着看手，”姑娘说。“我一眼就看得出来。”

===Original===

“You can?”

“Oh, certainly. You have a strong, dominating nature and a keen incisive mind. You have great breadth of vision, iron determination, and marvellous insight. Yet with it all you are at heart gentle, kind and lovable; deeply altruistic and generous to a fault. You have it in you to be a leader of men. You remind me of Julius Caesar, Shakespeare and Napoleon Bonaparte.”

“Tell me more,” said Hamilton Beamish.

“If you ever fell in love.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“是吗？”

“哦，当然。您天性刚强，有威慑力，头脑敏锐，看问题一针见血。您目光远大，意志如铁，洞察力惊人。可尽管如此，您内心温柔、善良、可爱；大公无私，慷慨得近乎没了分寸。您天生有领袖群伦之才。您让我想起尤利乌斯·恺撒、莎士比亚和拿破仑·波拿巴。”

“接着说。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。

“您要是恋爱了……”

===Original===

“If I ever fell in love.﻿ ﻿…”

“If you ever fell in love,” said the girl, raising her eyes to his and drawing a step closer, “you would.﻿ ﻿…”

“Mr. Delancy Cabot,” announced the maid.

“Oh, darn it!” said Madame Eulalie. “I forgot I had an appointment. Send him in.”

“May I wait?” breathed Hamilton Beamish devoutly.

“Please do. I shan’t be long.” She turned to the door. “Come in, Mr. Cabot.”

===Chinese===

“我要是恋爱了……”

“您要是恋爱了，”姑娘抬起眼睛望着他，又凑近一步，“那您就会……”

“德兰西·卡博特（Delancy Cabot）先生到。”女仆通报道。

“哦，真该死！”尤拉莉夫人说。“我忘了还有个预约。请他进来。”

“我能在这儿等吗？”汉密尔顿·比米什虔敬地低声问。

“请便。误不了多久。”她转向门口。“卡博特先生，请进。”

===Original===

Hamilton Beamish wheeled around. A long, stringy person was walking daintily into the room. He was richly, even superbly, dressed in the conventional costume of the popular clubman and pet of Society. He wore lavender gloves and a carnation in his buttonhole, and a vast expanse of snowy collar encircled a neck which suggested that he might be a throwback to some giraffe ancestor. A pleasing feature of this neck was an Adam’s apple that could have belonged to only one man of Hamilton Beamish’s acquaintance.

“Garroway!” cried Hamilton Beamish. “What are you doing here? And what the devil does this masquerade mean?”

The policeman seemed taken aback. His face became as red as his wrists. But for the collar, which held him in a grip of iron, his jaw would no doubt have fallen.

“I didn’t expect to find you here, Mr. Beamish,” he said apologetically.

===Chinese===

汉密尔顿·比米什猛地转身。一个又高又瘦的人正袅袅婷婷地踱进屋来。他衣着华贵，甚至可以说气派非凡，一身走红社交圈的俱乐部名士的标准行头。他戴着淡紫色手套，纽孔里插一朵康乃馨，一大片雪白的衣领围着的脖颈，让人疑心此人是某种长颈鹿祖先的返祖。这条脖颈上有一个悦目的特点：一颗喉结——在汉密尔顿·比米什认识的人里，长这颗喉结的只可能有一个人。

“加罗威（Garroway）！”汉密尔顿·比米什叫道。“你在这儿干什么？这场化装把戏究竟是什么意思？”

那警察似乎吓了一跳。他的脸红得跟他的手腕一个颜色。若不是那条衣领像铁箍一样锁着他，他的下巴颏无疑早就掉下来了。

“没想到会在这儿碰见您，比米什先生。”他满怀歉意地说。

===Original===

“I didn’t expect to find you here, calling yourself De Courcy Bellville.”

“Delancy Cabot, sir.”

“Delancy Cabot, then.”

“I like the name,” urged the policeman. “I saw it in a book.”

The girl was breathing hard.

“Is this man a policeman?” she cried.

===Chinese===

“我也没想到会在这儿碰见你，还自称什么德·库西·贝尔维尔（De Courcy Bellville）。”

“是德兰西·卡博特，先生。”

“那就德兰西·卡博特。”

“我喜欢这个名字。”警察恳求道。“我在一本书里看到的。”

姑娘呼吸急促起来。

“这个人是警察？”她嚷道。

===Original===

“Yes, he is,” said Hamilton Beamish. “His name is Garroway, and I am teaching him to write poetry. And what I want to know,” he thundered, turning on the unhappy man, whose Adam’s apple was now leaping like a young lamb in the springtime, “is what are you doing here, interrupting a﻿—interrupting a﻿—in short interrupting, when you ought either to be about your constabulary duties or else sitting quietly at home studying John Drinkwater. That,” said Hamilton Beamish, “is what I want to know.”

Officer Garroway coughed.

“The fact is, Mr. Beamish, I did not know that Madame Eulalie was a friend of yours.”

“Never mind whose friend she is.”

“But it makes all the difference, Mr. Beamish. I can now go back to headquarters and report that Madame Eulalie is above suspicion. You see, sir, I was sent here by my superior officers to effect a cop.”

===Chinese===

“他就是。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“他叫加罗威，我在教他写诗。而我想知道的是，”他转脸对着那个倒霉蛋，劈头一声怒喝——此人这时的喉结已蹦得像春日里的小羊羔——“你跑到这儿来搅和什么？搅和一场——搅和一场——总而言之，搅和！你本职不是该去巡街值勤，就是该安安静静坐在家里研读约翰·德林克沃特（John Drinkwater）。这个，”汉密尔顿·比米什说，“就是我想知道的。”

加罗威警官咳嗽了一声。

“实情是这样的，比米什先生，小的起先不知道尤拉莉夫人是您的朋友。”

“别管她是谁的朋友。”

“可这就大不一样了，比米什先生。小的这下可以回总局去禀报，说尤拉莉夫人嫌疑全无。您瞧，先生，是上级长官派小的到这儿来落实一桩警务的。”

===Original===

“What do you mean, effect a cop?”

“To make an arrest, Mr. Beamish.”

“Then do not say ‘effect a cop.’ Purge yourself of these vulgarisms, Garroway.”

“Yes, sir. I will indeed, sir.”

“Aim at the English Pure.”

“Yes, sir. Most certainly, Mr. Beamish.”

===Chinese===

“你说的‘落实一桩警务’，是什么意思？”

“就是执行逮捕，比米什先生。”

“那就别说‘落实一桩警务’。把这些粗鄙字眼从你的舌根上清洗出去，加罗威。”

“是，先生。小的决不敢了，先生。”

“要以纯正英语为鹄的。”

“是，先生。一定照办，比米什先生。”

===Original===

“And what on earth do you mean by saying that you were sent here to arrest this lady?”

“It has been called to the attention of my superior officers, Mr. Beamish, that Madame Eulalie is in the habit of telling fortunes for a monetary consideration. Against the law, sir.”

Hamilton Beamish snorted.

“Ridiculous! If that’s the law, alter it!”

“I will do my best, sir.”

“I have had the privilege of watching Madame Eulalie engaged upon her art, and she reveals nothing but the most limpid truth. So go back to your superior officers and tell them to jump off the Brooklyn Bridge.”

===Chinese===

“那你说派你来逮捕这位夫人，究竟是什么意思？”

“有情况已经传到上级长官耳朵里，比米什先生，说尤拉莉夫人惯常收费给人算命。这是犯法的，先生。”

汉密尔顿·比米什哼了一声。

“荒唐！法律若是这样，那就改法律！”

“小的尽力而为，先生。”

“我曾有幸亲见尤拉莉夫人施展她的艺业，她揭示的无一不是澄澈见底的真话。所以你回你的上级长官那里去，叫他们从布鲁克林大桥上跳下去。”

===Original===

“Yes, sir. I will, sir.”

“And now leave us. We would be alone.”

“Yes, Mr. Beamish,” said Officer Garroway humbly. “At once, Mr. Beamish.”

For some moments after the door had closed, the girl stood staring at Hamilton Beamish with wondering eyes.

“Was that man really a policeman?”

“He was.”

===Chinese===

“是，先生。小的这就去，先生。”

“现在你退下吧。我们要单独待着。”

“是，比米什先生。”加罗威警官低声下气地说。“这就走，比米什先生。”

门关上之后好一会儿，姑娘还站在那里，用惊疑不定的眼睛望着汉密尔顿·比米什。

“那个人真是个警察？”

“他是。”

===Original===

“And you handled him like that, and he said, ‘Yes, sir!’ and ‘No, sir!’ and crawled out on all fours.” She drew a deep breath. “It seems to me that you are just the sort of friend a lonely girl needs in this great city.”

“I am only too delighted that I was able to be of service.”

“Service is right! Mr. Beamish.﻿ ﻿…”

“My first name is Hamilton.”

She looked at him, amazed.

===Chinese===

“您把他整治成那样，他还满口‘是，先生！’‘不是，先生！’，四脚着地爬了出去。”她深深吸了一口气。“要我说，在这座大城市里，一个孤单的女孩子正需要您这样的朋友。”

“能为姑娘效劳，我高兴还来不及。”

“‘效劳’二字，说得再确切不过了！比米什先生……”

“我的名字叫汉密尔顿。”

她惊异地望着他。

===Original===

“You are not *the* Hamilton Beamish? Not the man who wrote the booklets?”

“I have written a few booklets.”

“Why, you’re my favourite author! If it hadn’t been for you I would still be mouldering in a little one-horse town where there wasn’t even a good soda-fountain. But I got hold of a couple of your Are You in a Groove? things, and I packed up my grip and came right along to New York to lead a larger life. If I’d known yesterday that you were Hamilton Beamish, I’d have kissed you on the doorstep!”

===Chinese===

“您该不会就是那位汉密尔顿·比米什？写小册子的那位？”

“我写过几本小册子。”

“哎呀，您是我最喜欢的作家！要不是您，我如今还烂在一个偏僻得可怜的乡下小镇上呢，那里连个像样的汽水柜台都没有。后来我弄到两本您写的《你是否墨守成规？》（Are You in a Groove?），当即收拾提包，直奔纽约，来过更辽阔的日子。昨天要是知道您就是汉密尔顿·比米什，我在台阶上就把您吻了！”

===Original===

It was Hamilton Beamish’s intention to point out that a curtained room with a closed door was an even more suitable place for such a demonstration, but, even as he tried to speak, there gripped him for the first time in his life a strange, almost George Finch-like shyness. One deprecates the modern practice of exposing the great, but candour compels one to speak out and say that at this juncture Hamilton Beamish emitted a simpering giggle and began to twiddle his fingers.

The strange weakness passed, and he was himself again. He adjusted his glasses firmly.

“Would you,” he asked, “could you possibly.﻿ ﻿… Do you think you could manage to come and lunch somewhere tomorrow?”

The girl uttered an exclamation of annoyance.

“Isn’t that too bad!” she said. “I can’t.”

===Chinese===

汉密尔顿·比米什本想指出：一间垂着窗帘、关着房门的屋子，才是行此大礼更合适的所在；可话未出口，一种奇怪的、几乎是乔治·芬奇式的羞怯，生平头一遭攫住了他。揭伟人之短，固为今人所不取；然而直笔难违，只得实话实说：值此关头，汉密尔顿·比米什发出一串嘿嘿的痴笑，手指头也绕捏起来。

这阵怪病过去了，他又恢复如常。他把眼镜扶得端端正正。

“不知您，”他问，“能不能劳驾……您看您明天能否设法出来，跟我到什么地方吃顿午饭？”

姑娘懊恼地惊呼了一声。

“真不巧！”她说。“我去不了。”

===Original===

“The day after?”

“I’m sorry. I’m afraid I shall be off the map for three weeks. I’ve got to jump on a train tomorrow and go visit the old folks back in East Gilead. It’s Pop’s birthday on Saturday, and I never miss it.”

“East Gilead?”

“Idaho. You wouldn’t have heard of the place, but it’s there.”

“But I have heard of it. A great friend of mine comes from East Gilead.”

===Chinese===

“后天呢？”

“对不起。只怕我有三个星期要销声匿迹了。明天我就得跳上火车，回东吉利亚德（East Gilead）看老家的人去。星期六是我老爸的生日，我从不错过。”

“东吉利亚德？”

“爱达荷州的。您多半没听说过这地方，可它就在那儿。”

“可我就听说过。我有位挚友，就是东吉利亚德人。”

===Original===

“You don’t say! Who?”

“A man named George Finch.”

She laughed amusedly.

“You don’t actually mean to tell me you know George Finch?”

“He is my most intimate friend.”

“Then I trust for your sake,” said the girl, “that he is not such a yap as he used to be.”

===Chinese===

“真的呀！谁呀？”

“一个名叫乔治·芬奇的人。”

她觉得有趣，笑了起来。

“您该不是说笑话，您认识乔治·芬奇吧？”

“他是我最亲密的朋友。”

“那看您的面子，”姑娘说，“但愿他不像从前那样，是个窝囊废了。”

===Original===

Hamilton Beamish reflected. Was George Finch a yap? How precisely did one estimate the yaphood of one’s friends?

“By the word ‘yap’ you mean.﻿ ﻿…”

“I mean a yap. The sort of fellow who couldn’t say Bo to a goose.”

Hamilton Beamish had never seen George Finch in conversation with a goose, but he thought he was a good enough judge of character to be able to credit him with the ability to perform the very trivial deed of daring indicated.

“I fancy New York has changed George,” he replied, after reflection. “In fact, now that I remember, it was on more or less that very subject that I called to see you in a professional capacity. The fact is, George Finch has fallen violently in love with Molly Waddington, the stepdaughter of your client, Mrs. Waddington.”

===Chinese===

汉密尔顿·比米什琢磨起来。乔治·芬奇是个窝囊废吗？朋友的窝囊废气质，究竟该按什么章程来鉴定？

“你说的‘窝囊废’，指的是……”

“我说窝囊废就是窝囊废。就是那种冲着一只鹅连一声‘呔’都不敢喊的家伙。”

汉密尔顿·比米什从没见过乔治·芬奇同一只鹅交谈，但他自信看人的眼力还不坏，这点微末的胆量，他相信乔治还是拿得出来的。

“我想，纽约已经把乔治改造过来了。”他想了想，答道。“其实，我如今想起来，我以主顾的身份登门拜访您，为的差不多正是这件事。实情是，乔治·芬奇正狂热地爱着莫莉·沃丁顿——您的顾主沃丁顿夫人的继女。”

===Original===

“You don’t say! And I suppose he’s too shy to come within a mile of her.”

“On the contrary. The night before last he seems to have forced his way into the house﻿—you might say, practically forced his way﻿—and now Mrs. Waddington has forbidden him to see Molly again, fearing that he will spoil her plan of marrying the poor child to a certain Lord Hunstanton.”

The girl stared.

“You’re right! George must have altered.”

“And we were wondering﻿—Molly and I﻿—if we could possibly induce you to stoop to a﻿—shall I say a benevolent little ruse. Mrs. Waddington is coming to see you today at five, and it was Molly’s suggestion that I should sound you as to whether you would consent to take a look in the crystal and tell Mrs. Waddington that you see danger threatening Molly from a dark man with an eyeglass.”

===Chinese===

“真的呀！我猜他羞得很，连她一英里之内都不敢靠近吧。”

“恰恰相反。前天夜里他好像硬闯进了那所房子——可以说是实实在在的硬闯——如今沃丁顿夫人已不许他再见莫莉，生怕他搅了她的一盘好计划：她要把这可怜的孩子嫁给一位亨斯坦顿勋爵。”

姑娘瞪大了眼睛。

“您说得对！乔治准是变了。”

“我们俩——莫莉和我——正在盘算，能不能有劳您屈尊，行一个——姑且叫它仁善的小小的巧计。沃丁顿夫人今天五点钟要来看您，莫莉的主意是，让我先来探探您的口风：您可肯朝水晶球里看上一看，告诉沃丁顿夫人，说您看见危险正从一位戴单片眼镜的黑发男子那边，步步逼近莫莉。”

===Original===

“Of course.”

“You will?”

“It isn’t much to do in return for all you have done for me.”

“Thank you, thank you,” said Hamilton Beamish. “I knew, the moment I set eyes on you, that you were a woman in a million. I wonder﻿—could you possibly come to lunch one day after you return?”

“I’d love it.”

“I’ll leave you my telephone number.”

===Chinese===

“那当然。”

“您肯了？”

“比起您为我做的一切，这点小事算不了什么。”

“谢谢，谢谢。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“我第一眼看见您就知道，您是万里挑一的女性。不知——等您回来之后，哪天能不能来吃顿午饭？”

“我乐意极了。”

“我把电话号码留给您。”

===Original===

“Thanks. Give George my regards. I’d like to see him when I get back.”

“You shall. Goodbye.”

“Goodbye, Mr. Beamish.”

“Hamilton.”

Her face wore a doubtful look.

“I don’t much like that name Hamilton. It’s kind of stiff.”

===Chinese===

“谢谢。替我问乔治好。我回来以后倒想见见他。”

“一定让您见。再见。”

“再见，比米什先生。”

“汉密尔顿。”

她脸上现出为难的神气。

“汉密尔顿这个名字我不大喜欢。有点儿板正。”

===Original===

Hamilton Beamish had a brief struggle with himself.

“My name is also James. At one time in my life many people used to call me Jimmy.” He shuddered a little, but repeated the word bravely: “Jimmy.”

“Put me on the list,” said the girl. “I like that much better. Goodbye, Jimmy.”

“Goodbye,” said Hamilton Beamish.

So ended the first spasm of a great man’s love-story. A few moments later, Hamilton Beamish was walking in a sort of dance-measure down the street. Near Washington Square he gave a small boy a dollar and asked him if he was going to be President some day.

===Chinese===

汉密尔顿·比米什内心短暂地斗争了一番。

“我也叫詹姆斯。我一生中有个时期，许多人都管我叫吉米。”他微微打了个寒噤，还是勇敢地把那个词重复了一遍：“吉米。”

“把我也列进那份名单吧。”姑娘说。“这个名字我喜欢得多。再见，吉米。”

“再见。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。

一位伟人的恋爱史，第一阵痉挛就此落幕。片刻之后，汉密尔顿·比米什已踩着一种近乎舞步的节拍走在街上。快到华盛顿广场时，他送给一个小男孩一美元，问他将来打算不打算当总统。

### V / 五

===Original===

“George,” said Hamilton Beamish, “I met someone today who knew you back in East Gilead. A girl.”

“What was her name? Did Molly give you any message for me?”

“Madame Eulalie.”

“I don’t remember anyone called that. Did Molly give you any message for me?”

“She is slim and graceful and has tender grey eyes like mists floating over some pool in Fairyland.”

===Chinese===

“乔治，”汉密尔顿·比米什说，“我今天遇见一个人，从前在东吉利亚德就认识你。一位姑娘。”

“她叫什么名字？莫莉有什么话托你带给我吗？”

“尤拉莉夫人。”

“我不记得有叫这个名字的人。莫莉有什么话托你带给我吗？”

“她苗条婀娜，一双温柔的灰眼睛，像雾霭浮在仙境的池水上。”

===Original===

“I certainly don’t remember anyone in East Gilead like that. Did Molly give you any message for me?”

“No.”

“She didn’t?” George flung himself despairingly into a chair. “This is the end!”

“Oh, yes, she did,” said Hamilton Beamish. “I was forgetting. She told me to tell you that, if you happened to be in Central Park tomorrow afternoon near the Zoo, you might meet her.”

“This is the maddest, merriest day of all the glad New Year,” said George Finch.

===Chinese===

“东吉利亚德这样的人，我实在不记得有过。莫莉到底有什么话托你带给我吗？”

“没有。”

“没有？”乔治绝望地瘫进一张椅子。“这下全完了！”

“哦，有的，有的。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“我倒忘了。她让我告诉你，明天下午你若碰巧在中央公园动物园附近，没准儿会碰见她。”

“这是欢乐新年里最疯狂、最快活的一天！”乔治·芬奇说。

## V / 第五章

### I / 一

===Original===

Madame Eulalie peered into the crystal that was cupped between her shapely hands. The face that had caused Hamilton Beamish to jettison the principles of a lifetime was concentrated and serious.

“The mists begin to clear away!” she murmured.

“Ah!” said Mrs. Waddington. She had been hoping they would.

===Chinese===

尤拉莉夫人（Madame Eulalie）凝神端详着捧在那双纤纤玉手之间的水晶球。那张曾令汉密尔顿·比米什（Hamilton Beamish）把毕生信条尽数抛弃的脸，此刻绷得紧紧的，神情庄重。

“迷雾开始消散了！”她喃喃道。

“啊！”沃丁顿夫人（Mrs. Waddington）应了一声。她一直在盼着迷雾消散呢。

===Original===

“There is someone very near to you.﻿ ﻿…”

“A spirit?” said Mrs. Waddington nervously, casting an apprehensive glance over her shoulder. She was never quite sure that something of the sort might not pop out at any moment from a corner of this dim-lit, incense-scented room.

“You misunderstand me,” said Madame Eulalie gravely. “I mean that that which is taking shape in the crystal concerns someone very near to you, some near relative.”

“Not my husband?” said Mrs. Waddington in a flat voice. A woman careful with her money, she did not relish the idea of handing over ten dollars for visions about Sigsbee H.

“Does your husband’s name begin with an *M*?”

“No,” said Mrs. Waddington, relieved.

===Chinese===

“有一位同您十分亲近的人……”

“是幽灵吗？”沃丁顿夫人紧张地问，惴惴地朝背后溜了一眼。她始终拿不太准，在这间灯光幽暗、熏香缭绕的屋子里，保不定哪一刻就会有什么那路货色从角落里蹦出来。

“您误解我的意思了，”尤拉莉夫人郑重其事地说。“我是说，水晶球里正在成形的景象，关系到一位同您十分亲近的人，一位近亲。”

“不是我丈夫吧？”沃丁顿夫人的声音平平的。她用钱向来精打细算，可不喜欢掏十美元去听关于西格斯比·H的什么幻象。

“您丈夫的名字，是以 *M* 打头的吗？”

“不是，”沃丁顿夫人松了口气。

===Original===

“The letter *M* seems to be forming itself among the mists.”

“I have a stepdaughter, Molly.”

“Is she tall and dark?”

“No. Small and fair.”

“Then it is she!” said Madame Eulalie. “I see her in a wedding-dress, walking up an aisle. Her hand is on the arm of a dark man with an eyeglass. Do you know such a person?”

===Chinese===

“迷雾之中，仿佛有个 *M* 字正在成形。”

“我有一个继女，叫莫莉（Molly）。”

“她个子高、头发黑吗？”

“不。个子小，头发金黄。”

“那么就是她了！”尤拉莉夫人说。“我看见她身穿婚纱，走在教堂的通道上。她的手挽着一位戴单片眼镜的黑发男人。您认得这样一个人吗？”

===Original===

“Lord Hunstanton!”

“I do seem to sense the letter *H*.”

“Lord Hunstanton is a great friend of mine, and devoted to Molly. Do you really see her marrying him?”

“I see her walking up the aisle.”

“It’s the same thing.”

===Chinese===

“亨斯坦顿勋爵（Lord Hunstanton）！”

“我确实仿佛感应到一个 *H* 字。”

“亨斯坦顿勋爵是我的至交，对莫莉又一往情深。您当真看见她要嫁给他了吗？”

“我看见她走在教堂的通道上。”

“那还不一样。”

===Original===

“No! For she never reaches the altar.”

“Why not?” asked Mrs. Waddington, justly annoyed.

“From the crowd a woman springs forth. She bars the way. She seems to be speaking rapidly, with great emotion. And the man with the eyeglass is shrinking back, his face working horribly. His expression is very villainous. He raises a hand. He strikes the woman. She reels back. She draws out a revolver. And then.﻿ ﻿…”

“Yes?” cried Mrs. Waddington. “Yes?”

“The vision fades,” said Madame Eulalie, rising briskly with the air of one who has given a good ten dollars’ worth.

===Chinese===

“不一样！因为她根本到不了圣坛。”

“为什么到不了？”沃丁顿夫人问，恼得颇有道理。

“人群里蹿出来一个女人。她拦住去路。她像是语速飞快地说着什么，情绪异常激动。那个戴单片眼镜的男人连连后退，脸相狰狞得吓人。他一脸的凶神恶煞。他举起一只手。他打了那女人。她踉跄着倒退。她掏出一把左轮手枪。接着……”

“接着怎么样？”沃丁顿夫人失声叫道。“怎么样？”

“幻象消散了。”尤拉莉夫人说着，轻快地站起身来，一副自认这十块钱花得值的神气。

===Original===

“But it can’t be! It’s incredible.”

“The crystal never deceives.”

“But Lord Hunstanton is a most delightful man.”

“No doubt the woman with the revolver found him so﻿—to her cost.”

===Chinese===

“这不可能！真是难以置信。”

“水晶球是从来不说谎的。”

“可亨斯坦顿勋爵是位顶顶讨人喜欢的男人呀。”

“那位带左轮手枪的女人只怕也这么觉得——结果吃了她的大亏。”

===Original===

“But you may have been mistaken. Many men are dark and wear an eyeglass. What did this man look like?”

“What does Lord Hunstanton look like?”

“He is tall and beautifully proportioned, with clear blue eyes and a small moustache which he twists between the finger and thumb of his right hand.”

“It was he!”

“What shall I do?”

===Chinese===

“可您兴许看走了眼。黑脸膛、戴单片眼镜的男人多的是。这个人长的是副什么模样？”

“亨斯坦顿勋爵长的是副什么模样？”

“他身材颀长，体态匀称得无可挑剔，一双碧眼清朗有神，唇上蓄着一撮小胡子，惯用右手的大拇指和食指捻着它玩。”

“就是他！”

“我该怎么办呢？”

===Original===

“Well, obviously it would be criminal to allow Miss Waddington to associate with this man.”

“But he’s coming to dinner tonight.”

Madame Eulalie, whose impulses sometimes ran away with her, was about to say: “Poison his soup”: but contrived in time to substitute for this remark a sober shrug of the shoulders.

“I must leave it to you, Mrs. Waddington,” she said, “to decide on the best course of action. I cannot advise. I only warn. If you want change for a large bill, I think I can manage it for you,” she added, striking the business note.

===Chinese===

“这个嘛，显而易见，任凭沃丁顿小姐同此人交往下去，简直是作孽。”

“可他今晚就要来吃晚饭了。”

尤拉莉夫人有时管不住自己的一时冲动，一句“在他的汤里下毒”已经蹿到嘴边，好歹及时收住，改换成一副端庄稳重的耸肩。

“最稳妥的对策如何定夺，只好有劳沃丁顿夫人您自己了，”她说。“我不能出谋划策，我只管示警。您要是想找开一张大票子，我想我还能替您想点办法。”末了一句，她已经把话锋转回了生意经。

===Original===

All the way home to Seventy-Ninth Street Mrs. Waddington pondered deeply. And, as she was not a woman who, as a rule, exercised her brain to any great extent, by the time she reached the house she was experiencing some of the sensations of one who has been hit on the head by a sandbag. What she felt that she needed above all things in the world was complete solitude: and it was consequently with a jaundiced eye that she looked upon her husband, Sigsbee Horatio, when, a few moments after her return, he shuffled into the room where she had planted herself down for further intensive meditation.

“Well, Sigsbee?” said Mrs. Waddington, wearily.

“Oh, there you are,” said Sigsbee H.

“Do you want anything?”

“Well, yes and no,” said Sigsbee.

===Chinese===

一路返回东七十九街的途中，沃丁顿夫人苦苦思索。她平素原不惯于怎么大动脑筋，等到了宅邸门口，已经尝到了几分被人用沙袋闷击后脑的滋味。她觉得举世万物之中，自己此刻最最需要的莫过于绝对的清静。因此，回到家没几分钟，丈夫西格斯比·霍雷肖（Sigsbee Horatio）拖着步子踱进那间屋子——她特意在那儿安顿下来，好接着沉思——的时候，她投过去的便是一副透着愠恼的冷眼。

“说吧，西格斯比？”沃丁顿夫人有气无力地问。

“哦，原来你在这儿，”西格斯比·H说。

“你有事吗？”

“这个嘛，说有事也有事，说没事也没事。”西格斯比说。

===Original===

Mrs. Waddington was exasperated to perceive at this point that her grave matrimonial blunder was slithering about the parquet floor in the manner of one trying out new dance-steps.

“Stand still!” she cried.

“I can’t,” said Sigsbee H. “I’m too nervous.”

Mrs. Waddington pressed a hand to her throbbing brow.

“Then sit down!”

“I’ll try,” said Sigsbee doubtfully. He tested a chair, and sprang up instantly as if the seat had been charged with electricity. “I can’t,” he said. “I’m all of a twitter.”

===Chinese===

沃丁顿夫人恼火地发现，就在这当口，她那桩婚姻大事上的重大失策，正像个排练新舞步的人似的，在细木条地板上蹭来蹭去。

“站住别动！”她喝道。

“站不住啊，”西格斯比·H说。“我紧张得厉害。”

沃丁顿夫人抬起一只手，按住突突直跳的额头。

“那就坐下！”

“我试试。”西格斯比将信将疑。他试着往一张椅子上坐，随即像椅面通了电似的腾地弹了起来。“坐不住，”他说。“我浑身直打颤。”

===Original===

“What in the world do you mean?”

“I’ve got something to tell you and I don’t know how to begin.”

“What do you wish to tell me?”

“I don’t wish to tell you at all,” said Sigsbee frankly. “But I promised Molly I would. She came in a moment ago.”

“Well?”

===Chinese===

“你这话究竟是什么意思？”

“我有件事要告诉你，可不知道打哪儿开口。”

“你要告诉我的到底是什么？”

“我压根儿就不想告诉你，”西格斯比说得坦白。“可我答应过莫莉要说的。她方才来过。”

“哦？”

===Original===

“I was in the library. She found me there and told me this.”

“Do kindly get to the point, Sigsbee!”

“I promised her I would break it gently.”

“Break what gently? You are driving me mad.”

===Chinese===

“我那时候在书房。她在书房里找到了我，把这事告诉了我。”

“劳驾你捡要紧的说，西格斯比！”

“我答应她，要婉转地把这消息透给你。”

“婉转地透什么？你要把我逼疯了。”

===Original===

“Do you remember,” asked Sigsbee, “a splendid young Westerner named Pinch who dropped in to dinner the night before last? A fine, breezy.﻿ ﻿…”

“I am not likely to forget the person you mention. I have given strict instructions that he is never again to be admitted to the house.”

“Well, this splendid young Pinch.﻿ ﻿…”

“I am not interested in Mr. Finch﻿—which is, I believe, his correct name.”

===Chinese===

“你还记得，”西格斯比问，“前天晚上跑来吃晚饭的那位姓平奇（Pinch）的出色西部青年吗？一表人才，风风火火的……”

“你说的那个人，我想忘也忘不掉。我已经下了严格的命令，此人从此永远不许再跨进这个家门。”

“就是这位出色的平奇青年……”

“芬奇先生的事，我没有兴趣——我记得这才是他的大名。”

===Original===

“Pinch, I thought.”

“Finch! And what does his name matter, anyway?”

“Well,” said Sigsbee, “it matters this much, that Molly seems to want to make it hers. What I’m driving at, if you see what I mean, is that Molly came in a moment ago and told me that she and this young fellow Finch have just gone and got engaged to be married!”

===Chinese===

“我记得是平奇吧。”

“芬奇！再说他姓什么又有什么要紧？”

“有要紧，”西格斯比说，“要紧就要紧在，莫莉好像打算把这个姓改成她自己的。我想说的是——但愿你明白我的意思——莫莉方才来过，告诉我说，她和这位芬奇小伙子已经跑去把婚订下了！”

### II / 二

===Original===

Having uttered these words, Sigsbee Horatio stood gazing at his wife with something of the spellbound horror of a man who has bored a hole in a dam and sees the water trickling through and knows that it is too late to stop it. He had had a sort of idea all along that the news might affect her rather powerfully, and his guess was coming true. Nothing could make a woman of Mrs. Waddington’s physique “leap from her chair”: but she had begun to rise slowly like a balloon half-filled with gas: and her face had become so contorted and her eyes so bulging that any competent medical man of sporting tastes would have laid seven to four on a fit of apoplexy in the next few minutes.

But by some miracle this disaster﻿—if you could call it that﻿—did not occur. For quite a considerable time the sufferer had trouble with her vocal chords and could emit nothing but guttural croaks. Then, mastering herself with a strong effort, she spoke.

“What did you say?”

“You heard,” said Sigsbee H. sullenly, twisting his fingers and wishing that he was out in Utah, rustling cattle.

===Chinese===

一言既出，西格斯比·霍雷肖便直愣愣地站在那儿望着妻子，那副神情，活像一个在水坝上钻了个窟窿、眼见水丝丝往外渗、心里明白为时已晚的人，中了魔怔似的惊恐万状。他一直隐隐觉得，这消息会叫她大受震动，如今他的估计应验了。任凭什么法子，也没法叫沃丁顿夫人这副体格的女人“从椅子上跳起来”：可她已经开始像一只打了一半气的气球，慢悠悠地往上升；她那张脸扭成了麻花，两只眼睛暴凸出来，但凡哪位好赌两把的合格医生瞧在眼里，都肯按七比四的赔率，赌她几分钟之内就要中风大发作。

可谓天可怜见，这场大祸——如果也算得上大祸的话——竟然没有降临。好一阵子，这位受难者声带失灵，只发得出几声粗嘎的咯咯声。随后，她奋力自持，开了口。

“你说什么？”

“你听见了。”西格斯比·H阴沉沉地说，手指头绞来绞去，恨不得自己远在犹他州干盗牛的营生。

===Original===

Mrs. Waddington moistened her lips.

“Did I understand you to say that Molly was engaged to be married to that Finch?”

“Yes, I did. And,” added Sigsbee H., giving battle in the first line of trenches, “it’s no good saying it was all my fault, because I had nothing to do with it.”

“It was you who brought this man into the house.”

“Well, yes.” Sigsbee had overlooked that weak spot in his defences. “Well, yes.”

===Chinese===

沃丁顿夫人舔了舔嘴唇。

“你的意思是说，莫莉同那个姓芬奇的订了婚？”

“对，我是这个意思。而且，”西格斯比·H补了一句，一上来就把第一道战壕也抢占了，“你们要想说这全怪我，那是白搭，这事儿跟我半点相干也没有。”

“是你把这个男人领进这个家来的。”

“这个嘛，是的。”西格斯比的防线上恰恰漏掉了这一处弱点。“这个嘛，是的。”

===Original===

There came upon Mrs. Waddington a ghastly calm like that which comes upon the surface of molten lava in the crater of a volcano just before the stuff shoots out and starts doing the local villagers a bit of no good.

“Ring the bell,” she said.

Sigsbee H. rang the bell.

“Ferris,” said Mrs. Waddington, “ask Miss Molly to come here.”

“Very good, madam.”

===Chinese===

沃丁顿夫人周身泛起一股阴惨惨的平静，恰似火山口里滚滚熔岩的表面，在岩浆喷薄而出、眼看要叫左近村民大倒其霉之前一刻所有的平静。

“按铃。”她说。

西格斯比·H按了铃。

“费里斯（Ferris），”沃丁顿夫人说，“请莫莉小姐到这儿来。”

“是，太太。”

===Original===

In the interval which elapsed between the departure of the butler and the arrival of the erring daughter, no conversation brilliant enough to be worth reporting took place in the room. Once Sigsbee said “Er﻿—” and in reply Mrs. Waddington said “Be quiet!” but that completed the dialogue. When Molly entered, Mrs. Waddington was looking straight in front of her and heaving gently, and Sigsbee H. had just succeeded in breaking a valuable china figure which he had taken from an occasional table and was trying in a preoccupied manner to balance on the end of a paper-knife.

“Ferris says you want to see me, mother,” said Molly, floating brightly in.

She stood there, looking at the two with shining eyes. Her cheeks were delightfully flushed: and there was about her so radiant an air of sweet, innocent, girlish gaiety that it was all Mrs. Waddington could do to refrain from hurling a bust of Edgar Allan Poe at her head.

“I do want to see you,” said Mrs. Waddington. “Pray tell me instantly what is all this nonsense I hear about you and.﻿ ﻿…” She choked. “… and Mr. Finch.”

“To settle a bet,” said Sigsbee H., “is his name Finch or Pinch?”

===Chinese===

从男管家退下到犯错的女儿驾到，中间那段空当里，屋里没产生过任何精彩到值得一记的对话。西格斯比有一回说了声“呃——”，沃丁顿夫人回了句“闭嘴！”，整场对话就到此为止。莫莉进门的时候，沃丁顿夫人正直勾勾瞪着前方，胸口微微起伏；西格斯比·H则刚刚失手摔碎了一尊值钱的瓷人——那是他从一张小茶几上捧下来、正心不在焉地打算让它稳稳当立在裁纸刀刀尖上的。

“费里斯说您要见我，妈妈。”莫莉欢天喜地地飘了进来。

她站在那儿，眼睛亮晶晶地望着两人。双颊泛着叫人看了欢喜的红晕：周身洋溢着那么灿烂的甜美、天真、少女的欢欣气韵，以致沃丁顿夫人费了好大的劲，才忍住没有抄起一座埃德加·爱伦·坡的半身像朝她脑袋砸过去。

“我是要见你，”沃丁顿夫人说。“请你立刻告诉我，我听来的那些关于你和……”她噎住了。“……和芬奇先生的混账话，究竟是怎么回事。”

“为了打赌解个闷，”西格斯比·H说，“他到底姓芬奇还是姓平奇？”

===Original===

“Finch, of course.”

“I’m bad at names,” said Sigsbee. “I was in college with a fellow called Follansbee and do you think I could get it out of my nut that that guy’s name was Ferguson? Not in a million years! I.﻿ ﻿…”

“Sigsbee!”

“Hello?”

“Be quiet.” Mrs. Waddington concentrated her attention on Molly once more. “Your father says that you told him some absurd story about being.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“当然姓芬奇。”

“我记名字的本事最糟糕，”西格斯比说。“念大学那会儿有个同学叫福兰斯比（Follansbee），你猜‘那家伙叫弗格森（Ferguson）’这个念头，我能不能把它从脑袋里赶出去？再活一百万年也休想！我……”

“西格斯比！”

“哎？”

“闭嘴。”沃丁顿夫人重新把注意力集中到莫莉身上。“你父亲说，你对他说了一篇关于你如何如何的荒唐话……”

===Original===

“Engaged to George?” said Molly. “Yes, it’s quite true. I am. By a most extraordinary chance we met this afternoon in Central Park near the Zoo.﻿ ﻿…”

“A place,” said Sigsbee H., “I’ve meant to go to a hundred times and never seen yet.”

“Sigsbee!”

“All right, all right! I was only saying.﻿ ﻿…”

“We were both tremendously surprised, of course,” said Molly. “I said ‘Fancy meeting you here!’ and he said.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“同乔治·芬奇（George Finch）订婚了？”莫莉说。“对，千真万确，我订婚了。也真是巧得出奇，今天下午我们在中央公园动物园旁边碰上了……”

“那地方，”西格斯比·H说，“我打算去上一百回了，到今天还没见识过。”

“西格斯比！”

“好好好！我就是随口一说……”

“我们俩当然都又惊又喜，”莫莉说。“我说‘真想不到在这儿碰见你！’然后他说……”

===Original===

“I have no wish to hear what Mr. Finch said.”

“Well, anyway, we walked round for awhile, looking at the animals, and suddenly he asked me to marry him outside the cage of the Siberian yak.”

“No, sir!” exclaimed Sigsbee H. with a sudden strange firmness, the indulgent father who for once in his life asserts himself. “When you get married, you’ll be married in St. Thomas’s like any other nice girl.”

“I mean it was outside the cage of the Siberian yak that he asked me to marry him.”

“Oh, ah!” said Sigsbee H.

===Chinese===

“芬奇先生说了什么，我没有兴趣听。”

“反正呢，我们逛了一大圈，看这看那的动物，忽然之间，就在西伯利亚牦牛的笼子外头，他向我求婚了。”

“不行，小姐！”西格斯比·H猛地喝了一声，透着一种说不出的坚定——一位百事纵容的父亲，平生头一遭自己拿了主意。“你结婚，就得像别的规规矩矩的姑娘一样，在圣托马斯教堂（St. Thomas’s）里办。”

“我的意思是说，他向我求婚，地点是在西伯利亚牦牛的笼子外头。”

“哦，呃！”西格斯比·H说。

===Original===

A dreamy look had crept into Molly’s eyes. Her lips were curved in a tender smile, as if she were reliving that wonderful moment in a girl’s life, when the man she loves beckons to her to follow him into Paradise.

“You ought to have seen his ears!” she said. “They were absolutely crimson.”

“You don’t say!” chuckled Sigsbee H.

“Scarlet! And, when he tried to speak, he gargled.”

“The poor simp!”

===Chinese===

一抹梦一般的神色悄悄爬上莫莉的眼睛。她的唇边弯起一弯温柔的笑意，仿佛正在重温一个姑娘一生里最美妙的那一刻——她心爱的男人朝她招手，唤她跟着自己一同走进天堂。

“你真该瞧瞧他那两只耳朵！”她说。“红得简直没治了。”

“真的假的！”西格斯比·H咯咯直乐。

“猩红！还有，他想开口说话的时候，喉咙里咯噜咯噜的，跟含着水漱口似的。”

“可怜的小傻瓜！”

===Original===

Molly turned on her father with flaming eyes.

“How dare you call my dear darling Georgie a simp?”

“How dare you call that simp your dear darling Georgie?” demanded Mrs. Waddington.

“Because he is my dear darling Georgie. I love him with all my heart, the precious lamb, and I’m going to marry him.”

“You are going to do nothing of the kind!” Mrs. Waddington quivered with outraged indignation. “Do you imagine I intend to allow you to ruin your life by marrying a despicable fortune-hunter?”

===Chinese===

莫莉两眼喷火，冲着她父亲转过身去。

“你怎么敢管我亲爱的宝贝乔吉（Georgie）叫小傻瓜？”

“你怎么敢管那个小傻瓜叫你亲爱的宝贝乔吉？”沃丁顿夫人喝问。

“因为他就是我亲爱的宝贝乔吉。我一颗心全扑在他身上，这只宝贝小羊羔，我就是要嫁给他。”

“这样的婚你休想结！”沃丁顿夫人气得浑身乱颤。“你以为我打算容许你毁掉自己的一生，去嫁一个卑鄙的淘金骗子吗？”

===Original===

“He isn’t a despicable fortune-hunter.”

“He is a penniless artist.”

“Well, I’m sure he is frightfully clever and will be able to sell his pictures for ever so much.”

“Tchah!”

“Besides,” said Molly defiantly, “when I marry I get that pearl necklace which father gave mother. I can sell that, and it will keep us going for years.”

===Chinese===

“他才不是什么卑鄙的淘金骗子。”

“他是个身无分文的画家。”

“哼，我敢保他聪明得不得了，他的画准能卖出天大的价钱。”

“呸！”

“再说，”莫莉倔头倔脑地扬言，“我结婚的时候，还能得到爸爸当年送妈妈的那串珍珠项链。我可以把它卖掉，那笔钱够我们过好几年呢。”

===Original===

Mrs. Waddington was about to reply﻿—and there is little reason to doubt that that reply would have been about as red-hot a comeback as any hundred and eighty pound woman had ever spoken﻿—when she was checked by a sudden exclamation of agony that proceeded from the lips of her husband.

“Whatever is the matter, Sigsbee?” she said, annoyed.

Sigsbee H. seemed to be wrestling with acute mental agitation. He was staring at his daughter with protruding eyes.

“Did you say you were going to sell that necklace?” he stammered.

“Oh, be quiet, Sigsbee!” said Mrs. Waddington. “What does it matter whether she sells the necklace or not? It has nothing to do with the argument. The point is that this misguided girl is proposing to throw herself away on a miserable, paint-daubing, ukulele-playing artist.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

沃丁顿夫人正要回嘴——毫无疑问，这一嘴回过去，定然是自有体重一百八十磅的女人以来顶顶火辣的一句——却不提防她丈夫嘴里突然迸出一声痛楚的惊呼，把话头生生截断了。

“西格斯比，你又是什么毛病？”她恼糟糟地问。

西格斯比·H像是正同剧烈的精神骚乱扭作一团。他双眼暴凸，直勾勾地瞪着女儿。

“你是说，你打算把那条项链卖掉？”他结结巴巴地问。

“哦，闭嘴，西格斯比！”沃丁顿夫人说。“她卖不卖项链有什么相干？这跟眼下的争辩毫不搭界。要紧的是，这个误入歧途的丫头，居然打算把自己糟蹋给一个可怜巴巴、抹涂抹涂、还弹尤克里里（ukulele）的画家……”

===Original===

“He doesn’t play the ukulele. He told me so.”

“… when she might, if she chose, marry a delightful man with a fine old English title who would.﻿ ﻿…”

Mrs. Waddington broke off. There had come back to her the memory of that scene in Madame Eulalie’s office.

Molly seized the opportunity afforded by her unexpected silence to make a counterattack.

“I wouldn’t marry Lord Hunstanton if he were the last man in the world.”

===Chinese===

“他不弹尤克里里。他自己亲口对我说的。”

“……可她只要愿意，本可以嫁一位可心意的、有古老尊贵英国头衔的男人，他会……”

沃丁顿夫人话到半截，戛然而止。尤拉莉夫人占卜室里的那一幕，重新浮上了她的心头。

莫莉趁着母亲这番出人意料的沉默所给的时机，发动了反攻。

“就算亨斯坦顿勋爵是天底下最后一个男人，我也不嫁。”

===Original===

“Honey,” said Sigsbee H. in a low, pleading voice, “I don’t think I’d sell that necklace if I were you.”

“Of course I shall sell it. We shall need the money when we are married.”

“You are not going to be married,” said Mrs. Waddington, recovering. “I should have thought any right-minded girl would have despised this wretched Finch. Why, the man appears to be so poor-spirited that he didn’t even dare to come here and tell me this awful news. He left it to you.﻿ ﻿…”

“George was not able to come here. The poor pet has been arrested by a policeman.”

===Chinese===

“宝贝儿，”西格斯比·H压着嗓子，一副央求的声气，“我要是你，就不卖那条项链。”

“我当然要卖。我们结了婚，是要用钱的。”

“你结不成这个婚，”沃丁顿夫人又还过魂来了。“照说呢，任何一个心智端正的姑娘，都该打心底里瞧不起这个可怜虫芬奇。瞧瞧吧，这个男人软骨头软到了家，连到这儿来亲口告诉我这个可怕消息都不敢。他竟让你来代劳……”

“乔治来不了。这可怜的小心肝儿让警察给逮走了。”

===Original===

“Ha!” cried Mrs. Waddington triumphantly. “And that is the sort of man you propose to marry! A gaol-bird!”

“Well, I think it shows what a sweet nature he has. He was so happy at being engaged that he suddenly stopped at Fifty-Ninth Street and Fifth Avenue and started giving away dollar-bills to everybody who came by. In about two minutes there was a crowd stretching right across to Madison Avenue, and the traffic was blocked for miles, and they called out the police-reserves, and George was taken away in a patrol-wagon, and I telephoned to Hamilton Beamish to go and bail him out and bring him along here. They ought to arrive at any moment.”

“Mr. Hamilton Beamish and Mr. George Finch,” said Ferris in the doorway. And the nicely-graduated way in which he spoke the two names would have conveyed at once to any intelligent listener that Hamilton Beamish was an honoured guest but that he had been forced to admit George Finch﻿—against all the promptings of his better nature﻿—because Mr. Beamish had told him to and he had been quelled by the man’s cold, spectacled eye.

“Here we are,” said Hamilton Beamish heartily. “Just in time, I perceive, to join in a jolly family discussion.”

Mrs. Waddington looked bleachingly at George, who was trying to hide behind a gate-leg table. For George Finch was conscious of not looking his best. Nothing so disorders the outer man as the process of being arrested and hauled to the coop by a posse of New York gendarmes. George’s collar was hanging loose from its stud: his waistcoat lacked three buttons: and his right eye was oddly discoloured where a high-minded officer, piqued by the fact that he should have collected crowds by scattering dollar-bills and even more incensed by the discovery that he had scattered all he possessed and had none left, had given him a hearty buffet during the ride in the patrol-wagon.

===Chinese===

“哈！”沃丁顿夫人得意洋洋地叫起来。“你打算嫁的就是这么一号人物！一个牢油子！”

“可我觉得，这恰恰说明他的心肠有多甜美。他订了婚太高兴了，走到第五十九街和第五大道的街口，忽然停住脚，见人就散发一元一张的钞票。不出两分钟，人群一直排到了麦迪逊大道，交通堵塞了好几英里，警方出动了后备警力，乔治让人塞进巡逻囚车带走了。我打电话给汉密尔顿·比米什，托他去把人保出来，再一道送到这儿来。这会儿他们随时都该到了。”

“汉密尔顿·比米什先生——乔治·芬奇先生。”费里斯在门口通报。他把两个名字念出来的那种层层递进的腔调，任何一个耳聪目明的听者都能立刻听出其中的分别：汉密尔顿·比米什是位贵客；至于乔治·芬奇，他是迫不得已才放行放进来的——全然违背了天良的种种驱策——只因比米什先生发过话，而那位先生镜片后面那双冷冰冰的眼睛，早把他镇压住了。

“我们来啦。”汉密尔顿·比米什热络地招呼。“依我看，来得正是时候，恰好赶上参加一场欢欢喜喜的家庭讨论。”

沃丁顿夫人朝乔治扫了一眼，那眼神简直能把人漂白。乔治正竭力往一张折腿餐桌背后躲。也难怪，乔治·芬奇自知这副仪表实在拿不出手。天底下再没有什么事，比被逮捕、被一大帮纽约警察老爷连拖带拽地押进局子，更能搅散一个人的体面了。乔治的硬领从领扣上松脱下来，晃晃荡荡；马甲上缺了三颗纽扣；右眼一片乌青，瞧着蹊跷——那是押上囚车之后，一位正义凛然的警官赏给他的一记结结实实的重拳。这位警官先是恼火此人竟敢靠撒钞票聚拢人群，随后又查出他撒的全是自己压箱底的家当、撒光了便一个子儿也不剩，愈发怒不可遏。

===Original===

“There is no discussion,” said Mrs. Waddington. “You do not suppose I am going to allow my daughter to marry a man like that.”

“Tut-tut!” said Hamilton Beamish. “George is not looking his best just now, but a wash and brush-up will do wonders.﻿ ﻿… What is your objection to George?”

Mrs. Waddington was at a momentary loss for a reply. Anybody, suddenly questioned as to why they disliked a slug or a snake or a black-beetle, might find it difficult on the spur of the moment to analyse and dissect their prejudice. Mrs. Waddington looked on her antipathy to George Finch as one of those deep, natural, fundamental impulses which the sensible person takes for granted. Broadly speaking, she objected to George because he was George. It was, as it were, his essential Georgeness that offended her. But, seeing that she was expected to be analytical, she forced her mind to the task.

“He is an artist.”

“So was Michelangelo.”

===Chinese===

“没有什么讨论，”沃丁顿夫人说。“你们总不至于以为，我会容许我女儿嫁给这样一个男人吧。”

“啧啧！”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“乔治这会儿是狼狈了点儿，可是洗把脸、拾掇拾掇，保管判若两人。……您反对乔治，理由何在？”

沃丁顿夫人一时语塞。任何人若是突然被问起为什么讨厌一条鼻涕虫、一条蛇或一只黑甲虫，只怕都难以在电光石火之间剖析出个所以然来。沃丁顿夫人一向把她对乔治·芬奇的反感，归入那种深沉、自然、根本的冲动之列——明理的人对这一类冲动，向来是视为理所当然、照单全收的。往大里说，她反对乔治，就因为乔治是乔治。不妨这么说，冒犯她的，正是他与生俱来的那股“乔治本质”（Georgeness）。可既然众人等着她条分缕析，她只得强按着自己的脑子开动起来。

“他是个画家。”

“米开朗琪罗（Michelangelo）也是画家。”

===Original===

“I never met him.”

“He was a very great man.”

Mrs. Waddington raised her eyebrows.

“I completely fail to understand, Mr. Beamish, why, when we are discussing this young man here with the black eye and the dirty collar, you should persist in diverting the conversation to the subject of a perfect stranger like this Mr. Angelo.”

“I merely wished to point out,” said Hamilton Beamish stiffly, “that the fact that he is an artist does not necessarily damn a man.”

===Chinese===

“我又没见过他。”

“他是一位顶顶伟大的人物。”

沃丁顿夫人扬了扬眉毛。

“比米什先生，我全然无法理解：我们明明在讨论眼前这位眼圈乌青、衣领肮脏的年轻人，您为什么偏要一个劲儿地把话头岔到安杰洛（Angelo）先生这样一位素昧平生的陌生人身上去。”

“我不过是想指出，”汉密尔顿·比米什板着脸说，“一个人是画家，这件事未必就足以毁掉他。”

===Original===

“There is no need,” retorted Mrs. Waddington with even greater stiffness, “to use bad language.”

“Besides, George is a rotten artist.”

“Rotten to the core, no doubt.”

“I mean,” said Hamilton Beamish, flushing slightly at the lapse from the English Pure into which emotion had led him, “he paints so badly that you can hardly call him an artist at all.”

“Is that so?” said George, speaking for the first time and speaking nastily.

===Chinese===

“用不着，”沃丁顿夫人回敬道，板得更僵，“满嘴污言秽语。”

“再说，乔治画画本来就是烂手艺。”

“烂到骨子里，那还用说。”

“我是说，”汉密尔顿·比米什说。方才一时动情，从“纯正英语”的规范上失足跌了下来，他脸上微微一红。“他画得实在太糟，简直连画家都称不上。”

“是嘛？”乔治开了腔——这是他头一回说话，而且说得相当不客气。

===Original===

“I am sure George is one of the cleverest artists living,” cried Molly.

“He is not,” thundered Hamilton Beamish. “He is an incompetent amateur.”

“Exactly!” said Mrs. Waddington. “And consequently can never hope to make money.”

Hamilton Beamish’s eyes lit up behind their spectacles.

“Is that your chief objection?” he asked.

===Chinese===

“我敢说，乔治是当世最聪明的画家之一。”莫莉嚷道。

“他才不是呢。”汉密尔顿·比米什声若洪钟。“他是个不称职的业余爱好者。”

“一点不错！”沃丁顿夫人说。“所以也一辈子休想指望挣到钱。”

汉密尔顿·比米什的双眼在镜片后面倏地亮了起来。

“这就是您头一条反对理由？”他问。

===Original===

“Is what my chief objection?”

“That George has no money?”

“But.﻿ ﻿…” began George.

“Shut up!” said Hamilton Beamish. “I ask you, Mrs. Waddington, would you give your consent to this marriage if my friend George Finch were a wealthy man?”

“It is a waste of time to discuss such.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“什么头一条反对理由？”

“乔治没有钱这一条？”

“可是……”乔治刚起头。

“闭嘴！”汉密尔顿·比米什说。“我问您，沃丁顿夫人：倘若我的朋友乔治·芬奇是个有钱人，您肯不肯应下这桩婚事？”

“讨论这种问题纯粹是白白浪费时间……”

===Original===

“Would you?”

“Possibly I would.”

“Then allow me to inform you,” said Hamilton Beamish, triumphantly, “that George Finch is an exceedingly wealthy man. His uncle Thomas, whose entire fortune he inherited two years ago, was Finch, Finch, Finch, Butterfield and Finch, the well-known Corporation Law firm. George, my boy, let me congratulate you. All is well. Mrs. Waddington has withdrawn her objections.”

Mrs. Waddington snorted, but it was the snort of a beaten woman, outgeneralled by a superior intelligence.

===Chinese===

“您肯不肯？”

“兴许肯。”

“那么请容我奉告，”汉密尔顿·比米什洋洋得意地说，“乔治·芬奇是一位极其富有的人。他两年前继承了叔父托马斯的全部遗产；老先生便是芬奇、芬奇、芬奇、巴特菲尔德与芬奇律师事务所（Finch, Finch, Finch, Butterfield and Finch）的东家——那家大名鼎鼎的公司法律师行。乔治，老弟，容我恭喜你。万事大吉。沃丁顿夫人已经撤回了她的反对。”

沃丁顿夫人鼻子里哼了一声，不过那是一个吃了败仗的女人的哼声——她被更高明的头脑用兵法将了一军。

===Original===

“But.﻿ ﻿…”

“No.” Hamilton Beamish raised his hand. “You cannot go back on what you said. You stated in distinct terms that, if George had money, you would consent to the marriage.”

“And, anyway, I don’t know what all the fuss is about,” said Molly. “Because I am going to marry him, no matter what anybody says.”

Mrs. Waddington capitulated.

===Chinese===

“可是……”

“不行。”汉密尔顿·比米什举起一只手。“说过的话，不能翻悔。您方才说得明明白白：只要乔治有钱，您就应允这桩婚事。”

“再说，我也不知道大伙儿吵吵嚷嚷还有什么可争的，”莫莉说。“反正不管谁说什么，这个婚我是结定了。”

沃丁顿夫人缴械了。

===Original===

“Very well! I am nobody, I see. What I say does not matter in the slightest.”

“Mother!” said George reproachfully.

“Mother?” echoed Mrs. Waddington, starting violently.

“Now that everything is so happily settled, of course I regard you in that light.”

“Oh, do you?” said Mrs. Waddington.

“Oh, I do,” said George.

===Chinese===

“好极了！我看出来了，我这个人无足轻重。我说的话半点分量也没有。”

“妈妈！”乔治责备地唤了一声。

“妈妈？”沃丁顿夫人跟着念了一遍，猛地一激灵。

“既然如今万事圆满解决，我自然就把您归到那一类称呼里去了。”

“哦，是吗？”沃丁顿夫人说。

“哦，正是。”乔治说。

===Original===

Mrs. Waddington sniffed unpleasantly.

“I have been overwhelmed and forced into consenting to a marriage of which I strongly disapprove,” she said, “but I may be permitted to say one word. I have a feeling that this wedding will never take place.”

“What do you mean?” said Molly. “Of course it will take place. Why shouldn’t it?”

Mrs. Waddington sniffed again.

===Chinese===

沃丁顿夫人不怀好意地嗤了一声。

“我是被人以势压人，逼着应下了一桩我深恶痛绝的婚事，”她说，“可总该容我说一句话吧。我有一种预感：这场婚礼永远办不成。”

“您这是什么意思？”莫莉说。“婚礼当然办得成。有什么办不成的？”

沃丁顿夫人又嗤了一声。

===Original===

“Mr. Finch,” she said, “though a very incompetent artist, has lived for a considerable time in the heart of Greenwich Village and mingled daily with Bohemians of both sexes and questionable morals.﻿ ﻿…”

“What are you hinting?” demanded Molly.

“I am not hinting,” replied Mrs. Waddington with dignity. “I am saying. And what I am saying is this. Do not come to *me* for sympathy if this Finch of yours turns out to have the sort of moral code which you might expect in one who deliberately and of his own free-will goes and lives near Washington Square. I say again, that I have a presentiment that this marriage will never take place. I had a similar presentiment regarding the wedding of my sister-in-law and a young man named John Porter. I said, ‘I feel that this wedding will never take place.’ And events proved me right. John Porter, at the very moment when he was about to enter the church, was arrested on a charge of bigamy.”

===Chinese===

“芬奇先生，”她说，“虽说是个很不称职的画家，却在格林尼治村的腹心地带住了好长的日子，天天同男女两性、道德可疑的波西米亚分子厮混……”

“您这是在影射什么？”莫莉逼问。

“我不是影射，”沃丁顿夫人端着架子答道，“我是明说。我要说的就是这一句：你这个芬奇，倘若到头来显出那一路的道德水准——一个人存心自愿、自己乐意跑到华盛顿广场（Washington Square）附近去住家，他的德行也就可想而知——你可别来找我讨同情。我再说一遍，我有一种预感：这桩婚事永远办不成。关于我小姑子和一个名叫约翰·波特（John Porter）的年轻人的婚事，我当年有过一模一样的预感。我说：‘我觉得这场婚礼永远办不成。’事实证明了我说得不错。约翰·波特，就在他即将跨进教堂的那一刻，让官方以重婚罪的罪名逮捕了。”

===Original===

George uttered protesting noises.

“But my morals are above reproach.”

“So you say.”

“I assure you that, as far as women are concerned, I can scarcely tell one from another.”

“Precisely,” replied Mrs. Waddington, “what John Porter said when they asked him why he had married six different girls.”

===Chinese===

乔治发出一片抗议的咕哝。

“可我的品行是无懈可击的。”

“那也只是你自说自话。”

“我向你担保，单说女人这一样，我简直分不清这一个和那一个有什么两样。”

“说得好，”沃丁顿夫人答道，“人家问约翰·波特为什么要娶六个不一样的姑娘，他答的正是这一套。”

===Original===

Hamilton Beamish looked at his watch.

“Well, now that everything is satisfactorily settled.﻿ ﻿…”

“For the moment,” said Mrs. Waddington.

“Now that everything is satisfactorily settled,” proceeded Hamilton Beamish, “I will be leaving you. I have to get back and dress. I am speaking at a dinner of the Great Neck Social and Literary Society tonight.”

The silence that followed his departure was followed by a question from Sigsbee H. Waddington.

===Chinese===

汉密尔顿·比米什看了看表。

“好吧，既然一切都圆满解决……”

“暂时解决。”沃丁顿夫人说。

“既然一切都圆满解决，”汉密尔顿·比米什接着说，“我就此告辞。我得回去换礼服。今晚我要在大颈社交文学学会（Great Neck Social and Literary Society）的宴会上演讲。”

他走后是一片静默；静默之后，是西格斯比·H·沃丁顿的一句问话。

===Original===

“Molly, my dear,” said Sigsbee H., “touching on that necklace. Now that this splendid young fellow turns out to be very rich, you will not want to sell it, of course?”

Molly reflected.

“Yes, I think I will. I never liked it much. It’s too showy. I shall sell it and buy something very nice with the money for George. A lot of diamond pins or watches or motorcars or something. And, whenever we look at them we will think of you, daddy dear.”

“Thanks,” said Mr. Waddington huskily. “Thanks.”

“Seldom in my life,” observed Mrs. Waddington, coming abruptly out of the brooding coma into which she had sunk, “have I ever had a stronger presentiment than the one to which I alluded just now.”

===Chinese===

“莫莉，我的宝贝儿，”西格斯比·H说，“说起那条项链。如今这位出色的小伙子原来家财万贯，你自然就不想卖它了吧？”

莫莉想了想。

“卖，我看还是要卖。我一向就不大喜欢它，太扎眼了。我要把它卖掉，拿这笔钱给乔治买些顶好的东西。好多好多钻石别针啦、表啦、汽车啦什么的。往后我们每回看见它们，就会想起你一回，亲爱的老爹。”

“多谢，”沃丁顿先生哑着嗓子说。“多谢。”

“我这辈子，”沃丁顿夫人从方才沉入的沉思昏迷中蓦地醒转过来，开言道，“还很少有哪一回预感，比方才提过的那句更要强烈。”

===Original===

“Oh, mother!” said George.

Hamilton Beamish, gathering up his hat in the hall, became aware that something was pawing at his sleeve. He looked down and perceived Sigsbee H. Waddington.

“Say!” said Sigsbee H. in a hushed undertone. “Say, listen!”

“Is anything the matter?”

“You bet your tortoiseshell-rimmed spectacles something’s the matter,” whispered Sigsbee H. urgently. “Say, listen. Can I have a word with you? I want your advice.”

===Chinese===

“哦，妈妈！”乔治说。

汉密尔顿·比米什在门厅里抓起帽子，忽觉有什么东西在扒拉自己的衣袖。他低头一看，看见了西格斯比·H·沃丁顿。

“喂！”西格斯比·H压着嗓子说。“喂，你听着！”

“出什么事了吗？”

“你拿你那副玳瑁边眼镜打赌，准保出了事，”西格斯比·H急切地耳语。“喂，你听着。能借一步说话吗？我想讨个主意。”

===Original===

“I’m in a hurry.”

“How long will you be before you start out for this Hoboken Clambake of yours?”

“The dinner of the Great Neck Social and Literary Society, to which I imagine you to allude, is at eight o’clock. I shall motor down, leaving my apartment at twenty minutes past seven.”

“Then it’s no good trying to see you tonight. Say, listen. Will you be home tomorrow?”

“Yes.”

“Right!” said Sigsbee H.

===Chinese===

“我赶时间。”

“你还要多久才动身，去赴你们那个霍博肯蛤蜊野餐会？”

“我猜想你指的是大颈社交文学学会的宴会，八点开席。我坐汽车前往，七点二十分从公寓动身。”

“那今晚想见你是没指望了。喂，你听着。明天你在家吗？”

“在。”

“好嘞！”西格斯比·H说。

## VI / 第六章

### I / 一

===Original===

“Say, listen!” said Sigsbee H. Waddington.

“Proceed,” said Hamilton Beamish.

“Say, listen!”

“I am all attention.”

“Say, listen!” said Mr. Waddington.

Hamilton Beamish glanced at his watch impatiently. Even at its normal level of imbecility, the conversation of Sigsbee H. Waddington was apt to jar upon his critical mind, and now, it seemed to him, the other was plumbing depths which even he had never reached before.

===Chinese===

“喂，听着！”西格斯比·H·沃丁顿（Sigsbee H. Waddington）说。

“请讲。”汉密尔顿·比米什（Hamilton Beamish）说。

“喂，听着！”

“我洗耳恭听。”

“喂，听着！”沃丁顿先生说。

汉密尔顿·比米什不耐烦地瞟了一眼手表。即便按西格斯比·H·沃丁顿谈话一贯的愚蠢水准，也够刺痛他那张挑剔的脑子了；而在他看来，眼下这位更是直往一口连他本人也从未探到底的深井里坠。

===Original===

“I can give you seven minutes,” he said. “At the end of that period of time I must leave you. I am speaking at a luncheon of the Young Women Writers of America. You came here, I gather, to make a communication to me. Make it.”

“Say, listen!” said Sigsbee H.

Hamilton Beamish compressed his lips sternly. He had heard parrots with a more intelligent flow of conversation. He was conscious of a strange desire to beat this man over the head with a piece of lead-piping.

“Say, listen!” said Sigsbee H. “I’ve gone and got myself into the devil of a jam.”

“A position of embarrassment?”

“You said it!”

===Chinese===

“我只能给你七分钟。”他说，“时限一到，我就必须离开。全美青年女作家协会（Young Women Writers of America）的午餐会还等我去演讲呢。据我推想，你登门是有事见告。那就请讲。”

“喂，听着！”西格斯比·H说。

汉密尔顿·比米什板起面孔，抿紧了嘴唇。他听过的鹦鹉，谈吐都要比这人流畅而有条理。他意识到自己心里涌起一种古怪的欲望：想抄起一根铅管，敲敲这家伙的脑袋。

“喂，听着！”西格斯比·H说，“我这回可是把自己搅进了一场天大的麻烦。”

“陷入窘境了？”

“让你说着了！”

===Original===

“State nature of same,” said Hamilton Beamish, looking at his watch again.

Mr. Waddington glanced quickly and nervously over his shoulder.

“It’s like this. You heard Molly say yesterday she was going to sell those pearls.”

“I did.”

“Well, say, listen!” said Mr. Waddington, lowering his voice and looking apprehensively about him once more, “They aren’t pearls!”

“What are they, then?”

===Chinese===

“请陈述窘境的性质。”汉密尔顿·比米什说，又看了一眼表。

沃丁顿先生神经质地飞快朝身后扫了一眼。

“是这么回事。昨天莫莉（Molly）说要把那串珍珠卖掉，你是听见了的。”

“听见了。”

“呃，喂，听着！”沃丁顿先生压低嗓门，又一次提心吊胆地四下张望，“那些根本不是珍珠！”

“那是什么？”

===Original===

“Fakes!”

Hamilton Beamish winced.

“You mean imitation stones?”

“That’s just what I do mean. What am I going to do about it?”

“Perfectly simple. Bring an action against the jeweller who sold them to you as genuine.”

“But they were genuine then. You don’t seem to get the position.”

===Chinese===

“假货！”

汉密尔顿·比米什皱了皱眉，缩了一下。

“你是说，仿造的宝石？”

“我就是这个意思。这叫我怎么办才好？”

“再简单不过。把当初把它们当真货卖给你的那家珠宝商告上法庭。”

“可那时候它们还是真货呀。你好像没弄明白这个处境。”

===Original===

“I do not.”

Sigsbee H. Waddington moistened his lips.

“Have you ever heard of the Finer and Better Motion Picture Company of Hollywood, Cal.?”

“Kindly keep to the point. My time is limited.”

“This is the point. Some time ago a guy who said he was a friend of mine tipped me off that this company was a wow.”

“A what?”

===Chinese===

“没有弄明白。”

西格斯比·H·沃丁顿舔了舔嘴唇。

“你可曾听说过加利福尼亚州好莱坞的更精更美电影公司（Finer and Better Motion Picture Company）？”

“请紧扣正题。我的时间是有限的。”

“这正是正题。前些日子，一个自称是我朋友的家伙给我递了个信儿，说这家公司要一炮打响。”

“一炮什么？”

===Original===

“A winner. He said it was going to be big and advised me to come in on the ground floor. The chance of a lifetime, he said it was.”

“Well?”

“Well, I hadn’t any money﻿—not a cent. Still, I didn’t want to miss a good thing like that, so I sat down and thought. I thought and thought and thought. And then suddenly something seemed to say to me ‘Why not?’ That pearl necklace, I mean. There it was, you get me, just sitting and doing nothing and I only needed the money for a few weeks till this Company started to clean up and﻿ ﻿… well, to cut a long story short, I sneaked the necklace away, had the fake stones put in, sold the others, bought the stock, and there I was, so I thought, all hotsy-totsy.”

“All﻿—what?”

“Hotsy-totsy. It seemed to me that I was absolutely hotsy-totsy.”

“And what has caused you to revise this opinion?”

===Chinese===

“打响。他说这公司将来大了去了，劝我趁底价抢先入股。他说这是千载难逢的机会。”

“后来呢？”

“唔，我那时一个子儿也没有——一分钱也没有。可我又舍不得错过那样的好买卖，就坐下来想。想啊，想啊，想啊。后来忽然间，仿佛有什么声音对我说：‘有什么不行的？’我是说那串珍珠项链。它就摆在那儿，你明白吗，成天闲着不干活，而我不过缺这几个星期的钱，等这家公司开始大捞特捞……呃，长话短说，我悄悄把项链偷了出来，请人换上假石头，把真货卖掉，买进股票。于是我就觉得——当时是那么想的——万事齐备，妥妥帖帖了。”

“妥——什么？”

“妥妥帖帖。照我看，当时的我简直是妥妥帖帖到了家。”

“那么，是什么促使你修订了这一看法？”

===Original===

“Why, I met a man the other day who said these shares weren’t worth a bean. I’ve got ’em here. Take a look at them.”

Hamilton Beamish scrutinised the documents with distaste.

“The man was right,” he said. “When you first mentioned the name of the company, it seemed familiar. I now recall why. Mrs. Henrietta Byng Masterson, the president of the Great Neck Social and Literary Society, was speaking to me of it last night. She also had bought shares and mentioned the fact with regret. I should say at a venture that these of yours are worth possibly ten dollars.”

“I gave fifty thousand for them.”

“Then your books will show a loss of forty-nine thousand, nine hundred and ninety. I am sorry.”

“But what am I to do?”

===Chinese===

“唉，前几天我碰上一个人，说这些股票连一颗豆子都不值。股票就在这儿。你给掌掌眼。”

汉密尔顿·比米什满脸嫌恶地审视那沓文书。

“那人说得不错。”他说，“你头一回报出这家公司的名字，我就觉得耳熟。这会儿想起缘由来了。大颈社交文学学会（Great Neck Social and Literary Society）的主席亨丽埃塔·宾·马斯特森夫人（Mrs. Henrietta Byng Masterson）昨晚还同我谈起过它。她也买了股票，言及此事，懊悔不已。要我冒昧估个价，你手里这些，兴许值十美元。”

“我花五万块买的。”

“那么你的账面上将记入四万九千九百九十美元的亏损。我很遗憾。”

“可我该怎么办呢？”

===Original===

“Write it off to experience.”

“But hell’s bells! Don’t you understand? What’s going to happen when Molly tries to sell that necklace and it comes out that it’s a fake?”

Hamilton Beamish shook his head. With most of the ordinary problems of life he was prepared to cope, but this, he frankly admitted, was beyond him.

“My wife’ll murder me.”

“I’m sorry.”

“I came here, thinking that you would be able to suggest something.”

===Chinese===

“就当花钱买教训，一笔勾销。”

“可真是见鬼！你到底明白不明白？等莫莉拿那条项链去卖，人家一瞧是假货，那会招来什么后果？”

汉密尔顿·比米什摇了摇头。人生在世的大多数寻常难题，他都有备而战；但这一桩，他坦率承认，非他力所能及。

“我老婆会宰了我。”

“我很遗憾。”

“我登门来，还当你总能出个主意。”

===Original===

“Short of stealing the necklace and dropping it in the Hudson River, I fear I can think of no solution.”

“You used to be a brainy sort of gink,” said Mr. Waddington reproachfully.

“No human brain could devise a way out of this impasse. You can but wait events and trust to Time the great healer eventually to mend matters.”

“That’s a lot of help.”

Hamilton Beamish shrugged his shoulders. Sigsbee H. Waddington regarded the stock-certificates malevolently.

“If the stuff’s no good,” he said, “what do they want to put all those dollar-signs on the back for? Misleading people! And look at that seal. And all those signatures.”

===Chinese===

“除了把项链偷出来丢进哈得孙河（Hudson River），只怕我想不出别的解救之道。”

“你从前不是个脑瓜顶好使的老兄嘛。”沃丁顿先生埋怨道。

“人脑再灵，也设计不出脱出这个僵局的门路。你唯有静观事态，把一切托付给时间这位伟大的疗愈圣手，盼它终有一日弥缝补合。”

“真是帮了大忙了。”

汉密尔顿·比米什耸了耸肩。西格斯比·H·沃丁顿恶狠狠地瞪着那些股票证书。

“既然这玩意儿不值钱，”他说，“他们干嘛在背面印上那么多美元符号？存心误导人！再瞧那枚大印。还有那一整排签名。”

===Original===

“I am sorry,” said Hamilton Beamish. He moved to the window and leaned out, sniffing the summer air. “What a glorious day.”

“No it isn’t,” said Mr. Waddington.

“Have you ever by any chance met Madame Eulalie, Mrs. Waddington’s palmist?” asked Hamilton Beamish dreamily.

“Darn all palmists!” said Sigsbee H. Waddington. “What am I going to do about this stock?”

“I have already told you that there is nothing that you can do, short of stealing the necklace.”

“There must be something. What would you do if you were me?”

===Chinese===

“我很遗憾。”汉密尔顿·比米什说。他踱到窗前，探出身去，嗅了嗅夏日的空气。“多美好的天儿。”

“美好什么呀。”沃丁顿先生说。

“你可曾有幸结识过尤拉莉夫人（Madame Eulalie），沃丁顿夫人的手相师？”汉密尔顿·比米什恍恍惚惚地问。

“叫手相师们都见鬼去吧！”西格斯比·H·沃丁顿说，“我这批股票到底怎么办？”

“我已经告诉过你了：除了把项链偷出来，你别无出路。”

“总归有个法子。换了是你，你怎么办？”

===Original===

“Run away to Europe.”

“But I can’t run away to Europe. I haven’t any money.”

“Then shoot yourself﻿ ﻿… stand in front of a train﻿ ﻿… anything, anything,” said Hamilton Beamish impatiently. “And now I must really go. Goodbye.”

“Goodbye. Thanks for being such a help.”

“Not at all,” said Hamilton Beamish. “Don’t mention it. I am always delighted to be of any assistance, always.”

He gave a last soulful glance at the photograph on the mantelpiece and left the room. Mr. Waddington could hear him singing an old French love-song as he waited for the elevator, and the sound seemed to set the seal upon his gloom and despair.

===Chinese===

“逃到欧洲去。”

“可我逃不到欧洲去呀。我身上一个钱也没有。”

“那就开枪把自己崩了……往火车跟前一站……怎么都行，怎么都行。”汉密尔顿·比米什不耐烦地说，“这会儿我真的非走不可了。再见。”

“再见。多谢你这么帮我。”

“不必客气。”汉密尔顿·比米什说，“不值一提。我随时乐于效劳，随时。”

他一往情深地最后望了一眼壁炉台上的那张照片，走出了房间。沃丁顿先生听得见，他在候电梯的时候哼着一支法国老情歌；那歌声像是给他满腔的阴郁与绝望盖上了最后一枚封印。

===Original===

“You big stiff!” said Mr. Waddington morosely.

He flung himself into a chair and gave himself up to melancholy meditation. For a while, all he could think of was how much he disliked Hamilton Beamish. There was a man who went about the place pretending to be clever, and yet the moment you came to him with a childishly simple problem which he ought to have been able to solve in half a dozen different ways in five minutes, he could do nothing but say he was sorry and advise a fellow to stand in front of trains and shoot himself. What on earth was the use of trying to be optimistic about a world which contained people like Hamilton Beamish?

And that idiotic suggestion of his about stealing the necklace! How could he possibly﻿ ﻿… ?

Sigsbee H. Waddington sat up in his chair. There was a gleam in his eyes. He snorted. Was it such an idiotic suggestion, after all?

He gazed into the future. At the moment the necklace was in safe custody at the bank, but, if Molly was going to marry this young Pinch, it would presumably be taken from there and placed on exhibition among the other wedding-presents. So that ere long there would undeniably be a time﻿—say, the best part of a day﻿—when a resolute man with a nimble set of fingers might.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“你这根大木头！”沃丁顿先生悻悻然骂道。

他一屁股栽进扶手椅，陷入了忧郁的冥想。有那么一阵，他满脑子只剩一个念头：他有多么厌恶汉密尔顿·比米什。世上有这么一种人，成天到晚装出一副聪明相；可你带着一个连孩童都嫌简单的难题去求他——这等难题，他理应能在五分钟之内用六种不同的法子解开——他却只会连声说遗憾，还劝人家去站到火车跟前、开枪自尽。一个世上既然容得下汉密尔顿·比米什之流的世界，还想对它抱持乐观，究竟图个什么？

还有他那关于偷项链的混账主意！他怎么可能……？

西格斯比·H·沃丁顿在椅子上坐直了身子。眼里闪出一点亮光。他哼了一声。那主意，当真就那么混账吗？

他凝望着未来。眼下项链安然存放在银行里；可只要莫莉嫁给这个姓平奇的小子，它多半就得从那儿取出来，同其余结婚礼物一道陈列示众。如此说来，用不了多久，无可否认会出现那么一段时光——比方说，大半个白天——一个心志坚决、十指灵巧的人未尝不可……

===Original===

Mr. Waddington sank back in his chair again. The light died out of his eyes. Philosophers tell us that no man really knows himself: but Sigsbee H. Waddington knew himself well enough to be aware that he fell short by several miles of the nerve necessary for such an action. Stealing necklaces is no job for an amateur. You cannot suddenly take to it in middle life without any previous preparation. Every successful stealer of necklaces has to undergo a rigorous and intensive training from early boyhood, starting with milk-cans and bags at railway-stations and working his way up. What was needed for this very delicate operation was a seasoned professional.

And there, felt Sigsbee H. Waddington bitterly, you had in a nutshell the thing that made life so difficult to live﻿—the tragic problem of how to put your hand on the right specialist at the exact moment when you required him. All these reference-books like the Classified Telephone Directory omitted the vital trades﻿—the trades whose members were of assistance in the real crisis of life. They told you where to find a Glass Beveller, as if anyone knew what to do with a Glass Beveller when they had got him. They gave you the address of Yeast Producers and Designers of Quilts: but what was the good of a producer of yeast when you wanted someone who would produce a jemmy and break into a house or a designer of quilts when what you required was a man who could design a satisfactory scheme for stealing an imitation-pearl necklace?

Mr. Waddington groaned in sheer bitterness of spirit. The irony of things afflicted him sorely. Every day the papers talked about the Crime Wave: every day a thousand happy crooks were making off in automobiles with a thousand bundles of swag: and yet here he was, in urgent need of one of these crooks, and he didn’t know where to look for him.

A deprecating tap sounded on the door.

“Come in!” shouted Mr. Waddington irritably.

He looked up and perceived about seventy-five inches of bony policeman shambling over the threshold.

===Chinese===

沃丁顿先生又颓然倒回椅背。眼里的光熄灭了。哲学家告诉我们，没有人真正认识自己；可西格斯比·H·沃丁顿对自己的认识已经足够，他知道，干这等勾当所需的胆量，自己还差着好几英里。偷项链绝不是业余爱好者干得了的行当。人到中年，毫无旧日的根底，休想骤然入行。每一个成功的偷项链贼，都得自幼年时代起经受严格而集中的训练，从火车站的奶罐和行李袋偷起，一步一步熬上来。这门精微绝伦的手艺，需要的是一位饱经风霜的职业老手。

西格斯比·H·沃丁顿辛酸地感到，人生之所以这样难以为继，症结正在于此：如何在恰需某位专才的那一刹那，恰恰把他寻到手。诸如分类电话簿一类的种种参考书，偏偏漏掉了要命的行当——那些从业者在人生真正的危机关头用得着的行当。它们告诉你上哪儿找一位玻璃磨边工，倒好像谁把玻璃磨边工弄到手之后，还知道拿他派什么用场似的。它们给你酵母制造商和被面设计师的地址：可当你想要的是一位能变出一根撬棍、撬门入宅的汉子，或者一位能设计出一套万全方案、窃取仿珠项链的被面设计师时，一位酵母制造商又顶什么用？

沃丁顿先生满心苦楚，呻吟了一声。世事的捉弄，叫他痛切难当。报纸上天天谈论着犯罪浪潮：天天都有一千个心满意足的骗子驾着汽车，带着一千包贼赃扬长而去；可偏偏轮到他十万火急需要这样一位骗子的时候，他却不知道上哪儿去找。

门上响起一声怯生生的轻叩。

“进来！”沃丁顿先生没好气地喝道。

他抬头一望，只见约莫七十五英寸高的一副瘦骨嶙峋的警察架子，正拖拖沓沓地跨过门槛。

### II / 二

===Original===

“I beg your pardon, sir, if I seem to intrude,” said the policeman, beginning to recede. “I came to see Mr. Beamish. I should have made an appointment.”

“Hey! Don’t go.” Said Mr. Waddington.

The policeman paused doubtfully at the door.

“But as Mr. Beamish is not at home.﻿ ﻿…”

“Come right in and have a chat. Sit down and take the weight off your feet. My name is Waddington.”

“Mine is Garroway,” replied the officer, bowing courteously.

===Chinese===

“先生，若嫌冒昧搅扰，务请恕罪。”警察说着，身子已开始往外退。“我是来拜会比米什先生的。本该事先约个时间。”

“嘿！别走。”沃丁顿先生说。

警察迟疑不决，在门口停住了脚。

“可是比米什先生不在家……”

“快进来聊聊。坐下歇歇脚，把浑身的分量都卸给椅子。我姓沃丁顿。”

“敝姓加罗威（Garroway）。”警官彬彬有礼地躬身答道。

===Original===

“Pleased to meet you.”

“Happy to meet you, sir.”

“Have a good cigar.”

“I should enjoy it above all things.”

“I wonder where Mr. Beamish keeps them,” said Sigsbee H., rising and routing about the room. “Ah, here we are. Match?”

“I have a match, thank you.”

===Chinese===

“幸会。”

“荣幸之至，先生。”

“来支好雪茄。”

“那真是天大的乐事。”

“不知比米什先生把它们收在哪儿。”西格斯比·H说着站起身，满屋子翻箱倒柜。“啊，在这儿。火柴？”

“我有火柴，谢谢。”

===Original===

“Capital!”

Sigsbee H. Waddington resumed his seat and regarded the other affectionately. An instant before, he had been bemoaning the fact that he did not know where to lay his hands on a crook, and here, sent from heaven, was a man who was probably a walking directory of malefactors.

“I like policemen,” said Mr. Waddington affably.

“That is very gratifying, sir.”

“Always have. Shows how honest I am, ha ha! If I were a crook, I suppose I’d be scared stiff, sitting here talking to you.” Mr. Waddington drew bluffly at his cigar. “I guess you come across a lot of criminals, eh?”

“It is the great drawback to the policeman’s life,” assented Officer Garroway, sighing. “One meets them on all sides. Only last night, when I was searching for a vital adjective, I was called upon to arrest an uncouth person who had been drinking home-brewed hootch. He soaked me on the jaw, and inspiration left me.”

===Chinese===

“好极了！”

西格斯比·H·沃丁顿坐回原位，亲亲热热地端详起对面这位。前一瞬间，他还在哀叹不知上哪儿寻一个骗子；这会儿，一位上天差遣来的人物就坐在眼前——此人八成是一部会走路的歹徒名录。

“我喜欢警察。”沃丁顿先生和和气气地说。

“这很令人欣慰，先生。”

“向来如此。可见我这人多么坦荡，哈哈！我要是个骗子，坐在这儿跟你说话，只怕早吓得浑身僵直了。”沃丁顿先生豪爽地吸着雪茄。“我猜，罪犯你准是常碰上，嗯？”

“这正是警察生涯的一大缺憾。”加罗威警官（Officer Garroway）叹着气表示赞同。“他们无处不在。就在昨夜，我正寻一个性命攸关的形容词，偏偏奉命去拘捕一个灌了家酿私酒的粗坯。他一拳捣在我下巴上，灵感登时弃我而去。”

===Original===

“Wouldn’t that give you a soft-pine finish!” said Mr. Waddington sympathetically. “But what I was referring to was real crooks. Fellows who get into houses and steal pearl necklaces. Ever met any of them?”

“I meet a great number. In pursuance of his duty, a policeman is forced against his will to mix with all sorts of questionable people. It may be that my profession biases me, but I have a hearty dislike for thieves.”

“Still, if there were no thieves, there would be no policemen.”

“Very true, sir.”

“Supply and demand.”

“Precisely.”

===Chinese===

“这一来，岂不是把你那上了漆的上好木料糟蹋成了软松木的收梢！”沃丁顿先生深表同情地说。“不过我指的是真正的骗子。溜门撬锁、偷珍珠项链的那种买卖人。可曾会过几位？”

“我遇过许许多多。履行职务之际，警察身不由己，不得不同各路可疑人物厮混。也许是职业使我存了偏见，但我打心底里厌恶窃贼。”

“话虽如此，可要是没有窃贼，也就不会有警察了。”

“此言极是，先生。”

“供求之道嘛。”

“正是。”

===Original===

Mr. Waddington blew a cloud of smoke.

“I’m kind of interested in crooks,” he said. “I’d like to meet a few.”

“I assure you that you would not find the experience enjoyable,” said Officer Garroway, shaking his head. “They are unpleasant, illiterate men with little or no desire to develop their souls. I make an exception, I should mention, however, in the case of Mr. Mullett, who seemed a nice sort of fellow. I wish I could have seen more of him.”

“Mullett? Who’s he?”

“He is an ex-convict, sir, who works for Mr. Finch in the apartment upstairs.”

“You don’t say! An ex-convict and works for Mr. Finch? What was his line?”

===Chinese===

沃丁顿先生喷出一团烟云。

“我对骗子这一号人物有几分兴趣。”他说，“很想结识那么几位。”

“我向你担保，那样的经历绝不会令人愉快。”加罗威警官摇头说。“他们面目可憎、目不识丁，对于修养灵魂毫无兴致，或兴致寥寥。不过应当说明，马利特先生（Mullett）是个例外，他瞧着是个挺好的老实人。我真希望当初能多同他盘桓盘桓。”

“马利特？他是谁呀？”

“他是个刑满释放人员，先生，在楼上芬奇先生（Mr. Finch）的公寓里帮工。”

“有这样的事！刑满释放人员，还在芬奇先生手下做事？他当年是干哪一行的？”

===Original===

“Inside burglary jobs, sir. I understand, however, that he has reformed and is now a respectable member of society.”

“Still, he was a burglar once?”

“Yes, sir.”

“Well, well!”

There was a silence. Officer Garroway, who was trying to find a good synonym for one of the adjectives in the poem on which he was occupied, stared thoughtfully at the ceiling. Mr. Waddington chewed his cigar intensely.

“Say, listen!” said Mr. Waddington.

===Chinese===

“入室行窃的活儿，先生。不过据我了解，他已然改邪归正，如今是社会上体面的一员。”

“可他毕竟当过窃贼？”

“是的，先生。”

“原来如此，原来如此！”

一阵沉默。加罗威警官正为手头那首诗里的一个形容词寻觅妥帖的同义词，若有所思地仰望着天花板。沃丁顿先生狠命地嚼着他的雪茄。

“喂，听着！”沃丁顿先生说。

===Original===

“Sir?” said the policeman, coming out of his reverie with a start.

“Suppose,” said Mr. Waddington, “suppose, just for the sake of argument, that a wicked person wanted a crook to do a horrible, nefarious job for him, would he have to pay him?”

“Undoubtedly, sir. These men are very mercenary.”

“Pay him much?”

“I imagine a few hundred dollars. It would depend on the magnitude of the crime contemplated, no doubt.”

“A few hundred dollars!”

===Chinese===

“先生？”警察一个激灵，从出神中醒转过来。

“假使，”沃丁顿先生说，“假使——姑且当作打个比方——一个歹人要找一个骗子，替他干一桩可怕而缺德的勾当，他是不是得付钱给那人？”

“毫无疑问，先生。这号人见钱眼开。”

“要付得多吗？”

“我估摸得几百美元。无疑，这要看所谋划罪案的规模大小。”

“几百美元！”

===Original===

“Two, perhaps, or three.”

Silence fell once more. Officer Garroway resumed his inspection of the ceiling. What he wanted was something signifying the aspect of the streets of New York, and he had used “sordid” in line two. “Scabrous!” That was the word. He was rolling it over his tongue when he became aware that his companion was addressing him.

“I beg your pardon, sir?”

Mr. Waddington’s eyes were glittering in a peculiar way. He leaned forward and tapped Officer Garroway on the knee.

“Say, listen! I like your face, Larrabee.”

“My name is Garroway.”

===Chinese===

“两百，兴许，或者三百。”

沉默再度降临。加罗威警官重新仰头勘察天花板。他要找的是一个能点出纽约街市面貌的词儿，第二行里他已经用过“乌烟瘴气”。“龌龊不堪！”就是这个词。他正把这个词在舌尖上滚来滚去，忽然察觉同伴在同他讲话。

“劳驾再说一遍，先生？”

沃丁顿先生的眼睛闪着一种异样的光。他探身向前，在加罗威警官的膝盖上轻轻一拍。

“喂，听着！我喜欢你这张脸，拉拉比。”

“我姓加罗威。”

===Original===

“Never mind about your name. It’s your face I like. Say, listen, do you want to make a pile of money?”

“Yes, sir.”

“Well, I don’t mind telling you that I’ve taken a fancy to you, and I’m going to do something for you that I wouldn’t do for many people. Have you ever heard of the Finer and Better Motion Picture Company of Hollywood, Cal.?”

“No, sir.”

“That’s the wonderful thing,” said Mr. Waddington in a sort of ecstasy. “Nobody’s ever heard of it. It isn’t one of those worn-out propositions like the Famous Players that everybody’s sick and tired of. It’s new. And do you know what I’m going to do? I’m going to let you have a block of stock in it for a quite nominal figure. It would be insulting you to give it to you for nothing, which is what I’d like to do, of course. But it amounts to the same thing. This stock here is worth thousands and thousands of dollars, and you shall have it for three hundred. Have you got three hundred?” asked Mr. Waddington, anxiously.

“Yes, sir, I have that sum, but.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“姓什么不打紧。我喜欢的又不是你的姓，是你的脸。喂，听着，你想不想发一大笔财？”

“想，先生。”

“那好，不瞒你说，我看上你了。我要为你办一件事，换成旁人我可舍不得办。你听说过加利福尼亚州好莱坞的更精更美电影公司吗？”

“没听说过，先生。”

“妙就妙在这儿。”沃丁顿先生说，一副如醉如狂的神气。“谁都没听说过。它可不是名演员公司（Famous Players）那种人人腻歪透了的老掉牙货色。它是新的。你知道我打算干什么吗？我要按一个纯粹象征性的小价钱，让给你一大笔它的股票。白送当然是我的本意，可那样就太失敬了。其实反正是一码事。这股票眼下值千千万万的美元，而你只消三百就能拿下。你手头有三百吗？”沃丁顿先生急切地问。

“有，先生，这笔钱我有，可是……”

===Original===

Mr. Waddington waved his cigar.

“Don’t use that word ‘but’! I know what you’re trying to say. You’re trying to tell me, I’m robbing myself. I know I am, and what of it? What’s money to me? The way I look at it is that, when a man has made his pile, like me, and has got enough to keep his wife and family in luxury, the least he can do as a lover of humanity is to let the rest go to folks who’ll appreciate it. Now you probably need money as much as the rest of them, eh?”

“I certainly do, sir.”

“Then here you are,” said Mr. Waddington, brandishing the bundle of stock-certificates. “This is where you get it. You can take it from me that the Finer and Better Motion Picture Company is the biggest thing since Marconi invented the victrola.”

Officer Garroway took the stock and fondled it thoughtfully.

“It’s certainly very nicely engraved,” he said.

===Chinese===

沃丁顿先生挥了挥雪茄。

“别跟我说那个‘可是’！我知道你想说什么。你想告诉我，我这是自己打劫自己。我知道，那又怎样？钱对我算什么？依我看哪，一个人像我这样发了家，钱财尽够让老婆孩子锦衣玉食，那么，作为一个博爱众生的人，他起码该把余钱让给那些懂得珍惜的人。想必你也同旁人一样亟需用钱，嗯？”

“确实亟需，先生。”

“那就接住，”沃丁顿先生说，把那捆股票证书挥舞得虎虎生风。“你的时运就在这儿。你只管信我：自从马可尼发明留声机以来，天底下最大的买卖，就是这更精更美电影公司。”

加罗威警官接过股票，若有所思地摩挲起来。

“票面雕印得倒确实精致。”他说。

===Original===

“You bet it is! And look at those dollar-signs on the back. Look at that seal. Cast your eye over those signatures. Those mean something. And you know what the motion-pictures are. A bigger industry than the beef business. And the Finer and Better is the greatest proposition of them all. It isn’t like other companies. For one thing, it hasn’t been paying out all its money in dividends.”

“No?”

“No, sir! Not wasted a cent that way.”

“It’s all still there?”

“All still there. And, what’s more, it hasn’t released a single picture.”

“All still there?”

===Chinese===

“那还用说！再瞧瞧背面那些美元符号。瞧瞧那枚大印。把那些签名扫上一眼。那可都有讲究。电影是什么买卖，你心里有数。比牛肉生意还大的一门行业。而更精更美又是其中最了不起的一家。它跟别的公司不一样。单讲一条：它从来没把自己的钱统统当股息分掉。”

“没有分吗？”

“没有，先生！一个子儿也没这么糟蹋过。”

“钱都还在？”

“一个子儿不少。不但这样，它连一部片子都还没发行过。”

“都还在？”

===Original===

“All still there. Lying on the shelves﻿—dozens of them. And then take the matter of overhead expenses﻿—the thing that cripples all these other film-companies. Big studios﻿ ﻿… expensive directors﻿ ﻿… high-salaried stars.﻿ ﻿…”

“All still there?”

“No, sir! That’s the point. They’re not there. The Finer and Better Motion Picture Company hasn’t any of these D. W. Griffiths and Gloria Swansons eating away its capital. It hasn’t even a studio.”

“Not even a studio?”

“No, sir. Nothing but a company. I tell you it’s big!”

Officer Garroway’s mild blue eyes widened.

===Chinese===

“一个子儿不少。齐齐整整躺在片库架子上——一躺几十部。再说说管理开支这档子事——拖垮其余各影片公司的正是它。宏大的制片厂……昂贵的导演……高薪的明星……”

“这些也都还在？”

“没有，先生！妙处就在这儿。压根儿没有。更精更美电影公司手底下，没有一个 D·W·格里菲斯（D. W. Griffiths）、格洛丽亚·斯旺森（Gloria Swanson）之流在蚕食它的资本。它连一间制片厂都没有。”

“连制片厂都没有？”

“没有，先生。除了一家公司，什么都没有。我跟你说，这买卖大发了！”

加罗威警官一双温和的蓝眼睛瞪圆了。

===Original===

“It sounds like the opportunity of a lifetime,” he agreed.

“The opportunity of a dozen lifetimes,” said Mr. Waddington. “And that’s the way to get on in the world﻿—by grabbing your opportunities. Why, what’s Big Ben but a wristwatch that saw its chance and made good?” Mr. Waddington paused. His forehead wrinkled. He snatched the bundle of stock from his companion’s grasp and made a movement towards his pocket. “No!” he said, “No! I can’t do it. I can’t let you have it, after all!”

“Oh, sir!”

“No. It’s too big.”

“Oh, but, Mr. Waddington.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“听着真是一生难逢的机会。”他附和道。

“是十二辈子才碰得上一回的机会。”沃丁顿先生说。“人在世上往上爬，靠的就是这个——一把攥住你的机会。可不是嘛，大本钟算个什么？还不就是一块瞅准了机会、混出名堂的手表？”沃丁顿先生顿了顿。额头皱了起来。他一把从同伴手里夺回那捆股票，作势要往口袋里塞。“不行！”他说，“不行！我不能这么干。说到底，这东西我不能给你！”

“哎，先生！”

“不行。这买卖太大份了。”

“哎，可是，沃丁顿先生……”

===Original===

Sigsbee H. Waddington seemed to come out of a trance. He shook himself and stared at the policeman as if he were saying “Where am I?” He heaved a deep, remorseful sigh.

“Isn’t money the devil!” he said. “Isn’t it terrible the way it saps all a fellow’s principles and good resolutions! Sheer greed, that was what was the matter with me, when I said I wouldn’t let you have this stock. Sheer, grasping greed. Here am I, with millions in the bank, and the first thing you know I’m trying to resist a generous impulse to do a fellow human-being, whose face I like, a kindly act.
