“On the roof. Right! Well, tinkety-tonk, then, for the moment,” said his lordship, and pattered off down the stairs.

===Chinese===
“要是我就近拐到哪个街角，匆匆垫补一口，你看怎么样？”他说。“我是说，谁也说不准这男仆家伙会不会翻脸不认人。替少主人死扛硬顶，我是说。真到那一步，肚里有点儿食，我这个人就能顶两个。只要一盘豆子什么的在肚里稳稳当当搁妥了，我就能尽我那份力。”

沃丁顿夫人轻蔑地打量着他。

“很好。但请务必尽快回来。”

“哎，一定，我以人格担保！只想匆匆忙忙塞一把西梅下去。您还没发觉我走了，我就回来了。”

“我在屋顶上等你。”

“屋顶。好嘞！那么——回见，暂且告个便，”勋爵大人说着，蹬蹬蹬小跑着下楼去了。

===Original===
Mrs. Waddington mounted another flight, and came out under the broad canopy of heaven. She found herself with a choice of views, the glittering city that stretched away below and the dark windows of the Finch lair. She chose the windows and watched them narrowly.

She had been watching them for some considerable time, when suddenly the middle ones, the French windows, lit up. And, as she stepped forward, her rosiest dreams were realised. Across the yellow blind there passed a shadow which was plainly that of a young female person, no doubt of a grade of morality so low that in any other place but Washington Square it would have provoked the raised eyebrow and the sharp intake of the breath. Mrs. Waddington advanced to the window and tapped upon it imperiously.

There was a startled exclamation from within. The blind shot up, revealing a stoutish man in sober black. The next moment the window was opened, and the stoutish man popped his head out.

===Chinese===
沃丁顿夫人又爬了一层，来到广袤的天穹之下。眼前有两种景致任她挑选：脚下铺展开去的灯火璀璨的城市，还有芬奇老巢那些黑黢黢的窗子。她选中了窗子，紧紧盯住不放。

她盯了好一阵子，忽然间，居中那几扇——那几扇落地窗——亮了。她上前一步，最瑰丽的梦想应验了。黄色卷帘上晃过一个影子，分明是个年轻女子的身形，其道德品级想必低到这般地步——倘若不是在华盛顿广场，换任何别的地方，都足以招来扬起的眉毛和倒抽的凉气。沃丁顿夫人逼近窗口，威风凛凛地敲起窗来。

屋里传来一声受惊的喊叫。卷帘唰地升起，露出一个穿素净黑衣的微胖男人。紧接着窗子开了，微胖男人把脑袋探了出来。

===Original===
“Who’s there?” he asked.

“I am,” said Mrs. Waddington.

“Jiminy Christmas!” said the stoutish man.

===Chinese===
“谁在那儿？”他问。

“是我，”沃丁顿夫人说。

“我的圣诞老天爷！”微胖男人说。

### II / 二

===Original===
Frederick Mullett had been in a nervous frame of mind all the afternoon, more nervous even than that of the ordinary bridegroom on his wedding-day. For he had been deeply exercised for many hours past by the problem of what his bride had been up to that afternoon.

Any bridegroom would be upset if his newly-made wife left him immediately after the ceremony on the plea that she had important business to attend to and would see him later. Frederick Mullett was particularly upset. It was not so much the fact that he had planned a golden afternoon of revelry including a visit to Coney Island and had had to forgo it that disturbed him. That the delightful programme should have been cancelled was, of course, a disappointment: but what really caused him mental anguish was the speculation as to what from the viewpoint of a girl like Fanny constituted important business. Her reticence on this vital question had spoiled his whole day.

He was, in short, in exactly the frame of mind when a man who has married a pickpocket and has watched her go off on important business does not want to hear people tapping sharply on windows. If a mouse had crossed the floor at that moment, Frederick Mullett would have suspected it of being a detective in disguise. He peered at Mrs. Waddington with cold horror.

===Chinese===
弗雷德里克·马利特整个下午都心绪紧张，比寻常新郎官在大喜之日的那份紧张还要紧张。因为过去好几个钟头，他一直被一个问题深深煎熬：他那新娘子今天下午到底干什么去了。

哪个新郎若碰上新婚妻子行完礼就借故走开，说是有要紧事待办、回头再见，心里都不会痛快，弗雷德里克·马利特尤其痛快不起来。真正搅得他不得安宁的，倒不是他原计划了一个金子般欢闹的下午，内中还包括康尼岛（Coney Island）一游，如今不得不忍痛割舍。这份美妙节目单横遭取消，固然令人扫兴；可真正叫他精神受罪的，是那个悬而未决的问题：照芬妮这等姑娘的眼光看，什么样的事才算得上要紧大事。她在这个性命攸关的问题上守口如瓶，害得他一整天全毁了。

总而言之，一个刚娶了扒手、又亲眼看着她出门办要紧大事去的男人，眼下这心境，正是最不乐意听见有人急促敲窗的心境。这当口倘若有只老鼠窜过地板，弗雷德里克·马利特都会疑心那是个乔装改扮的侦探。他遍体生寒地瞪着沃丁顿夫人。

===Original===
“What do you want?”

“I wish to see and question the young woman who is in this apartment.”

Mullett’s mouth felt dry. A shiver ran down his spine.

“What young woman?”

“Come, come!”

===Chinese===
“你要干什么？”

“我想见一见、盘问盘问这套公寓里的那位年轻女士。”

马利特只觉口干舌燥。一股寒气顺着脊梁骨直蹿下去。

“什么年轻女士？”

“行了行了！”

===Original===
“There isn’t any young woman here.”

“Tut, tut!”

“There isn’t, I tell you.”

Mrs. Waddington’s direct mind was impatient of this attempt to deceive.

“I will make it worth your while to tell the truth,” she said.

===Chinese===
“这儿根本没有什么年轻女士。”

“啧啧！”

“没有，我跟您说。”

沃丁顿夫人是直来直去的脑子，容不得这等弄虚作假。

“你说了实话，我自有你的好处，”她说。

===Original===
Mullett recoiled. The thought that he was being asked to sell his bride on the very day of their wedding revolted him. Not that he would have sold her at any time, of course, but being asked to do so on this day of all days made the thing seem, as Officer Garroway would have said, so peculiarly stark and poignant.

With a frenzied gesture of abhorrence he slammed the window. He switched off the light and with agonised bounds reached the kitchen, where Mrs. Frederick Mullett was standing at the range stirring a Welsh rarebit.

“Hello, sweetie!” cooed his bride, looking up. “I’m just fixing the rabbit. The soup’s ready.”

“And we’re in it,” said Mullett hollowly.

“Why, whatever do you mean?”

===Chinese===
马利特朝后一缩。居然要他在大喜当天出卖自己的新娘，这念头叫他作呕。话说回来，当然，换哪一天他也不会卖她；可偏偏挑在这一天来开口，这事便显得——用加罗威警官的话说——分外地凄怆，分外地沉痛。

他做了一个深恶痛绝的狂乱手势，砰地摔上窗子。他关掉电灯，一路痛苦万状地蹦跳着冲进厨房；厨房里，弗雷德里克·马利特夫人正站在炉灶前，搅着一锅威尔士干酪吐司（Welsh rarebit）。

“你好呀，甜心！”新娘抬起头，柔声细气地说。“我正在弄那锅兔子呢。汤已经好了。”

“咱们也要一块儿炖在里头了，”马利特声音发空地说。

“哎，你这是什么意思？”

===Original===
“Fanny, where did you go this afternoon?”

“Just down into the country, dearie. I told you.”

“Yes, but you didn’t tell me what you did there.”

“It’s a secret for the present, darling. I want to keep it as a surprise. It’s something to do with some money that’s coming to us.”

Mullett eyed her wanly.

===Chinese===
“芬妮，你今天下午上哪儿去了？”

“就下乡去了一趟，亲爱的。我跟你说过。”

“说过，可你没告诉我你在那儿干了什么。”

“眼下还是个秘密，宝贝儿。我想给你留个惊喜。跟一笔快到手的钱有关系。”

马利特一脸惨白地打量着她。

===Original===
“Fanny, were you doing a job this afternoon down there in the country?”

“Why, Freddy Mullett! What an idea!”

“Then what are the bulls here for?”

“The bulls!”

“There’s a female dick out on the roof right now. And she’s asking for you.”

===Chinese===
“芬妮，你今天下午在那乡下地方，是不是干活儿去了？”

“哎呀，弗雷迪·马利特！亏你想得出来！”

“那这儿来的条子是干什么的？”

“条子！”

“这会儿楼顶上就守着个女密探。还指名道姓要找你。”

===Original===
Fanny stared, round-eyed.

“Asking for me? You’re crazy.”

“She said ‘I wish to see and question the young woman who is in this apartment.’ Those were her very words.”

“I’ll take a peek at her.”

“Don’t let her see you,” begged Mullett, alarmed.

“Is it likely!”

===Chinese===
芬妮瞪圆了眼睛。

“找我？你疯啦。”

“她说：‘我想见一见、盘问盘问这套公寓里的那位年轻女士。’一字不差，就是这么说的。”

“我去瞅她一眼。”

“可千万别让她瞧见你，”马利特惊惶地央告。

“那还用你说！”

===Original===
Fanny walked composedly to the sitting-room. She felt no concern. The most comforting possession in the world is, of course, a quiet conscience: but almost as good is the knowledge that you have left no tracks behind you. Fanny was positive that, on taking her departure from the Waddington home at Hempstead that afternoon, she had made a nice clean getaway and could not possibly have been followed to this place by even the most astute of female dicks. Mullett, she was convinced, must have misunderstood this woman, whoever she might be.

She drew the blind aside an inch and looked cautiously out. The intruder was standing so close to the window that it was possible even in the uncertain light to get an adequate view of her: and what she saw reassured Fanny. She returned to her anxious husband with words of cheer.

“That’s no dick,” she said. “I can tell ’em a mile off.”

“Then who is she?”

===Chinese===
芬妮神色自若地朝起居室走去。她心里踏实得很。世上最叫人安心的财产，当然是问心无愧：可紧随其后的，就是知道自己没留下丝毫踪迹。芬妮满有把握：今天下午从亨普斯特德的沃丁顿公馆脱身时，她走得漂漂亮亮、干干净净，天底下再精明的女密探也不可能跟她跟到这地方来。她深信，不管这女人是谁，马利特准是听岔了。

她把卷帘掀开一英寸，小心翼翼朝外张望。那不速之客站得离窗口极近，即便在昏昧的光线下也足以看清全貌：而所见之物让芬妮放了心。她带着宽心话回到焦灼的丈夫跟前。

“那根本不是密探，”她说。“隔一英里远我都认得出他们来。”

“那她是谁？”

===Original===
“You’d better ask her. Listen, you go and kid her along and I’ll sneak out. Then we can meet somewhere when you’re through. It’s a shame having to waste this nice supper, but we’ll go to a restaurant. Listen, I’ll be waiting for you at the Astor.”

“But if she’s not a dick, why not stay where we are?”

“You don’t want people knowing that I’m here, do you? Suppose your boss heard of it, what would he say?”

“That’s true. All right, then. Wait for me at the Astor. Though it’s kind of a swell place, isn’t it?”

“Well, don’t you want a swell place to dine at on your wedding-night?”

“You’re right.”

===Chinese===
“这个你最好自己问她。听着，你去把她哄住，我悄悄溜出去。等你完事了，咱们找个地方碰头。这么好的一顿晚饭白白糟蹋了，真可惜，不过咱们上饭馆去。听着，我在阿斯特饭店（the Astor）等你。”

“可她既然不是密探，干吗不待在原地？”

“你总不想让人知道我在这儿吧？万一你东家听说了，他会怎么说？”

“这话在理。那好，在阿斯特等我。不过那地方是不是忒气派了点儿？”

“嗐，新婚之夜，难道你不想上一处气派的地方吃饭？”

“你说得对。”

===Original===
“I’m always right,” said Fanny, giving her husband’s cheek a loving pinch. “That’s the first thing you’ve got to get into your head, now you’re a married man.”

Mullett returned to the sitting-room and switched on the light again. He felt fortified. He opened the window with something of an air.

“You were saying, ma’am?”

Mrs. Waddington was annoyed.

“What do you mean by going away and slamming the window in my face?”

===Chinese===
“我一向是对的，”芬妮说着，爱怜地在丈夫脸上拧了一把。“你既然成了有家室的人，这是你头一件得记进脑子的事。”

马利特回到起居室，重新拉亮电灯。他觉得底气足了些。他带着几分派头打开了窗子。

“太太，您方才说什么来着？”

沃丁顿夫人恼了。

“你这是什么意思，转身就走，还当着我的面砰地摔窗子？”

===Original===
“Had to see to something in the kitchen, ma’am. Is there anything I can do for you?”

“There is. I wish to know who the young woman is who is in the apartment.”

“No young woman in this apartment, ma’am.”

Mrs. Waddington began to feel that she was approaching this matter from the wrong angle. She dipped in her bag.

“Here is a ten-dollar bill.”

“Thank you, ma’am.”

===Chinese===
“厨房里有点事非照看不可，太太。有什么能为您效劳的吗？”

“有。我要知道这套公寓里的那位年轻女士是什么人。”

“这套公寓里没有年轻女士，太太。”

沃丁顿夫人开始觉得自己切入这件事的路子不对。她把手伸进提包。

“这里是一张十美元钞票。”

“谢谢您，太太。”

===Original===
“I should like to ask you a few questions.”

“Very good, ma’am.”

“And I shall be obliged if you will answer them truthfully. How long have you been in Mr. Finch’s employment?”

“About a couple of months, ma’am.”

“And what is your opinion of Mr. Finch’s morals?”

===Chinese===
“我想问你几个问题。”

“遵命，太太。”

“倘若您能据实回答，我将不胜感激。您在芬奇先生手下做事多久了？”

“两个月上下，太太。”

“那么您对芬奇先生的道德品行作何评价？”

===Original===
“They’re swell.”

“Nonsense. Don’t attempt to deceive me. Is it not a fact that during your term of employment you have frequently admitted female visitors to this apartment?”

“Only models, ma’am.”

“Models!”

“Mr. Finch is an artist.”

“I am aware of it,” said Mrs. Waddington with a shiver. “So you persist in your statement that Mr. Finch’s mode of life is not irregular?”

===Chinese===
“好得没挑。”

“胡说。休想蒙我。在您当差期间，您是不是屡屡放女性访客进这套公寓？”

“只有模特儿，太太。”

“模特儿！”

“芬奇先生是位画家。”

“这我知道，”沃丁顿夫人说着打了个寒噤。“那么您一口咬定，芬奇先生的生活方式并无不轨？”

===Original===
“Yes, ma’am.”

“Then,” said Mrs. Waddington, twitching the ten-dollar bill neatly from his grasp, “It may interest you to know that I do not believe you.”

“Here, hey!” cried Mullett, deeply moved. “You gave me that!”

“And I have taken it back,” said Mrs. Waddington, replacing the bill in her bag. “You do not deserve it.”

Mullett slammed the window, outraged in his finest feelings. For some moments he stood, fermenting. Then, seething with justifiable indignation, he switched off the light once more and went out.

===Chinese===
“是的，太太。”

“那么，”沃丁顿夫人说着，麻利地把那张十美元钞票从他手里一抽，“告诉您一件事您也许会感兴趣：我不信您的话。”

“哎，喂！”马利特大受震动地嚷道。“那可是您给我的！”

“我又收回来了，”沃丁顿夫人说着把钞票放回提包。“您不配。”

马利特砰地摔上窗子，最精细的感情受了奇耻大辱。他在原地发酵了好一阵。随后，一腔合乎正义的义愤鼎沸起来，他再度关掉电灯，出门而去。

===Original===
He had reached the foot of the stairs, when he heard his name spoken, and, turning, was aware of a long policeman regarding him with a mild friendliness.

“Surely it is Mr. Mullett?” said the policeman.

“Hullo?” said Mullett, somewhat embarrassed. Habit is not easily overcome, and there had been a time when the mere sight of a policeman had made him tremble like a leaf.

“You remember me? My name is Garroway. We met some weeks ago.”

“Why, sure,” said Mullett, relieved. “You’re the poet.”

===Chinese===
他刚下到楼梯口，忽听得有人唤他的名字；一回头，只见一个瘦长的警察正满脸温和友善地望着他。

“这位想必是马利特先生吧？”警察说。

“喂？”马利特应道，有几分局促。积习究竟难改，曾几何时，光是瞧见警察就能叫他抖得像片树叶。

“还记得我吗？我姓加罗威。几个礼拜前咱们见过。”

“哦，当然，”马利特放下心来。“您就是那位诗人。”

===Original===
“It is very nice of you to say so,” said Officer Garroway, simpering a little. “I am about to call at Mr. Beamish’s apartment now with my latest effort. And how has the world been using you, Mr. Mullett?”

“All right. Everything hunky-dory with you?”

“Completely. Well, I must not detain you. No doubt you are on your way to some important appointment.”

“That’s right. Say!” said Mullett, suddenly inspired. “Are you on duty?”

“Not for the moment.”

“But you wouldn’t object to making a cop?”

===Chinese===
“承蒙您这么说，”加罗威警官说着，有点儿忸怩地笑了。“我这正要带着近作去比米什先生府上请教。近来这世道待您可还客气，马利特先生？”

“挺好。您那边万事顺当吧？”

“顺当极了。好了，我不该耽搁您。想必您正赶着赴什么要紧的约。”

“正是。哎，对了！”马利特灵光忽现。“您在当班吗？”

“这会儿不算。”

“那您不会反对执行一回逮捕喽？”

===Original===
“By no means. I am always willing﻿—and, indeed, anxious﻿—to make a cop.”

“Well, there was a suspicious character on our roof just now. A woman. I didn’t like the look of her.”

“Indeed? This is extremely interesting.”

“She was snooping around, looking in at our windows, and I don’t think she’s up to any good. You might go and ask her what she wants.”

“I will attend to the matter immediately.”

===Chinese===
“绝不反对。我随时都愿意——不客气地说，是求之不得——执行一回逮捕。”

“是这么回事，方才我们屋顶上有个可疑人物。一个女人。我瞧着她那副嘴脸就不对劲。”

“哦？这可太有意思了。”

“她鬼鬼祟祟地转悠，往我们窗户里张望，我看准没安好心。您不妨去问问她想干什么。”

“我立即去处理此事。”

===Original===
“If I was you, I’d pinch her on suspicion. So long.”

“Good night, Mr. Mullett.”

Mullett, with the elation which comes from a good deed done, moved buoyantly off to his tryst. Officer Garroway, swinging his nightstick, climbed thoughtfully up the stairs.

===Chinese===
“我要是您，就凭嫌疑先把她抓起来。回见。”

“晚安，马利特先生。”

马利特怀着一桩善举告成后的欢畅，脚步轻快地赴他的幽会去了。加罗威警官甩着警棍，若有所思地拾级而上。

### III / 三

===Original===
Mrs. Waddington, meanwhile, had not been content with a policy of watchful waiting. She was convinced that the shadow which she had seen on the blind had been that of a young woman: and instinct told her that in an apartment near Washington Square where there was a young woman present events were not likely to remain static for any considerable length of time. No doubt the man she had questioned would have warned the young woman of her visit, and by now she had probably gone away. But she would return. And George Finch would return. It was simply a question of exercising patience.

But she must leave the roof. The roof was the first place the guilty pair would examine. If they found it empty, their fears would be lulled. The strategic move indicated was to go downstairs and patrol the street. There she could stay until things began to happen again.

She was about to move away, and had already taken a step towards the door that led to the stairs, when a slight creaking noise attracted her attention and she was surprised to observe the window swinging open.

It opened some six inches: then, caught by a gust of wind, closed again. A moment later, there was another creak and it moved outward once more. Apparently, in the agony of losing his ten dollars, the man had omitted to fasten the catch.

===Chinese===
与此同时，沃丁顿夫人并不满足于静观待变的方针。她确信，方才在卷帘上晃过的影子是个年轻女子；直觉又告诉她，华盛顿广场附近的一套公寓里既然有年轻女子在场，事态就不可能长久保持静止。毫无疑问，她盘问过的那个男人准会向年轻女子通风报信，此刻她多半已经走了。可她还会回来。乔治·芬奇也会回来。剩下的无非是拿出耐性来。

但她必须离开屋顶。屋顶是这对罪人回来后头一处要查看的地方。倘若发现上面空无一人，他们的戒心便会松懈。上策是下楼去，到街上巡游。她可以在那儿守着，直到事情再度发生。

她正要动身，已经朝通往楼梯的那扇门迈出一步，忽然一声轻微的嘎吱引起了她的注意；她诧异地发现那扇窗正缓缓荡开。

窗子开了约莫六英寸：随即一阵风来，又合上了。片刻之后，又是一声嘎吱，它重新向外荡开。看样子，那男人痛失十美元之际，忘了扣上窗闩。

===Original===
Mrs. Waddington stopped. She drew a step nearer. She grasped the handle and, pulling the window wide open, peered into the dark room. It seemed to be empty, but Mrs. Waddington was a cautious woman.

“My man!” she called.

Silence.

“I wish to speak to you.”

===Chinese===
沃丁顿夫人止步。她又近前一步。她抓住窗柄，把窗子拉到大开，朝黑洞洞的屋里窥视。屋里似乎空无一人，可沃丁顿夫人是个谨慎的女人。

“喂，伙计！”她唤道。

没有回应。

“我有话同你讲。”

===Original===
More silence. Mrs. Waddington applied the supreme test.

“I want to return that ten-dollar bill to you.”

Still silence. Mrs. Waddington was convinced. She crossed the threshold and started to feel round the walls for the switch. And, as she did so, something came to her through the throbbing darkness.

It was the smell of soup.

===Chinese===
回应她的唯有更深的沉默。沃丁顿夫人使出了终极一招。

“我想把那张十美元钞票还给你。”

仍是沉默。沃丁顿夫人这才信了。她跨过门槛，开始沿着墙壁摸索电灯开关。就在这时，有什么东西穿过悸动的黑暗向她袭来。

那是汤的气味。

---

===Original===
Mrs. Waddington stiffened like a pointing dog. Although when sitting in the vestibule of the Ritz-Carlton with Lord Hunstanton she had apparently been impervious to the fragrant scents which had so deeply affected his lordship, she was human. It was long past the hour at which she usually dined, and in the matter of sustenance she was a woman of regular habits. Already, while standing on the roof, she had been aware of certain pangs, and now she realised beyond all possibility of doubt that she was hungry. She quivered from head to foot. The smell of that soup seemed to call to the deeps of her being like the voice of an old, old love.

Moving forward like one in a trance, she groped along the wall, and found herself in an open doorway that appeared to lead into a passage. Here, away from the window, the darkness was blacker than ever: but, if she could not see, she could smell, and she needed no other guide than her nose. She walked along the passage, sniffing, and, coming to another open door, found the scent so powerful that she almost reeled. It had become a composite odour now, with a strong Welsh rarebit motif playing through it. Mrs. Waddington felt for the switch, pressed it down, and saw that she was in a kitchen. And there, simmering on the range, was a saucepan.

There are moments when even the most single-minded of women will allow herself to be distracted from the main object of her thoughts. Mrs. Waddington had reached the stage where soup seemed to her the most important﻿—if not the only﻿—thing in life. She removed the lid from the saucepan, and a meaty steaminess touched her like a kiss.

She drew a deep breath. She poured some of the soup into a plate. She found a spoon. She found bread. She found salt. She found pepper.

And it was while she was lovingly sprinkling the pepper that a voice spoke behind her.

“You’re pinched!” said the voice.

===Chinese===
沃丁顿夫人像一头锁定目标的猎犬般僵直了身子。虽说方才坐在里茨-卡尔顿大饭店门厅里、与亨斯坦顿勋爵同处一堂时，那些深深撼动勋爵大人的芬芳气味似乎丝毫没能撼动她，可她终究也是血肉之躯。早已过了她平日进晚餐的钟点，而在吃喝这件大事上，她是个习惯一丝不苟的女人。方才立在屋顶上时，她便已觉出几分饿意的绞痛；此刻她再明白不过：她是饿了。她从头到脚一阵战栗。那汤的气味仿佛一段古老旧爱的声音，直唤到她存在的深处。

她像中了魔一般向前挪动，扶着墙摸索前行，发觉自己来到一扇敞开的门前，门里似乎通着一条过道。这里远离窗口，黑暗比方才更其浓黑：然而她纵然看不见，却闻得到，除了一只鼻子，她不需要任何向导。她一路嗅着走过过道，来到又一扇敞开的门前，只觉香气浓冽，几乎叫她一个趔趄。这气味此刻已交响成曲，其中一支威尔士干酪吐司的主题旋律强劲地贯穿始终。沃丁顿夫人摸到开关，按了下去，看清自己是在一间厨房里。就在那儿，炉灶上煨着的一口深锅正咕嘟作响。

总有些时刻，连心思最专一的女人也会容许自己从念头的正题上分神旁骛。沃丁顿夫人已经到了这般田地：在她眼里，这锅汤即便算不上生活中唯一，也算得上头等重要的事了。她掀开锅盖，一股肉汤的热气拂上面颊，温柔得像一记热吻。

她深深吸了一口气。她舀了些汤倒进盘里。她找到了一把勺。她找到了面包。她找到了盐。她找到了胡椒。

正在她满怀爱意地撒着胡椒的时候，身后响起一个说话声。

“你被逮住了！”那声音说。

### IV / 四

===Original===
There were not many things which could have diverted Mrs. Waddington’s attention at that moment from the plate before her. An earthquake might have done it. So might the explosion of a bomb. This voice accomplished it instantaneously. She spun round with a sharp scream, her heart feeling as if it were performing one of those eccentric South Seas dances whose popularity she had always deplored.

A policeman was standing in the doorway.

“Arrested, I should have said,” added the policeman with a touch of apology. He seemed distressed that in the first excitement of this encounter he had failed to achieve the Word Beautiful.

Mrs. Waddington was not a woman often at a loss for speech, but she could find none now. She stood panting.

===Chinese===
在那个时刻，能让沃丁顿夫人的注意力从眼前盘子上移开的东西并不多。地震兴许能。炸弹爆炸也兴许能。而这个声音顷刻之间办到了。她尖叫一声，猛地转过身去，心脏狂跳得仿佛正在表演南太平洋诸岛那种乖张古怪的舞蹈——那种舞蹈竟能风行于世，她一向痛心疾首。

门口立着一个警察。

“‘逮捕’——我方才该说的是这个词，”警察带着几分歉意补充道。初次交锋的一阵激动里，竟没能成就那个最美的词，他似乎颇以为憾。

沃丁顿夫人不是那种动辄张口结舌的女人，可这会儿她一个字也找不出来。她站在那里，气喘吁吁。

===Original===
“I must ask you, if you will be so good,” said the policeman courteously, “to come along with me. And it will avoid a great deal of unpleasantness if you come quietly.”

The torpor consequent upon the disintegrating shock of this meeting began to leave Mrs. Waddington.

“I can explain!” she cried.

“You will have every opportunity of doing so at the station-house,” said the policeman. “In your own interests I should advise you until then to say as little as possible. For I must warn you that in pursuance of my duty I shall take a memorandum of any statement which you may make. See, I have my notebook and pencil here in readiness.”

===Chinese===
“我得请您，”警察彬彬有礼地说，“行个方便，随我走一趟。您若安静配合，便可省去许多不愉快。”

这场相逢所带来的崩塌性震骇的木然余波，开始从沃丁顿夫人身上退去。

“我可以解释！”她喊道。

“到了警察分局，您有的是机会解释，”警察说。“为您自己着想，我劝您在那之前尽量少说话。因为我必须警告您，为恪尽职守，您所作的任何陈述我都将笔录在案。您瞧，我的笔记本和铅笔已在此恭候。”

===Original===
“I was doing no harm.”

“That, is for the judge to decide. I need scarcely point out that your presence in this apartment is, to say the least, equivocal. You came in through a window﻿—an action which constitutes breaking and entering, and, furthermore, I find you in the act of purloining the property of the owner of the apartment﻿—to wit, soup. I am afraid I must ask you to accompany me.”

Mrs. Waddington started to clasp her hands in a desperate appeal: and, doing so, was aware that some obstacle prevented this gesture.

It was suddenly borne in upon her that she was still holding the pepper-pot. And suddenly a thought came like a full-blown rose, flushing her brow.

“Ha!” she exclaimed.

“I beg your pardon?” said the policeman.

===Chinese===
“我可没干坏事。”

“那个，自有法官定夺。您在这套公寓里的现身，往轻里说也是形迹可疑，这一点几乎无须我多嘴。您从窗户进来——此举已构成破窗侵入；况且，我当场拿获您正在窃取公寓主人的财产——此即：汤。恐怕我不得不请您随我走一趟了。”

沃丁顿夫人正要双手合十，作绝望的哀恳；动作做到一半，却觉察有物作梗，这个手势竟做不下去。

她猛然醒悟，自己手里还攥着那只胡椒瓶。蓦地，一个念头像一朵盛放的玫瑰般袭来，红霞飞上她的眉梢。

“哈！”她喊了一声。

“您说什么？”警察说。

===Original===
Everything in this world, every little experience which we undergo or even merely read about, is intended, philosophers tell us, to teach us something, to help to equip us for the battle of life. It was not, according to this theory, mere accident, therefore, which a few days before had caused Mrs. Waddington to read and subconsciously memorise the report that had appeared in the evening paper to which she subscribed of a burglary at the residence of a certain leading citizen of West Orange, New Jersey. The story had been sent to help her.

Of the less important details of this affair she retained no recollection: but the one salient point in connection with it came back to her now with all the force of an inspiration from above. Cornered by an indignant householder, she recalled, the West Orange burglar had made his escape by the simple means of throwing about two ounces of pepper in the householder’s face.

What this humble, probably uneducated man had been able to achieve was surely not beyond the powers of a woman like herself﻿—the honorary president of twenty-three charitable societies and a well-known lecturer on the upbringing of infants. Turning coyly sideways, she began to unscrew the top of the pot.

===Chinese===
哲人们告诉我们：这世上的一切，我们亲身经历的也好、哪怕仅仅读到过的也罢，每一点小小阅历，都自有其教益，都是为了装备我们去打人生这场仗。照这一理论说来，几天之前沃丁顿夫人在自己订的晚报上读到一则报道——新泽西州西奥兰治某位头面人物的宅邸遭了窃——读罢还下意识地记在了心里，这就绝非偶然。那条新闻正是上天派来搭救她的。

此案的次要细节她一概没有印象；可其中唯一显要的要点，此刻却带着神启般的力量回到了她脑海。她记起来了，那西奥兰治的窃贼被怒不可遏的户主堵住去路时，只使了个简单的法子脱身——把约莫两盎司胡椒扬到了户主脸上。

一个卑微的、多半目不识丁的男人所能办到的事，断不至于就超出一位像她这样的女子的能力——她可是二十三家慈善团体的名誉主席、幼儿教养方面知名的演说家。她忸怩地侧转身去，开始拧那只瓶子的盖。

===Original===
“You will understand,” said the policeman deprecatingly, “that this is extremely unpleasant for me.﻿ ﻿…”

He was perfectly right. Unpleasant, he realised a moment later, was the exact adjective which the most punctilious stylist would have chosen. For suddenly the universe seemed to dissolve in one great cloudburst of pepper. Pepper tickled his mouth: pepper filled his nose: pepper strayed into his eyes and caressed his Adam’s apple. For an instant he writhed blindly: then, clutching at the table for support, he began to sneeze.

With the sound of those titanic sneezes ringing in her ears, Mrs. Waddington bumped her way through the darkness till she came to the open window: then, galloping across the roof, hurled herself down the fire-escape.

===Chinese===
“还望您体谅，”警察不胜歉然地说，“这实在叫我难堪之至。……”

他说得千真万确。片刻之后他便省悟，纵然是最吹毛求疵的文体家，能挑中的形容词也无非“难堪”二字。因为刹那间，整个宇宙仿佛消融在一场倾盆而下的胡椒暴雨里。胡椒撩拨他的嘴：胡椒灌满他的鼻子：胡椒窜进他的眼睛，还轻抚他的喉结。有那么一瞬，他两眼昏花地乱扭：随后，他一把抓住桌子撑住身子，打起了喷嚏。

趁着那惊天动地的喷嚏声犹在耳畔，沃丁顿夫人跌跌撞撞地摸黑走到敞开的窗前：随即飞奔着掠过屋顶，纵身扑下防火梯。

### V / 五

===Original===
The only thing in the nature of a policy or plan of action which Mrs. Waddington had had when making for the fire-escape had been a general desire to be as far away as possible from the representative of the Law when he stopped sneezing and opened his eyes and began to look around him for his assailant. But, as her feet touched the first rungs, more definite schemes began to shape themselves. Fire-escapes, she knew, led, if followed long enough, to the ground: and she decided to climb to safety down this one. It was only when she had descended as far as the ninth floor that, glancing below her, she discovered that this particular fire-escape terminated not, as she had supposed, in some back-alley, but in the gaily-lighted outdoor premises of a restaurant, half the tables of which were already filled.

This sight gave her pause. In fact, to be accurate, it froze her stiff. Nor was her agitation without reason. Those of the readers of this chronicle who have ever thrown pepper in a policeman’s face, and skimmed away down a fire-escape are aware that fire-escapes, considered as a refuge, have the defect of being uncomfortably exposed to view. At any moment, felt Mrs. Waddington, the policeman might come to the edge of the roof and look down: and to deceive him into supposing that she was merely an ashcan or a milk-bottle was, she knew, beyond her histrionic powers.

The instinct for self-preservation not only sharpens the wits, but at the same time dulls the moral sensibility. It was so with Mrs. Waddington now. Her quickened intelligence perceived in a flash that if she climbed in through the window outside which she was now standing she would be safe from scrutiny: and her blunted moral sense refused to consider the fact that such an action﻿—amounting, as it did, to what her policeman playmate had called breaking and entering﻿—would be most reprehensible. Besides, she had broken and entered one apartment already that night, and the appetite grows by what it feeds on. Some ten seconds later, therefore, Mrs. Waddington was once more groping through the darkness of somebody else’s dwelling-place.

===Chinese===
沃丁顿夫人扑向防火梯之际，若说有什么方针或行动计划，那便只是一条笼统的愿望：等那位法律的代表停止喷嚏、睁开眼睛、开始环顾四周搜寻袭击者的时候，自己离他越远越好。

她知道，防火梯这东西，只要一路走下去，终究能走到地面：于是她决定顺着这架梯子下到安全地带。直到下到第九层，她朝脚下一望，才发现这架防火梯的终点并不是她以为的某条后巷，而是一家餐馆灯火通明的露天场子，半数的桌子已经客满。

这景象叫她踌躇了。说得准确些，是把她冻僵在原地。她的惊惶也并非没有道理。本编年史的读者诸君之中，但凡曾把胡椒扬上警察的脸、再顺着防火梯飘然遁走者，都明白防火梯这种东西，若论藏身之处，有个毛病：暴露在众目睽睽之下，叫人如芒在背。沃丁顿夫人暗想，那警察随时可能走到屋顶边缘向下张望；而要骗得他以为自己不过是一只垃圾桶或一瓶牛奶，她自知，凭她那点演技是万万办不到的。

===Original===
A well-defined scent of grease, damp towels and old cabbages told her that the room through which she was creeping was a kitchen: but the blackness was so uniform that she could see nothing of her surroundings. The only thing she was able to say definitely of this kitchen at the moment was that it contained a broom. This she knew because she had just stepped on the end of it and the handle had shot up and struck her very painfully on the forehead.

“Ouch!” cried Mrs. Waddington.

She had not intended to express any verbal comment on the incident, for those who creep at night through other people’s kitchens must be silent and wary: but the sudden agony was so keen that she could not refrain from comment. And to her horror she found that her cry had been heard. There came through the darkness a curious noise like the drawing of a cork, and then somebody spoke.

“Who are you?” said an unpleasant, guttural voice.

===Chinese===
自保的本能非但能使头脑敏锐，同时也会让道德感变得迟钝。此刻的沃丁顿夫人正是如此。她那灵敏起来的头脑电光石火般看透：只要从她此刻站着的那扇窗外爬进去，便可免受审视之苦；而她那迟钝下去的道德感，则拒绝去想这样一个事实：此举既已够上她那位警察玩伴口中的“破窗侵入”，实在应当受到最严厉的责备。何况今天夜里她已经破窗闯过一套公寓了，而这类胃口，向来是越吃越大的。于是，不过十来秒钟之后，沃丁顿夫人再度在别人宅邸的黑暗中摸索起来了。

一股油污、潮抹布和陈年圆白菜浓烈分明的气味告诉她，她正匍匐其中的这间屋子是间厨房：可黑暗均匀而纯粹，四周的物件她什么也看不见。此刻关于这间厨房，她唯一能够确切断言的，就是屋里有一把扫帚。这一点她之所以知道，是因为她刚刚踩上了帚梢，帚柄倏地弹起，结结实实敲在她脑门上，疼得钻心。

“哎哟！”沃丁顿夫人叫出了声。

她原不打算对这桩意外发表任何口头评论，因为深更半夜匍匐穿行于别人厨房的人，务须噤声戒备：可那猝然的剧痛太过尖利，她实在按捺不住。更叫她惊骇的是，她发现这声叫喊竟有了听众。黑暗中传来一阵怪响，好似拔瓶塞一般，接着有谁开了口。

===Original===
Mrs. Waddington stopped, paralysed. She would not, in the circumstances, have heard with any real pleasure the most musical of speech: but a soft, sympathetic utterance would undoubtedly have afflicted her with a shade less of anguish and alarm. This voice was the voice of one without human pity; a grating, malevolent voice; a voice that set Mrs. Waddington thinking quiveringly in headlines:

“**Society Leader Found Slain in Kitchen.**”

“Who are you?”

“**Body Dismembered Beneath Sink.**”

===Chinese===
“你是谁？”一个令人不快的、粗嘎的嗓音说。

沃丁顿夫人钉在原地，动弹不得。处在此情此景，纵然是世间最悦耳的言语，她听着也决计谈不上愉快；不过一句轻柔的、满怀抚慰意味的话，无疑会让她的痛苦与惊惶稍稍减淡一分。而这嗓音里没有半点人间恻隐；它粗粝而刻毒；它叫沃丁顿夫人浑身发颤，脑子里打出的尽是新闻标题：

“**社交名媛陈尸厨房。**”

“你是谁？”

===Original===
“Who are you?”

“**Severed Head Leads Trackers to Death-Spot.**”

“Who are you?”

Mrs. Waddington gulped.

“I am Mrs. Sigsbee H. Waddington,” she faltered. And it would have amazed Sigsbee H., had he heard her, to discover that it was possible for her to speak with such a winning meekness.

===Chinese===
“**尸身水槽之下遭肢解。**”

“你是谁？”

“**断首引路，追踪者直抵毙命之地。**”

“你是谁？”

沃丁顿夫人喉咙里咯噔一声。

===Original===
“Who are you?”

“Mrs. Sigsbee H. Waddington, of East Seventy-Ninth Street and Hempstead, Long Island. I must apologise for the apparent strangeness of my conduct in.﻿ ﻿…”

“Who are you?”

Annoyance began to compete with Mrs. Waddington’s terror. Deaf persons had always irritated her, for like so many women of an impatient and masterful turn of mind she was of opinion that they could hear perfectly well if they took the trouble. She raised her voice and answered with a certain stiffness.

===Chinese===
“我是西格斯比·H·沃丁顿夫人，”她结结巴巴地说。西格斯比·H倘若听见，定会大为惊异：原来她也能用这般温婉驯顺的腔调说话。

“西格斯比·H·沃丁顿夫人，宅邸在东七十九街，还有长岛的亨普斯特德。我方才的行止显然有失常态，对此我不能不道歉，我……”

“你是谁？”

恼意开始同恐惧分庭抗礼。聋子向来招她心烦，因为正如许许多多性子急躁、飞扬跋扈的女人一样，她认定这类人只要肯费心，听得一清二楚。她提高嗓门，带着几分生硬答道：

===Original===
“I have already informed you that I am Mrs. Sigsbee H. Waddington.﻿ ﻿…”

“Have a nut,” said the voice, changing the subject.

Mrs. Waddington’s teeth came together with a sharp click. All the other emotions which had been afflicting her passed abruptly away, to be succeeded by a cold fury. Few things are more mortifying to a proud woman than the discovery that she had been wasting her time being respectful to a parrot: and only her inability to locate the bird in the surrounding blackness prevented a rather unpleasant brawl. Had she been able to come to grips with it, Mrs. Waddington at that moment would undoubtedly have done the parrot no good whatever.

“Brrh!” she exclaimed, expressing her indignation as effectively as was possible by mere speech: and, ignoring the other’s request﻿—in the circumstances, ill-timed and tasteless﻿—that she should stop and scratch its head, she pushed forward in search of the door.

Reaction had left her almost calm. The trepidation of a few moments back had vanished; and she advanced now in a brisk and businesslike way. She found the door and opened it. There was more darkness beyond, but an uncurtained window gave sufficient light for her to see that she was in a sitting-room. Across one corner of this room lay a high-backed chesterfield. In another corner stood a pedestal desk. And about the soft carpet there were distributed easy chairs in any one of which Mrs. Waddington, had the conditions been different, would have been delighted to sit and rest.

===Chinese===
“我已经告知过您，我是西格斯比·H·沃丁顿夫人。……”

“吃颗坚果吧，”那嗓音说，岔开了话头。

沃丁顿夫人的牙齿咔的一声咬到了一处。方才折磨着她的种种情绪霎时间烟消云散，继之而起的是一股冰冷的狂怒。对一个骄傲的女人而言，很少有比发现自己竟枉费工夫、对一只鹦鹉毕恭毕敬更难堪的事了：只因四下漆黑，寻不见那扁毛畜生的踪影，否则一场颇为难看的恶战势所难免。倘若能揪住它贴身肉搏，此刻的沃丁顿夫人断然不会叫那鹦鹉有任何好下场。

“哼！”她哼了一声，把满腔义愤用语言所能及的最大效力宣泄了出来；至于对方那口头上的请求——请她停下脚步、给它挠挠头——此情此景之下，既不合时宜，又不合体统——她置之不理，径自摸索着找门去了。

后怕退去，她几乎平静了下来。方才那阵心惊肉跳已无影无踪；此刻她迈着干脆利落、公事公办的步子。她摸到了门，把门打开。门外是更深的黑暗，但一扇没挂帘子的窗户透进的光，已足够让她看清自己进了一间起居室。屋子一角横陈着一张高背切斯特菲尔德沙发。另一角立着一张独脚写字台。柔软的地毯上散布着几张安乐椅——倘若时势不同，随便哪一张，沃丁顿夫人都会乐于坐上去歇歇。

===Original===
But, though she had been on her feet some considerable time now and was not a woman who enjoyed standing, prudence warned her that the temptation to relax must be resisted. It was a moment for action, not repose. She turned to the door which presumably led into the front hall and thence to the stairs and safety: and had just opened it when there came the click of a turning key.

Mrs. Waddington acted swiftly. The strange calm which had been upon her dissolved into a panic fear. She darted back into the sitting-room: and, taking the chesterfield in an inspired bound, sank down behind it and tried not to snort.

“Been waiting long?” asked some person unseen, switching on the light and addressing an invisible companion.

The voice was strange to Mrs. Waddington: but about the one that replied to it there was something so fruitily familiar that she stiffened where she lay, scarcely able to credit her senses. For it was the voice of Ferris, her butler. And Ferris, if the truth was in him, should by now have been at the sickbed of a relative.

===Chinese===
然而，尽管她已经站了老大一会儿，又素来不是甘愿罚站的女人，谨慎还是告诫她：松懈的诱惑必须顶住。眼下是行动的当口，不是休憩的辰光。她转向那扇想必通往前厅、由此再通往楼梯、通往安全的门：刚一拉开，便听见钥匙转动的咔嗒声。

沃丁顿夫人当机立断。笼罩着她的那阵异样的平静，霎时化作没顶的惊慌。她飞身蹿回起居室：一个神来之笔的腾跃掠过切斯特菲尔德沙发，在沙发后头伏低身子，拼命忍住不喷鼻息。

“等了很久吧？”一个看不见其人的什么人说着，拉亮电灯，向一位看不见的同伴发问。

这嗓音沃丁顿夫人闻所未闻：可应答的那一个，却有一种果子熟透般的熟稔味道，她伏在原地浑身僵直，几乎不敢相信自己的耳朵。因为那是费里斯的声音，她的男管家。而费里斯倘若句句属实，此刻本该守在一位亲戚的病榻之前。

===Original===
“Some little time, sir, but it has caused me no inconvenience.”

“What did you want to see me about?”

“I am addressing Mr. Lancelot Biffen, the editor-in-chief of *Town Gossip*?”

“Yes. Talk quick. I’ve got to go out again in a minute.”

“I understand, Mr. Biffen, that *Town Gossip* is glad to receive and pay a substantial remuneration for items of interest concerning those prominent in New York Society. I have such an item.”

===Chinese===
“有些时候了，先生，不过并未给我造成什么不便。”

“你找我有什么事？”

“我此刻讲话的对象，是《街谈巷议》（Town Gossip）主笔兰斯洛特·比芬（Lancelot Biffen）先生本人？”

“正是。快说。过一分钟我还得出门。”

“据我所知，比芬先生，《街谈巷议》乐于收纳纽约社交名流的趣闻轶事，并致以丰厚稿酬。我手上正有这么一条。”

===Original===
“Who’s it about?”

“My employer﻿—Mrs. Sigsbee H. Waddington, sir.”

“What’s she been doing?”

“It is a long story.﻿ ﻿…”

“Then I haven’t time to listen to it.”

===Chinese===
“说的是谁？”

“我的女东家——西格斯比·H·沃丁顿夫人，先生。”

“她干什么了？”

“说来话长。……”

“那我没工夫听。”

===Original===
“It concerns the sensational interruption to the marriage of Mrs. Waddington’s stepdaughter.﻿ ﻿…”

“Didn’t the wedding come off, then?”

“No, sir. And the circumstances which prevented it.﻿ ﻿…”

Mr. Biffen uttered an exclamation. He had apparently looked at his watch and been dismayed by the flight of time.

“I must run,” he said. “I’ve a date at the Algonquin in a quarter of an hour. Come and talk to me at the office tomorrow.”

===Chinese===
“事关沃丁顿夫人继女婚典横遭打断的那桩轰动事件。……”

“这么说婚礼没办成？”

“没有，先生。而那场婚事何以未成，其中种种情由。……”

比芬先生惊呼了一声。看样子他瞄了眼手表，光阴飞逝叫他大惊失色。

“我得跑了，”他说。“一刻钟后我在阿尔冈金饭店（the Algonquin）有个约。明天到办公室来找我谈。”

===Original===
“I fear that will be impossible, sir, owing to.﻿ ﻿…”

“Then, see here. Have you ever done any writing?”

“Yes, sir. At Little-Seeping-in-the-Wold I frequently contributed short articles to the parish magazine. The vicar spoke highly of them.”

“Then sit down and write the thing out. Use your own words and I’ll polish it up later. I’ll be back in an hour, if you want to wait.”

“Very good, sir. And the remuneration?”

===Chinese===
“恐怕这办不到，先生，因为……”

“那这么着。你写过东西没有？”

“写过，先生。在利特尔-西平-因-沃尔德，我时常给教区杂志投些短文。牧师对它们评价极高。”

“那就坐下来，把这事原原本本写出来。用你自己的话，回头我来润色。一小时后回来，你要愿意等就等着。”

“遵命，先生。那么稿酬呢？”

===Original===
“We’ll talk about that later.”

“Very good, sir.”

Mr. Biffen left the room. There followed a confused noise﻿—apparently from his bedroom, in which he seemed to be searching for something. Then the front door slammed, and quiet descended upon the apartment.

===Chinese===
“那个回头再谈。”

“遵命，先生。”

比芬先生出了房。随之而来的是一阵杂沓的声响——似乎出自他的卧室，他像是在里头翻寻什么东西。接着前门砰然关上，整套公寓沉入了寂静。

---

===Original===
Mrs. Waddington continued to crouch behind her chesterfield. There had been a moment, immediately after the departure of Mr. Biffen, when she had half risen with the intention of confronting her traitorous butler and informing him that he had ceased to be in her employment. But second thoughts had held her back. Gratifying as it would undoubtedly be to pop her head up over the back of the sofa and watch the man cower beneath her eye, the situation, she realised, was too complicated to permit such a procedure. She remained where she was, and whiled away the time by trying out methods to relieve the cramp from which her lower limbs had already begun to suffer.

From the direction of the desk came the soft scratching of pen on paper. Ferris was plainly making quite a job of it, putting all his energies into his task. He seemed to be one of those writers, like Flaubert, who spared no pains in the quest for perfect clarity and are prepared to correct and re-correct indefinitely till their artist-souls are satisfied. It seemed to Mrs. Waddington as though her vigil was to go on forever.

But in a bustling city like New York it is rarely that the artist is permitted to concentrate for long without interruption. A telephone-bell broke raspingly upon the stillness: and the first sensation of pleasure which Mrs. Waddington had experienced for a very long time came to her as she realised that the instrument was ringing in the passage outside and not in the room. With something of the wild joy which reprieved prisoners feel at the announcement of release she heard the butler rise. And presently there came from a distance his measured voice informing some unseen inquirer that Mr. Biffen was not at home.

Mrs. Waddington rose from her form. She had about twenty seconds in which to act, and she wasted none of them. By the time Ferris had returned and was once more engrossed in his literary composition, she was in the kitchen.

She stood by the window, looking out at the fire-escape. Surely by this time, she felt, it would be safe to climb once more up to the roof. She decided to count three hundred very slowly and risk it.

===Chinese===
沃丁顿夫人继续在切斯特菲尔德沙发后面蹲伏着。比芬先生前脚刚走那阵子，她一度半抬起身，打算挺身而出，当面痛斥她的叛徒管家，通知他从此不再是她的雇员。可转念一想，又把她按住了。从沙发靠背顶上倏地探出脑袋，看那家伙在她的目光下瑟瑟发抖，这自然痛快无比；然而她明白，眼下的局面太过复杂，容不得这一手。她伏在原地不动，靠试验各种缓解抽筋的法子消磨时光——她的下肢已经开始领受这份罪了。

写字台那边传来笔尖划纸的沙沙轻响。费里斯分明把这事当作一项大工程，全部心力都扑在了创作上。他像是那种作家——福楼拜（Flaubert）一流——为求得澄澈无瑕的明晰不惜呕心沥血，改了又改，无休无止，不叫他们那艺术家灵魂称心决不罢休。在沃丁顿夫人看来，这场守夜简直要守到地老天荒。

然而在纽约这样一座闹哄哄的城市，艺术家想要长久凝神而不受搅扰，向来是难上加难。一阵电话铃刺耳地划破沉寂：而沃丁顿夫人许久以来的第一缕欢悦涌了上来——她发觉那铃是在外头的过道里响，并不在这间屋里。她听见管家起身，心头涌起一股狂喜，恍若获得缓刑的死囚听见获释的宣告。不多时，远处传来他那四平八稳的嗓音，告知某位看不见的来问者：比芬先生不在家。

沃丁顿夫人从蹲伏处立起身来。她约有二十秒钟可供行动，而她一秒也不曾浪费。等费里斯回到屋里、重新埋头于他的文学创作时，她已经在厨房里了。

她立在窗边，望着外头的防火梯。她想，到了这会儿，再爬回屋顶上去，总该安全了吧。她打定主意极慢极慢地数上三百个数，然后冒险一试。

## XV / 第十五章

===Original===

Molly and Sigsbee Horatio, the latter muttering “Gallagher! Gallagher! Gallagher!” to himself in order that the magic name should not again escape him, had started out in the two-seater about a quarter of an hour after the departure of Mrs. Waddington’s Hispano-Suiza. Halfway to New York, however, a blowout had arrested their progress: and the inability of Sigsbee H. to make a quick job of fixing the spare wheel had further delayed them. It was not, therefore, till almost at the exact moment when Mrs. Waddington was committing the rash act which had so discomposed Officer Garroway, that Molly, having dropped her father at Police Headquarters, arrived at the entrance of the Sheridan.

She hurried up the stairs and rang George’s front-door bell. For awhile it seemed as if her ringing was to meet with no response: then, after some minutes, footsteps made themselves heard coming along the passage. The door opened, and Molly found herself gazing into the inflamed eyes of a policeman.

She looked at him with surprise. She had never seen him before, and she rather felt that she would have preferred not to see him now: for he was far from being a pleasing sight. His nose, ears and eyes were a vivid red: and his straggling hair dripped wetly on to the floor. With the object of diminishing the agony caused by the pepper, Officer Garroway had for some time been holding his head under the tap in the kitchen: and he now looked exactly like the body which had been found after several days in the river. The one small point that differentiated him from a corpse was the fact that he was sneezing.

===Chinese===

莫莉和西格斯比·霍雷肖——后者嘴里不住地咕哝着“加拉格尔（Gallagher）！加拉格尔！加拉格尔！”，惟恐这个神奇的名字再一次从舌尖溜掉——在沃丁顿夫人的西班牙-苏萨开出大约一刻钟之后，也乘着那辆双座小汽车上了路。然而半道上，一只轮胎砰地爆了，拦住了他们的行程：而西格斯比·H 手脚迟钝，一时换不好备用车轮，又叫他们耽搁了更多工夫。于是，直到几乎恰好是沃丁顿夫人干下那桩搅得加罗威警官好不狼狈的鲁莽勾当的时刻，莫莉才把父亲送到警察总局，赶到了谢里丹公寓楼的门口。

她急匆匆奔上楼梯，按响了乔治家的前门门铃。有一阵子，铃声仿佛注定无人应答：过了几分钟，才听见走廊那头传来了脚步声。门开了，莫莉发现自己凝视进了一双红肿发炎的眼睛——一名警察的眼睛。

她惊讶地打量着他。这个人她从前从未见过，而且她颇觉得自己情愿眼下也别看见他：因为他这副模样实在说不上悦目。他的鼻子、耳朵和双眼红得刺眼，蓬乱的头发湿淋淋地往地板上滴水。为了减轻胡椒烧出的剧痛，加罗威警官已经把脑袋按在厨房的水龙头底下冲了好一阵子：此刻他看上去，活脱脱就是从河里泡了好几天才捞起来的那具尸首。他与死尸之间仅有的一点小小分别，在于他正在打喷嚏。

===Original===

“What are you doing here?” exclaimed Molly.

“Achoo!” replied Officer Garroway.

“What?” said Molly.

The policeman, with a nobility which should have earned him promotion, checked another sneeze.

“There has been an outrage,” he said.

===Chinese===

“你在这儿干什么？”莫莉失声叫道。

“啊嚏！”加罗威警官答道。

“什么？”莫莉说。

那警察强忍住又一个喷嚏，那份高尚的情操，本该给他挣来一场升职。

“这里发生了一桩暴行，”他说。

===Original===

“Mr. Finch has not been hurt?” cried Molly, alarmed.

“Mr. Finch hasn’t. I have.”

“Who are you?”

“My name is Gar-hosh-hoosh-hish.”

“What?”

“Gar-ish-wash-*wush*﻿ ﻿… Garroway,” said the policeman, becoming calmer.

===Chinese===

“芬奇先生没受伤吧？”莫莉惊惶地嚷道。

“芬奇先生没受伤。我受了。”

“您是哪一位？”

“我名叫加——嚯什——嚯什——嚯什。”

“什么？”

“加——伊什——沃什——*呜什*……加罗威，”警察说着，渐渐平静下来。

===Original===

“Where is Mr. Finch?”

“I could not say, miss.”

“Have you a cold?”

“No, miss, not a ker-osh-wosh-osh. A woman threw pepper in my face.”

“You ought not to know such women,” said Molly severely.

===Chinese===

“芬奇先生在哪儿？”

“这个我说不上来，小姐。”

“您是伤风了吗？”

“没有，小姐，不是伤——咳——哦什——沃什——哦什。有个女人朝我脸上撒了一把胡椒粉。”

“您本不该认识这种女人的。”莫莉正颜厉色地说。

===Original===

The injustice of this stung Officer Garroway.

“I did not know her socially. I was arresting her.”

“Oh, I see.”

“I found her burgling this apartment.”

===Chinese===

这份不白之冤刺痛了加罗威警官。

“我同她并无私交。我当时正在逮捕她。”

“哦，原来如此。”

“我撞见她正在这所公寓里行窃。”

===Original===

“Good gracious!”

“And when I informed her that I was compelled to take her into custody, she threw pepper in my face and escaped.”

“You poor man!”

“Thank you, miss,” said Officer Garroway gratefully. A man can do with a bit of sympathy on these occasions, nor is such sympathy rendered less agreeable by the fact that the one who offers it is young and charming and gazes at you with large, melting blue eyes. It was at this point that Officer Garroway began for the first time to be aware of a distinct improvement in his condition.

===Chinese===

“天哪！”

“而当我通告她说，我不得不将她收押归案的时候，她就朝我脸上撒了一把胡椒，逃之夭夭了。”

“您这可怜的人儿！”

“多谢您，小姐。”加罗威警官感激地说。男人在这种场合，总还消受得起一点儿同情；何况施与同情的人又年轻又可爱，一双水汪汪的大蓝眼睛脉脉含情地望着你，这就叫那份同情愈发地受用了。正是打这一刻起，加罗威警官头一回觉察到，自己的状况有了明显的好转。

===Original===

“Can I get you anything?” said Molly.

Officer Garroway shook his head wistfully.

“It’s against the law, miss, now. In fact, I am to be one of a posse this very night that is to raid a restaurant which supplies the stuff.”

“I meant something from a drugstore. Some ointment or something.”

===Chinese===

“我能给您弄点儿什么吗？”莫莉说。

加罗威警官怅怅地摇了摇头。

“如今这可是犯法的了，小姐。不瞒您说，就在今天夜里，我还要充当一支查缉队（posse）里的一员，去突击搜查一家供应这种货色的餐馆呢。”

“我是说上药房买点儿什么。药膏啦什么的。”

===Original===

“It is extremely kind of you, miss, but I could not dream of putting you to so much trouble. I will look in at a drugstore on my way to the station-house. I fear I must leave you now, as I have to go and drish-hosh-hish.”

“What?”

“Dress, miss.”

“But you are dressed.”

“For the purposes of the raid to which I alluded it is necessary for our posse to put on full evening drah-woosh. In order to deceive the staff of the rish-wish-wosh, and lull them into a false security. It would never do, you see, for us to go there in our uniforms. That would put them on their guard.”

===Chinese===

“您真是个大好人，小姐，可我怎么敢想劳您这么大的驾。去分局的路上，我顺道进一家药房就是了。眼下我恐怕得告辞了，因为我得去换装——嚯什——嚯什。”

“什么？”

“换装，小姐。”

“可您这不是穿着呢嘛。”

“为着我方才提到的那场突击搜查，我们这队人马必须换上全套的大礼——嗷什。好去蒙哄那家餐——伊什——威什——哦什里的伙计，叫他们高枕无忧、松懈大意。您瞧，我们若是穿着制服上那儿去，可就万万使不得了。那会叫他们警觉起来。”

===Original===

“How exciting! What restaurant are you raiding?”

Officer Garroway hesitated.

“Well, miss, it is in the nature of an official secret, of course, but on the understanding that you will let it go no further, the rosh-ow-wush is the Purple Chicken, just round the corner. I will wish you good night, miss, as I really must be off.”

“But wait a moment. I came here to meet Mr. Finch. Have you seen anything of him?”

===Chinese===

“多带劲！你们要突击的是哪家餐馆？”

加罗威警官迟疑了一下。

“这个嘛，小姐，按性质讲自然是一项公务机密，不过只要您答应决不外传——那家啰什——噢——沃什，就是拐角上的紫鸡餐厅。我祝您晚安，小姐，我是真的非动身不可了。”

“且慢，等一等。我是上这儿来会芬奇先生的。您可见过他的人影？”

===Original===

“No, miss. Nobody has visited the apartment while I have been there.”

“Oh, then I’ll wait. Good night. I hope you will feel better soon.”

“I feel better already, miss,” said Officer Garroway gallantly, “thanks to your kind sympathy. Good nish-nosh, miss.”

Molly went out on to the roof, and stood there gazing over the million twinkling lights of the city. At this height the voice of New York sank to a murmur, and the air was sweet and cool. Little breezes rustled in the potted shrubs over which Mullett was wont to watch with such sedulous care, and a half-moon was shining in rather a deprecating way, as if conscious of not being at its best in such surroundings. For, like Sigsbee H. Waddington (now speeding towards his third Gallagher), the moon, really to express itself, needs the great open spaces.

===Chinese===

“没见过，小姐。我在此守着的这阵子，没有一个人来过这所公寓。”

“哦，那我等着吧。晚安。但愿您快点儿好起来。”

“我已经觉得好多了，小姐，”加罗威警官殷勤地说，“多蒙您好心的垂怜。晚安——呢什——呢什，小姐。”

莫莉走到屋顶上，站在那儿，眺望着全城百万盏闪烁的灯火。在这样的高处，纽约的喧声低成了喃喃絮语，空气甜爽而清凉。微风在盆栽灌木间窸窣作响——平日里马利特照管它们是何等殷勤小心——半轮月亮洒下清辉，神情颇有几分讪讪的，仿佛自知在这样一片环境里实在算不得仪态万方。因为，正如西格斯比·H·沃丁顿（此刻正飞车赶奔他的第三位加拉格尔）一样，月亮这东西，若要真正一抒怀抱，是非得有辽阔的天地不可的。

===Original===

Molly, however, found nothing to criticise in that pale silver glow. She felt a proprietary interest in the moon. It was her own private and personal moon, and should have been shining in through the windows of the drawing-room of the train that bore her away on her wedding-journey. That that journey had been postponed was in no way the fault of the moon: and, gazing up at it, she tried to convey by her manner her appreciation of the fact.

It was at this point that a strangled exclamation broke the stillness: and, turning, she perceived George Finch.

George Finch stood in the moonlight, staring dumbly. Although what he saw before him had all the appearance of being Molly, and though a rash and irreflective observer would no doubt have said that it was Molly, it was so utterly impossible that she could really be there that he concluded that he was suffering from an hallucination. The nervous strain of the exacting day through which he had passed had reduced him, he perceived, to the condition of those dying travellers in the desert who see mirages. And so he remained where he was, not daring to approach closer: for he knew that if you touch people in dreams they vanish.

But Molly was of a more practical turn of mind. She had come twenty miles to see George. She had waited for George for what seemed several hours. And here George was. She did the sensible thing. Uttering a little squeak of rapture, she ran at him like a rabbit.

===Chinese===

然而，对那一派淡淡的银辉，莫莉挑不出半点错处。她觉得对这月亮自己握有一份业主似的权益。这是她一个人的、专属私己的月亮，本该透进那列载她驶上蜜月之旅的火车的客厅车窗里去。那趟旅程之延期，却无论如何怪不到月亮头上：她仰望着它，竭力想用神色向它表明，这一层公道，她是认账的。

正是在这时，一声像被掐住喉咙似的惊呼划破了寂静：她转过身，看见了乔治·芬奇。

乔治·芬奇站在月光里，目瞪口呆地凝望着。尽管眼前所见的一切，无不表明这就是莫莉；尽管一个轻率卤莽、欠加思索的旁观者准会一口咬定这就是莫莉；可她真会在这儿，实在太不可能了，于是他断定自己害上了幻觉症。他这才醒悟，熬过这劳神费力的一天，神经的紧绷已经把他折腾到了沙漠里垂死的旅人望见海市蜃楼的那种境地。所以他待在原地，不敢再挪近一步：他知道，梦里的人，你一碰，就不见了。

可莫莉的心思要实际得多。她跑了二十英里路来看乔治。她等乔治等了仿佛有几个钟头之久。而如今乔治就在眼前。于是她做了最明智的事。她欢欣地短促尖叫了一声，像只兔子似的直朝他冲过去。

===Original===

“Georgie! My pet!”

One lives and learns. George found that he had been all wrong, and that his preconceived ideas about dreams and what could and could not happen in them must be revised. For, so far from vanishing when touched, his wraith appeared to be growing more substantial every moment.

He shut his eyes and kissed her tentatively. He opened his eyes. She was still there.

“Is it really you?” said George.

===Chinese===

“乔吉！我的心肝！”

人活着，就能长见识。乔治发现自己大谬不然，他关于梦境、关于梦里能与不能发生什么的成见，必须加以修正了。因为他这位幽魂，非但没有一碰即散，反倒似乎每一刻都变得愈发有血有肉。

他闭上眼睛，试探着吻了吻她。他睁开眼睛。她还在那儿。

“当真是你吗？”乔治说。

===Original===

“Yes, really me.”

“But how﻿ ﻿… what﻿ ﻿… ?”

It was borne in upon George﻿—for he was a young man of good average intelligence﻿—that he was spoiling a golden moment with unseasonable chatter. This was no time for talk. He talked, accordingly, no more; and there was silence on the roof. The moon looked down, well pleased. There is not much of interest for a moon to look at in a large city, and this was the sort of thing it liked best﻿—the only sort of thing, if you came right down to it, that made it worth a moon’s while to shine at all.

George clung to Molly, and Molly clung to George, like two shipwrecked survivors who have come together on a wave-swept beach. And the world moved on, forgotten.

===Chinese===

“嗯，当真是我。”

“可是怎么……什么……？”

乔治渐渐省悟过来——他毕竟是个天资平平偏上的年轻人——自己正拿不合时宜的絮叨，糟蹋着一个黄金时刻。这可不是说话的时候。于是他不再言语了；屋顶上安静下来。月亮俯瞰着，心满意足。在大都市里，月亮实在没多少有意思的东西可看，而眼前这一幕，恰是它顶爱看的那一种——说到底，也唯有这一类的事，才不枉月亮出来亮这一遭。

乔治紧紧搂着莫莉，莫莉紧紧搂着乔治，像两个海难的幸存者，在浪涛横扫的滩头重逢。而这世界照旧运转，被忘在了脑后。

===Original===

But the world will never allow itself to be forgotten for long. Suddenly George broke away with an exclamation. He ran to the wall and looked over.

“What’s the matter?”

George returned, reassured. His concern had been groundless.

“I thought I saw someone on the fire-escape, darling.”

“On the fire-escape? Why, who could it be?”

===Chinese===

可这世界从来不肯让人把它忘得太久。突然，乔治惊叫一声，挣脱开来。他奔到矮墙边，探头朝下望去。

“怎么回事？”

乔治走了回来，安下心来。他的担心原来是场虚惊。

“我方才仿佛瞧见防火梯上有人来着，亲爱的。”

“防火梯上？哎呀，那会是谁呢？”

===Original===

“I thought it might be the man who has the apartment on the floor below. A ghastly, sneaking, snooping fellow named Lancelot Biffen. I’ve known him to climb up before. He’s the editor of *Town Gossip*, the last person we want to have watching us.”

Molly uttered a cry of alarm.

“You’re sure he wasn’t there?”

“Quite sure.”

“It would be awful if anyone saw me here.”

===Chinese===

“我还当是楼下那套公寓的主人呢。一个叫兰斯洛特·比芬（Lancelot Biffen）的家伙，讨厌透顶，鬼鬼祟祟，专爱刺探。从前我就撞见他往上爬过。他是《街谈巷议》（Town Gossip）的编辑，是咱们顶不乐意叫他盯着咱们的那号人。”

莫莉惊叫了一声。

“你拿准他没在那儿？”

“拿得准。”

“要是叫什么人看见我在这儿，可就糟了。”

===Original===

George silently cursed the too vivid imagination which had led him to suppose that he had seen a dark form outlined against the summer sky. He had spoiled the golden moment, and it could not be recaptured.

“Don’t be afraid, dear,” he said. “Even if he had seen you, he would never have guessed who you were.”

“You mean he would naturally expect to find you up here kissing some girl?”

George was in the state of mind when a man cannot be quite sure what his words mean, if anything: but so positive was he that he did not mean this that he got his tongue tied in a knot trying to say so in three different ways simultaneously.

“Well, after what happened this afternoon﻿ ﻿…” said Molly.

===Chinese===

乔治在心里咒骂自己那过分生动的想象力，是它叫自己疑心看见了映衬在夏夜天幕上的一个黑影。黄金时刻已叫他亲手糟蹋了，再也无法追回。

“别怕，亲爱的，”他说，“就算真叫他看见了，他也决计猜不出你是谁。”

“你的意思是，他本来就觉得天经地义，料定你会在这楼顶上吻哪个姑娘？”

乔治这时的心理状态，正是一个人拿不准自己那些话——倘若那些话当真有什么意思的话——究竟是什么意思的状态：可他又万分肯定自己绝没有那个意思，以致于想同时用三种说法来表白这一点，把舌头都打成了结。

“这个嘛，今天下午出了那样的事……”莫莉说。

===Original===

She drew away. She was not normally an unkind girl, but the impulse of the female of the species to torture the man it loves is well-known. Woman may be a ministering angel when pain and anguish rack the brow: but, if at other times she sees a chance to prod the loved one and watch him squirm, she hates to miss it.

George’s tongue appeared to him to be now in the sort of condition a ball of wool is in after a kitten has been playing with it. With a supreme effort he contrived to straighten out a few of the major kinks, just sufficient to render speech possible.

“I swear to you,” began George, going so far in his emotion as to raise a passionate fist towards the moon.

Molly gurgled delightedly. She loved this young man most when he looked funny: and he had seldom looked funnier than now.

===Chinese===

她抽身退开。她本来并不是个狠心的姑娘，可是雌性这一族折磨其所爱者的那种冲动，是尽人皆知的。当痛苦和哀伤蹙紧眉头时，女人或许是位救苦救难的天使：可在旁的时辰，只要一有机会戳一戳心爱的人、看他扭动挣扎，她是死也不肯错过的。

在乔治自己的感觉里，他的舌头这会儿正处在一团毛线被小猫玩耍过之后的境地。他拼尽全力，才总算把几处大疙瘩捋直了，刚刚够把话说出口。

“我对你发誓，”乔治开口道，激动之下，竟朝着月亮扬起了一只满腔热忱的拳头。

莫莉高兴得咯咯作声。这位年轻人模样滑稽的时候，她最是爱他：而他平生也很少有比此刻更滑稽的模样了。

===Original===

“I swear to you on my solemn oath that I had never seen that infernal girl before in my life.”

“She seemed to know you so well.”

“She was a perfect, complete, total, utter and absolute stranger.”

“Are you sure? Perhaps you had simply forgotten all about her.”

“I swear it,” said George, and only just stopped himself from adding “by yonder moon.” “If you want to know what I think.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“我在庄严的誓约之下向你起誓，那个万恶的丫头，我平生从没见过。”

“她好像对你熟得很呢。”

“她是一个十全十美、彻头彻尾、十足全然、不折不扣、绝绝对对的陌生人。”

“你拿得准？兴许你只是把她忘得一干二净了。”

“我起誓，”乔治说，好容易才忍住没在后面添上一句“指着头上那轮明月”。“要是你想知道我是怎么看的……”

===Original===

“Oh, I do.”

“I believe she was mad. Stark, staring mad.”

Molly decided that the anguish had lasted long enough. A girl has to judge these things to a nicety. Sufficient agony is good for a man, stimulating his mind and keeping him bright and alert: but too much is too much.

“Poor old Georgie!” she said soothingly. “You don’t really suppose for a moment that I believe a word of what she said, do you?”

===Chinese===

“哦，我正想知道。”

“我看她是疯了。疯得彻头彻尾，疯得货真价实。”

莫莉断定，这份苦刑也拖得够久的了。这种事，姑娘家必须掐算得分毫不差。适度的苦楚对男人有好处，能磨砺他的心智，叫他保持伶俐警醒：可过了量就是过了量。

“可怜的乔吉！”她抚慰道。“她说的那些话，你总不会当真以为我有一句信了吧，是不是？”

===Original===

“What! You didn’t?”

“Of course I didn’t.”

“Molly,” said George, weighing his words, “you are without exception the dearest, sweetest, loveliest, most perfect and angelic thing that ever lived.”

“I know. Aren’t you lucky?”

===Chinese===

“什么！你没信？”

“我当然没信。”

“莫莉，”乔治斟酌着字句说，“你是自有生以来绝无仅有的最亲爱、最甜美、最可爱、最完美、最天使的宝贝。”

“我知道。你的福气大不大？”

===Original===

“You saw at once that the girl was mad, didn’t you? You realised immediately that she was suffering from some sort of obsession, poor soul, which made her.﻿ ﻿…”

“No, I didn’t. I couldn’t think what it was all about at first, and then father came in and said that my pearl necklace had disappeared, and I understood.”

“Your pearl necklace? Disappeared?”

“She stole it. She was a thief. Don’t you see? It was really awfully clever. She couldn’t have got it any other way. But when she burst in and said all those things about you, naturally she took everybody’s attention off the wedding-presents. And then she pretended to faint on the table, and just snapped the necklace up and rushed out, and nobody guessed what had happened.”

===Chinese===

“你一眼就看穿那丫头是疯了，是不是？你立刻就明白了，可怜的人儿，她害的是某种魔怔，才闹得她……”

“不，我没看穿。起初我根本闹不明白这究竟是怎么回事，后来父亲进来，说我的珍珠项链不见了，我就明白了。”

“你的珍珠项链？不见了？”

“是她偷的。她是个贼。你还不明白吗？这一手其实高明得很。换别的法子，她休想得手。可她闯进门来，把你数落了那么一大通，自然就把众人的注意力从结婚礼物上引开了。接着她装出一头栽在桌上晕过去的样子，就势一把抄起项链，冲出门去，谁也没猜出出了什么事。”

===Original===

George drew in a whistling breath. His fists clenched. He stared coldly at one of the potted shrubs as if it had done him a personal injury.

“If ever I meet that girl.﻿ ﻿…”

Molly laughed.

“Mother still insists that you had known her before and that the story she told was true and that she only took the necklace as an afterthought. Isn’t she funny!”

===Chinese===

乔治嘶地倒抽了一口冷气。他攥紧双拳。他冷冰冰地瞪着其中一丛盆栽灌木，仿佛那灌木刚给了他一记人身侮辱。

“我要是哪天撞见那丫头……”

莫莉笑了。

“母亲到现在还咬定，说你从前早就认识她，说她讲的那一套是真的，还说她拿走项链不过是事后临时起的意。你说她逗不逗！”

===Original===

“Funny,” said George heavily, “is not the word. She is one long scream from the rise of the curtain, and ought to be beaten over the head with a blackjack. If you want my candid and considered opinion of that zymotic scourge who has contrived to hook herself on to your family in the capacity of stepmother to you and general mischief-maker to the rest of the world, let me begin by saying.﻿ ﻿… However, there is no time to go into that now.”

“No, there isn’t. I must be getting back.”

“Oh no!”

“Yes. I must go home and pack.”

===Chinese===

“逗，”乔治沉痛地说，“可不是那个字眼。从大幕升起那一刻起，她就是连绵不断的一声怪叫，真该拿包铅短棍敲她的脑袋。你要是想听一听，我对那场行走的瘟疫——那个千方百计把自己挂上你们家钩子、给你当继母、给其余世人当总捣乱专家的祸水——有什么坦率而深思熟虑的看法，那我的开场白是……不过，眼下没工夫细说了。”

“是啊，没工夫。我得回去了。”

“哦，别！”

“非回不可。我得回家收拾行李去。”

===Original===

“Pack?”

“Just a suitcase.”

The universe reeled about George.

“Do you mean you’re going away?” he quavered.

“Yes. Tomorrow.”

===Chinese===

“收拾行李？”

“就收拾一只手提箱。”

天旋地转，宇宙绕着乔治打起转来。

“你是说，你要走了？”他颤声问道。

“嗯。明天。”

===Original===

“Oh, heavens! For long?”

“Forever. With you.”

“With﻿ ﻿… ?”

“Of course. Don’t you understand? I’m going home now to pack a suitcase. Then I’ll drive back to New York and stay the night at an hotel, and tomorrow we’ll be married early in the morning, and in the afternoon we’ll go off together, all alone, miles and miles from everybody.”

===Chinese===

“哦，天哪！去很久吗？”

“一辈子。跟着你。”

“跟……？”

“那当然。你还不明白？我现在这就回家去收拾手提箱。然后开车回纽约，在旅馆过一夜，明天一大早我们就成婚，到了下午，我们就双双离开，就我们两个，离一切人远远的，一程又一程。”

===Original===

“Molly!”

“Look at that moon. About now it ought to have been shining into our drawing-room on the train.”

“Yes.”

“Well, there will be just as good a moon tomorrow night.”

===Chinese===

“莫莉！”

“瞧那月亮。这会儿工夫，它本该正照进我们火车上的那间客厅了。”

“是啊。”

“嘿，明儿晚上也会有一个不差分毫的好月亮。”

===Original===

George moistened his lips. Something seemed to be tickling his nose, and inside his chest a curious growth had begun to swell, rendering breathing difficult.

“And half an hour ago,” he said, “I thought I would never see you again.”

“Come down and put me in the car,” said Molly briskly. “I left it at the door.”

They descended the stairs. Owing to the eccentricity of the elevator, George had frequently had to go up and down these stairs before: but it was only now that he noticed for the first time a peculiarity about them that made them different from the stairs of every other apartment-house he had visited. They were, he observed, hedged about with roses and honeysuckle, and many more birds were singing on them than you would expect in an apartment-house. Odd. And yet, as he immediately realised, all perfectly in order.

===Chinese===

乔治舔了舔嘴唇。似乎有什么东西在他鼻子里痒痒地撩拨，胸腔里头也有一团奇怪的赘物开始发胀，压得他喘不上气来。

“就在半个钟头以前，”他说，“我还当自己再也见不着你了。”

“陪我下去，把我弄上汽车，”莫莉爽利地说，“车我停在门口了。”

他们下了楼。由于那部电梯脾气古怪，乔治从前没少在这些楼梯上爬上爬下：可直到此刻，他才头一回注意到这些楼梯有一桩特异之处，同他到过的每一座公寓楼的楼梯都不相同。他发觉，这些楼梯四下里给蔷薇和忍冬围簇着，栖在上头唱歌的鸟儿，也比你在任何一座公寓楼里所敢指望的多出许多。怪事。然而他随即就明白过来：其实这一切都再合规矩不过。

===Original===

Molly climbed into the two-seater: and George mentioned a point which had presented itself to him.

“I don’t see why you need hurry off like this.”

“I do. I’ve got to pack and get away before mother gets home.”

“Is that blas﻿ ﻿… is your stepmother in New York?”

===Chinese===

莫莉爬进双座小汽车：乔治提出了一个刚刚在他心里冒头的问题。

“我不懂你干吗非得这样急着走。”

“我懂。我得赶在母亲到家以前收拾停当、抽身脱走。”

“那个该——你的继母在纽约吗？”

===Original===

“Yes. She came in to see the police.”

Until this moment George had been looking on New York as something rather out of the common run of cities﻿—he particularly liked the way those violets were sprouting up through the flagstones: but on receipt of this information he found that it had lost a little of its charm.

“Oh, she’s in New York, is she?”

“Probably on her way home by now.”

===Chinese===

“在。她进城来见警察了。”

直到这一刻为止，乔治一直把纽约看成一座颇不落寻常城市窠臼的地方——他尤其钟爱那些紫罗兰从石板缝里探头探脑往外冒的模样：可这个消息一到手，他便发觉纽约的魅力损耗了几分。

“哦，她在纽约，是吧？”

“这会儿多半已经在回家的路上了。”

===Original===

“You don’t think there’s time for us to go and have a little dinner somewhere? Just a cosy little dinner at some quiet little restaurant?”

“Good gracious, no! I’m running it very fine as it is.” She looked at him closely. “But, Georgie darling, you’re starving. I can see it. You’re quite pale and worn-out. When did you last have anything to eat?”

“Eat? Eat? I don’t remember.”

“What did you do after that business this afternoon?”

===Chinese===

“你看咱们怕是没工夫上哪儿吃一顿小小的晚饭了吧？就在哪家清静的小馆子里，亲亲热热吃一顿小小的晚饭？”

“天老爷，可不行！本来就掐得分秒必争了。”她凑近把他细细端详。“可是，乔吉，亲爱的，你在饿肚子呢。我看得出来。你脸色好白，人也熬乏了。你上一回吃东西，是什么时候？”

“吃？吃东西？记不得了。”

“今天下午那桩事之后，你都干了些什么来着？”

===Original===

“I﻿—well, I walked around for awhile. And then I hung about in the bushes for awhile, hoping you would come out. And then﻿—I believe I went to the station and took a train or something.”

“You poor darling! Go and eat something at once.”

“Why can’t I come back to Hempstead with you?”

“Because you can’t.”

===Chinese===

“我——呃，我瞎走了一阵。后来就在树丛里守了一阵，盼着你出来。再后来——我记得是上车站搭了趟火车还是什么的。”

“你这可怜的心肝！马上去吃点儿东西。”

“我为什么不能陪你一道回亨普斯特德？”

“因为你就是不能。”

===Original===

“What hotel will you go to tonight?”

“I don’t know. But I’ll come and see you for a minute before I go there.”

“What, here? You’ll come here?”

“Yes.”

===Chinese===

“你今晚去哪家旅馆？”

“不知道。不过在上那儿去之前，我会来瞧你一小会儿。”

“什么，这儿？你要上这儿来？”

“嗯。”

===Original===

“You’ll come back here?”

“Yes.”

“You promise?”

“Yes, if you will go and have some dinner. You look perfectly ghastly.”

“Dinner? All right, I’ll have some.”

===Chinese===

“你要回到这儿来？”

“嗯。”

“你答应？”

“嗯，只要你肯去吃顿晚饭。你瞧上去真真吓人。”

“晚饭？行，我吃就是。”

===Original===

“Mind you do. If you haven’t by the time I get back, I’ll go straight home again and never marry you as long as I live. Goodbye, darling, I must be off.”

The two-seater moved away and turned into Washington Square. George stood looking after it long after there was nothing to look at but empty street. Then he started off, like some knight of old on a quest commanded by his lady, to get the dinner on which she had so strongly insisted. She had been wrong, of course, in telling him to go and dine: for what he wanted to do and what any good doctor would have recommended him to do was to return to the roof and gaze at the moon. But her lightest wish was law.

Where could he go most quickly and get the repulsive task done with the minimum waste of time?

The Purple Chicken. It was just round the corner, and a resolute man if he stuck to their prix fixe table-d’hôte at one-dollar-fifty, could shovel a meal into himself in about ten minutes﻿—which was not long to ask the moon to wait.

Besides, at the Purple Chicken you could get “it” if they knew you. And George, though an abstemious young man, felt that “it” was just what at the moment he most required. On an occasion like this he ought, of course, to sip golden nectar from rare old crystal: but, failing that, synthetic whisky served in a coffeepot was perhaps the next best thing.

===Chinese===

“你可得做到。等我回来你还没吃上，我就径直回家去，这辈子再也不嫁你了。再见，亲爱的，我非走不可了。”

双座小汽车开动了，拐进华盛顿广场。乔治站在原地目送着，直到眼前除了空荡荡的街道再没什么可看，还在望着。随后他迈步上路，活像古时候奉贵妇之命出门寻访的骑士，去办她再三坚持的那顿晚饭。她叫他前去用饭，这吩咐自然是大错特错：因为他想做的事，随便哪位高明的医生也都会建议他做的事，是回到屋顶上去，凝望月亮。然而她最微末的一个心愿，于他就是法律。

哪里能让他去得最快，把这桩可憎的差事办完，又把时间的浪费压到最小？

紫鸡餐厅。它就在街角上，一个果断的人，只要咬定他们那一美元五十美分的定食套餐（prix fixe table-d’hôte）不撒口，十来分钟就能把一顿饭铲进肚里——这点工夫，请月亮稍候片刻，不算苛求。

何况，在紫鸡餐厅，只要人家认得你，就能弄到“那个”。而乔治虽然是个有节制的年轻人，也觉得眼下自己最需要的，恰恰就是“那个”。这样的场合，照理他本该用稀世的古董水晶杯啜饮金色的琼浆：可既然没有，那末用咖啡壶端上来的合成威士忌（synthetic whisky），大约也就算得上次一等的最好选择了。

## XVI / 第十六章

### I / 一

===Original===
The Purple Chicken seemed to be having a big night. The room opening on to the street, when George reached it, was so crowded, that there was no chance of getting a table. He passed through, hoping to find a resting-place in the open-air section which lay beyond: and was struck, as he walked, by the extraordinarily fine physique of many of the diners.
As a rule, the Purple Chicken catered for the intelligentsia of the neighbourhood, and these did not run to thews and sinews. On most nights in the week you would find the tables occupied by wispy poets and slender futurist painters: but now, though these were present in great numbers, they were supplemented by quite a sprinkling of granite-faced men with knobby shoulders and protruding jaws. George came to the conclusion that a convention from one of the outlying States must be in town and that these men were members of it, bent upon seeing Bohemia.
He did not, however, waste a great deal of time in speculation on this matter, for, stirred by the actual presence of food, he had begun now to realise that Molly had been right, as women always are, and that, while his whole higher self cried out for the moon, his lower self was almost equally as insistent on taking in supplies. And at this particular restaurant it was happily possible to satisfy both selves simultaneously: for there, as he stepped into what the management called the garden﻿—a flagged backyard dotted with tables﻿—was the moon, all present and correct, and there, also, were waiters waiting to supply the prix fixe table-d’hôte at one-dollar-fifty.

===Chinese===
紫鸡餐厅今晚似乎生意兴隆。乔治赶到时，那间临街的堂屋已经挤得水泄不通，想占一张桌子毫无指望。他穿过堂屋往里走，指望在后面那片露天场子里寻个落脚的地方；一路走过去，许多食客那副出格健壮的体格给了他很深的印象。
平心而论，紫鸡餐厅招待的一向是左近一带的知识界人士，而知识界是不长腱子肉的。一星期里多数晚上，你会看到桌边坐满弱柳扶风的诗人，外加瘦骨伶仃的未来派画家；可今晚，这类人物虽照旧成群结队，却又点缀着相当一批面如花岗岩的汉子——肩膀疙疙瘩瘩，下巴颏朝前撅着。乔治得出的结论是：外州某个团体准是在本市开年会，这些汉子便是与会代表，特意志见识见识波西米亚的。
不过，他没有在这件事上多费心思猜测。一来当真见着了吃食，他这才醒悟莫莉说得没错——女人从来不错：他整个高尚的自我在声声呼唤月亮，可低下的那个自我，对补给军粮的坚持也毫不逊色。而在这家特别的馆子里，两个自我竟能皆大欢喜地同时得到满足：他一脚跨进店掌柜口中的“园子”——一块铺着石板、零零星星摆着桌子的后院——月亮就在那里，圆圆满满、一丝不苟地当值；侍者也在那里候着，预备端上定价一块五美元一位的定食套餐。

===Original===
It seemed to George the neatest possible combination: and his only anxiety now was with regard to the securing of a seat. At first glance it appeared that every table was occupied.
This conjecture was confirmed by a second glance. But, though all the other tables had their full quota, there was one, standing beside the Sheridan’s back wall and within a few feet of its fire-escape, that was in the possession of a single diner. This diner George approached, making his expression as winning as possible. He did not, as a rule, enjoy sharing a table with a stranger, but as an alternative to going away and trudging round in search of another restaurant it seemed a good plan now.
“Excuse me, sir,” said George, “would you mind if I came to this table?”

===Chinese===
在乔治看来，这是天底下再妙不过的二合一；眼下他唯一的焦虑，便是能不能捞到一个座位。乍一看去，每张桌子似乎都有主了。
再看一眼，猜想得了证实。不过，其余各桌虽说全都满员，却独有一张——就在谢里丹公寓后墙边上、离防火梯几步之遥——只由一位食客独占。乔治朝这位食客走了过去，脸上摆出尽可能讨喜的神情。跟陌生人拼桌，按他的脾性向来是不乐意的；可比起掉头走人、拖着两条腿满城另寻馆子，眼下这着棋看着不坏。
“对不起，先生，”乔治说，“我到这张桌上来坐，您不介意吧？”

===Original===
The other looked up from the *poulet rôti aux pommes de terre* and *salade Bruxelloise* which had been engaging his attention. He was plainly one of the convention from the outlying State, if physique could be taken as a guide. He spread upwards from the table like a circus giant and the hands which gripped the knife and fork had that same spaciousness which George had noted in the diners in the other room. Only as to the eyes did this man differ from his fellows. They had had eyes of a peculiarly steely and unfriendly type, the sort of eyes which a motorist instinctively associates with traffic-policemen and a professional thief with professional detectives. This man’s gaze was mild and friendly, and his eyes would have been attractive but for the redness of their rims and the generally inflamed look which they had.
“By no means, sir,” he replied to George’s polite query.
“Place very crowded tonight.”

===Chinese===
那人从正吃得津津有味的烤鸡配土豆（poulet rôti aux pommes de terre）和布鲁塞尔沙拉（salade Bruxelloise）上抬起头来。若以体格为凭，他分明是外州代表团的一员。他从桌边向上铺展开去，活像马戏团的巨人；握着刀叉的那双手，也同乔治在里屋食客身上注意到的一样宽大雄壮。唯独眼睛，此人与他的同类不同。那些人的眼睛一律是钢铁般的冷硬型，是叫开车的人本能地联想到交通警察、叫惯窃本能地联想到刑警侦探的那一种；而这位仁兄的目光却温和友善，那一双眼睛本可以生得相当动人，只可惜眼圈发红，整体看去一副害了炎症的模样。
“岂敢岂敢，先生，”他回答乔治客气的问话。
“店里今晚人真多。”

===Original===
“Extremely.”
“Then, if you won’t mind, I’ll sit here.”
“Delighted,” said the other.
George looked round for a waiter and found one at his elbow. However crowded the Purple Chicken might be, its staff never neglected the old habitué: and it had had the benefit of George’s regular custom for many months.

===Chinese===
“多得很。”
“那么，您不介意的话，我就坐这儿了。”
“欢迎之至。”那人说。
乔治环顾找侍者，发现一位已经站在自己手肘边。紫鸡餐厅再挤，店里的伙计也从不怠慢熟客老主顾；而乔治惠顾此店，论月计已有好几个月了。

===Original===
“Good evening, sare,” said the waiter, smiling the smile which had once broken hearts in Assisi.
“Good evening, Guiseppe,” said George. “I’ll take the dinner.”
“Yes, sare. Sick or glear zoop?”
“Sick. Crowded tonight, Guiseppe.”
“Yes, sare. Lots of guys here tonight. Big business.”

===Chinese===
“晚上好，先森，”侍者说，脸上堆起那种曾经在阿西西（Assisi）迷倒众生的微笑。
“晚上好，朱塞佩（Guiseppe），”乔治说，“我来一份客饭。”
“是，先森。能汤还是清汤？”
“能汤。今晚人多，是吧，朱塞佩。”
“是，先森。今晚老些客人。大生意。”

===Original===
“The waiter appears to know you,” said George’s companion.
“Oh, yes,” said George, “I’m in here all the time.”
“Ah,” said the other, thoughtfully.
The soup arrived, and George set about it with a willing spoon. His companion became hideously involved with spaghetti.

===Chinese===
“这位侍者好像认得你，”乔治的邻座说。
“哦，可不是，”乔治说，“我是这儿的老主顾。”
“哦。”那人说，若有所思。
汤上来了，乔治兴致勃勃地挥勺开动。他的邻座则同意大利细面条展开了一场惊心动魄的缠斗。

===Original===
“This your first visit to New York?” asked George, after an interval.
“No, indeed, sir. I live in New York.”
“Oh, I thought you were up from the country.”
“No, sir. I live right here in New York.”

===Chinese===
“头一回来纽约？”过了一会儿，乔治问。
“哪里的话，先生。我就住在纽约。”
“哦，我还当你是乡下上来的。”
“不是，先生。我就住在这纽约城里。”

===Original===
A curious idea that he had seen this fellow before somewhere came over George. Yes, at some time and in some place he could have sworn that he had gazed upon that long body, that prominent Adam’s apple, and that gentle expanse of face. He searched his memory. Nothing stirred.
“I have an odd feeling that we have met before,” he said.
“I was thinking just the same myself,” replied the other.
“My name is Finch.”

===Chinese===
乔治心头泛起一个古怪的念头：这家伙他从前在哪儿见过。是的，不知什么时候、也不知什么地方，他敢起誓，自己曾经端详过那副长身量、那个突出的喉结，还有那片和气的宽脸盘。他搜刮记忆。毫无动静。
“我说不清怎么，总觉得咱们从前见过，”他说。
“我方才正想着同一件事，”那人答道。
“我姓芬奇。”

===Original===
“Mine is Cabot. Delancy Cabot.”
George shook his head.
“I don’t remember the name.”
“Yours is curiously familiar. I have heard it before, but cannot think when.”

===Chinese===
“我姓卡博特。德兰西·卡博特。”
乔治摇了摇头。
“这名字我没印象。”
“你的名字出奇地耳熟。从前听人提起过，可想不起是什么时候。”

===Original===
“Do you live in Greenwich Village?”
“Somewhat further uptown. And you?”
“I live in the apartment on top of this building here at the back of us.”
A sudden light that seemed that of recognition came into the other’s face. George observed it.

===Chinese===
“你住在格林尼治村吗？”
“再往城里走上一些。你呢？”
“我就住在咱们背后这座楼顶上的公寓里。”
那人脸上倏地掠过一道光，瞧着像是认出了什么。乔治看在眼里。

===Original===
“Have you remembered where we met?”
“No, sir. No, indeed,” said the other hastily. “It has entirely escaped me.” He took a sip of ice water. “I recall, however, that you are an artist.”
“That’s right. You are not one, by any chance?”
“I am a poet.”

===Chinese===
“想起咱们在哪儿见过面了吗？”
“没有，先生，实在没有，”那人急忙说，“忘得一干二净了。”他呷了一口冰水。“不过我记得，你是位画家。”
“没错。你该不会恰好也是吧？”
“我是位诗人。”

===Original===
“A poet?” George tried to conceal his somewhat natural surprise. “Where does your stuff appear mostly?”
“I have published nothing as yet, Mr. Finch,” replied the other sadly.
“Tough luck. I have never sold a picture.”
“Too bad.”
They gazed at one another with kindly eyes, two fellow-sufferers from the public’s lack of taste. Guiseppe appeared, bearing deep-dish apple-pie in one hand, *poulet rôti* in the other.

===Chinese===
“诗人？”乔治竭力掩住那份不算过分的惊讶，“你的作品多半登在哪儿？”
“我还什么都没发表过，芬奇先生，”那人黯然答道。
“运气够呛。我一张画也没卖出去过。”
“真糟糕。”
两人以充满善意的眼光彼此凝望，同是大众鉴赏力匮乏之下的受难兄弟。朱塞佩来了，一手托着深盘苹果派，一手端着烤鸡。

===Original===
“Guiseppe,” said George.
“Sare?”
George bent his lips towards the waiter’s attentive ear.
“Bzz﻿ ﻿… Bzz﻿ ﻿… Bzz﻿ ﻿…” said George.
“Yes, sare. Very good, sare. In one moment, sare.”

===Chinese===
“朱塞佩，”乔治说。
“先森？”
乔治把嘴唇凑近侍者恭听的耳朵。
“滋滋……滋滋……滋滋……”乔治说。
“是，先森。好嘞，先森。马上就来，先森。”

===Original===
George leaned back contentedly. Then it occurred to him that he had been a little remiss. He was not actually this red-eyed man’s host, but they had fraternised and they both knew what it was to toil at their respective arts without encouragement or appreciation.
“Perhaps you will join me?” he said.
“Join you, sir?”
“In a highball. Guiseppe has gone to get me one.”

===Chinese===
乔治心满意足地靠回椅背。随即他想起自己多少有点失礼。严格说来，这位红眼圈并不是他的客人，可两人已经称兄道弟，而且彼此都懂得那是什么滋味——埋头各自的艺业，却无人喝彩、无人赏识地苦熬。
“也许你愿意陪我一杯？”他说。
“陪你一杯，先生？”
“来杯威士忌苏打。朱塞佩已经给我取去了。”

===Original===
“Indeed? Is it possible to obtain alcoholic refreshment in this restaurant?”
“You can always get it if they know you.”
“But surely it is against the law?”
“Ha, ha!” laughed George. He liked this pleasant, whimsical fellow. “Ha, ha! Deuced good!”
He looked at him with that genial bonhomie with which one looks at a stranger in whom one has discovered a sly sense of humour. And, looking, he suddenly congealed.

===Chinese===
“当真？这家馆子里居然弄得到含酒精的饮料？”
“只要人家认得你，就总能弄到。”
“可这分明是犯法的呀？”
“哈哈！”乔治大笑。他喜欢上了这位有趣的妙人。“哈哈！妙透了！”
他望着对方，满脸和蔼的亲善——一个人发现陌生人身怀诙谐的幽默感时，正是这么望着的。望着望着，他突然僵住了。

===Original===
Stranger?
“Great Scott!” ejaculated George.
“Sir?”
“Nothing, nothing.”
Memory, though loitering by the way, had reached its goal at last. This man was no stranger. George recollected now where he had seen him before﻿—on the roof of the Sheridan, when the other, clad in policeman’s uniform, had warned him of the deplorable past of Frederick Mullett. The man was a cop, and under his very eyes, red rims and all, he had just ordered a highball.

===Chinese===
陌生人？
“我的老天爷！”乔治失声叫道。
“先生？”
“没什么，没什么。”
记忆虽然一路闲逛，毕竟还是到了终点。此人绝非陌生人。乔治此刻回想起来了，从前在哪儿见过他——就在谢里丹公寓的屋顶上，当时此人一身警察制服，告诫过他弗雷德里克·马利特那段见不得人的过去。这人是个警察，而就在他眼皮子底下——红眼圈一并算上——自己方才刚点了一杯威士忌苏打。

===Original===
George gave a feverish laugh.
“I was only kidding, of course,” he said.
“Kidding, Mr. Finch?”
“When I said that you could get it here. You can’t, of course. What Guiseppe is bringing me is ginger-ale.”
“Indeed?”
“And my name isn’t Finch,” babbled George. “It﻿—it is﻿—er﻿—Briskett. And I don’t live in that apartment up there, I live in.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
乔治发出一阵狂乱的干笑。
“我方才不过是打趣，”他说。
“打趣，芬奇先生？”
“我说这儿能弄到酒，不过是打趣。当然弄不到。朱塞佩给我端来的是姜汁汽水。”
“哦？”
“还有，我不姓芬奇，”乔治语无伦次地咕哝，“我——我是——呃——布里斯基特（Briskett）。楼上那间公寓也不是我住的，我住在……”

===Original===
He was aware of Guiseppe at his side. And Guiseppe was being unspeakably furtive and conspiratorial with a long glass and a coffeepot. He looked like one of the executive staff of the Black Hand plotting against the public weal.
“Is that my ginger-ale?” twittered George. “My ginger-ale, is that what you’ve got there?”
“Yes, sare. Your ginger-ale. Your ginger-ale, Mr. Feench, ha, ha, ha! You are vairy fonny gentleman,” said Guiseppe approvingly.
George could have kicked the man. If this was what the modern Italian was like, no wonder the country had had to have a dictatorship.
“Take it away,” he said, quivering. “I don’t want it in a coffeepot.”
“We always sairve the whisky in the coffeepot, Mr. Feench. You know that.”

===Chinese===
他察觉朱塞佩已经到了身边。而朱塞佩正拿着一只长杯和一把咖啡壶，做出一副说不出的鬼鬼祟祟、密谋串通的架势，看上去活像“黑手会”（Black Hand）班子里的执行要员，正在密谋危害公众福祉。
“那是我的姜汁汽水吗？”乔治尖着嗓子问，“我的姜汁汽水，你手里那个就是？”
“是，先森。您的姜汁汽水。您的姜汁汽水，芬琪先生，哈哈哈！你真是非常风趣的先森。”朱塞佩赞许地说。
乔治恨不能一脚踹在这家伙身上。倘若现代意大利人都是这副德行，难怪那个国家非得来一场独裁不可。
“拿走，”他哆嗦着说，“我不要装在咖啡壶里的。”
“威士忌我们向来是拿咖啡壶伺候的，芬琪先生。你是知道的。”

===Original===
Across the table George was appalled by a sinister sight. The man opposite was rising. Yards and yards of him were beginning to uncoil, and on his face there was a strange look of determination and menace.
“You’re.﻿ ﻿…”
George knew what the next word would have been. It would have been the verb “pinched.” But it was never uttered. With a sudden frenzy, George Finch acted. He was not normally a man of violence, but there are occasions when violence and nothing but violence will meet the case. There flashed through his mind a vision of what would be, did he not act with promptitude and despatch. He would be arrested, haled to jail, immured in a dungeon-cell. And Molly would come back and find no one there to welcome her and﻿—what was even worse﻿—no one to marry her on the morrow.

===Chinese===
桌子对面，一幅凶险的景象看得乔治魂飞魄散。对面的汉子站了起来。一码又一码的身量开始舒展开来，他脸上现出一种决绝与威吓交织的怪相。
“你这就给——”
乔治知道接下来那个词会是什么。那会是动词“逮个正着”。但它永远没能出口。乔治·芬奇骤然发了狂似的行动起来。他平素并非动粗之人，可有些场合，唯有动粗、非动粗不能解决问题。他脑海里闪过一幅图景：倘若他不雷厉风行、快刀斩乱麻，将会是什么下场。他会被捕，被拖进牢里，塞进地牢小黑屋。而莫莉回来时，将发现没有人在那儿迎接她——还有更糟的——明天没有人娶她。

===Original===
George did not hesitate. Seizing the tablecloth, he swept it off in a hideous whirl of apple-pie, ice water, bread, potatoes, salad and *poulet rôti*. He raised it on high, like a *retarius* in the arena, and brought it down in an enveloping mass on the policeman’s head. Interested cries arose on all sides. The Purple Chicken was one of those jolly, informal restaurants in which a spirit of clean Bohemian fun is the prevailing note, but even in the Purple Chicken occurrences like this were unusual and calculated to excite remark. Four diners laughed happily, a fifth exclaimed “Hot pazazas!” and a sixth said “Well, would you look at that!”
The New York police are not quitters. They may be down, but they are never out. A clutching hand emerged from the tablecloth and gripped George’s shoulder. Another clutching hand was groping about not far from his collar. The fingers of the first hand fastened their hold.
George was not in the frame of mind to be tolerant of this sort of thing. He hit out and smote something solid.

===Chinese===
乔治没有迟疑。他一把揪住桌布，猛地一抽，抽出一团可怖的旋风——苹果派、冰水、面包、土豆、沙拉和烤鸡四下横飞。他把桌布高高举起，宛如竞技场上的网斗士（retiarius），然后兜头罩下，把警察整个裹了起来。四下里响起兴致盎然的喊叫。紫鸡餐厅本属那种欢畅而不拘礼的馆子，清新的波西米亚乐趣是它的主旋律；可就连在紫鸡，这样的场面也属罕见，足够引人议论了。四位食客开怀大笑，第五位惊呼“好家伙！”，第六位说“哟，快瞧那个！”
纽约的警察不是轻易服输的人。他们可以被撂倒，却绝不会出局。一只抓挠的手从桌布里钻了出来，攥住乔治的肩膀。另一只抓挠的手在他领口不远处摸索。第一只手的手指扣紧了。
乔治此刻的心境可容不下这类把戏。他挥拳打去，击中了什么结实的东西。

===Original===
“*Casta dimura salve e pura!* Attaboy! Soak him again,” said Guiseppe, the waiter, convinced now that the man in the tablecloth was one who had not the best interests of the Purple Chicken at heart.
George did so. The tablecloth became still more agitated. The hand fell from his shoulder.
At this moment there was a confused noise of shouting from the inner room, and all the lights went out.
George would not have had it otherwise. Darkness just suited him. He leaped for the fire-escape and climbed up it with as great a celerity as Mrs. Waddington, some little time before, had used in climbing down. He reached the roof and paused for an instant, listening to the tumult below. Then, hearing through the din the sound of somebody climbing, he ran to the sleeping-porch and dived beneath the bed. To seek refuge in his apartment was, he realised, useless. That would be the first place the pursuer would draw.
He lay there, breathless. Footsteps came to the door. The door opened, and the light was switched on.

===Chinese===
“*Casta dimura salve e pura!* 好样的！再给他一下，”侍者朱塞佩说——他如今已认定，桌布里裹着的那个家伙，决计不是把紫鸡餐厅的利益放在心上的人。
乔治遵命照办。桌布翻腾得愈发激烈。那只手从他的肩头滑了下去。
就在此刻，里屋传来一片乱哄哄的叫喊，跟着所有的灯全灭了。
乔治巴不得如此。黑暗正合他的意。他纵身扑向防火梯，以不下于片刻之前沃丁顿夫人爬下来时的那股敏捷向上攀去。到了屋顶，他停了一瞬，倾听下面的骚动。随即，从喧闹声中听出有人在往上爬，他便奔向睡廊，一头钻到床底下。他很清楚，躲进自己的公寓毫无用处。那必是追捕的人头一个搜到的地方。
他屏息躺在那里。脚步声来到门前。门开了，灯亮了。

### II / 二

===Original===
In supposing that the person or persons whom he had heard climbing up the fire-escape were in pursuit of himself, George Finch had made a pardonable error. Various circumstances had combined to render his departure from the Purple Chicken unobserved.
In the first place, just as Officer Garroway was on the point of releasing his head from the folds of the tablecloth, Guiseppe, with a loyalty to his employers which it would be difficult to overpraise, hit him in the eye with the coffeepot. This had once more confused the policeman’s outlook, and by the time he was able to think clearly again the lights went out.
Simultaneously the moon, naturally on George’s side and anxious to do all that it could to help, went behind a thick cloud and stayed there. No human eye, therefore, had witnessed the young man’s climb for life.
The persons whom he had heard on the fire-escape were a couple who, like himself, had no object in mind other than a swift removal of themselves from the danger-zone. And so far were they from being hostile to George that each, had they seen him, would have urged him on and wished him luck. For one of them was Madame Eulalie and the other no less a man than J. Hamilton Beamish in person.

===Chinese===
乔治·芬奇以为他听见爬上防火梯的那一位或那几位是在追捕自己——这个错，怪得情有可原。种种情况凑在一起，使他撤离紫鸡餐厅的行动神不知鬼不觉。
